Module 4 · Document databases

25 questions · Lecture 7 and exercise session E03

Lesson 13 · One object, one document: the document model and how to design it

Lesson 13 · One object, one document: the document model and how to design it · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain why document-based databases were introduced. Describe the impedance mismatch problem with an example and explain how a document store addresses it.

Show model answer

The lecture gives three reasons: document databases handle schema changes well (easy development), they solve the impedance mismatch problem, and they follow the rise of JSON. The impedance mismatch is the difference between the shape of the data in the application and in a relational database. In the program an order is one object with a customer, a list of line items and payment details; in a relational database it is split over the tables orders, customers, order lines and credit cards, so every read must join the tables and every write must take the object apart. A document store keeps the order as one JSON-like document, with the line items as an array of sub-documents and the payment details as an embedded sub-document. The object is read and written as a unit, with one fetch and no join, and its data is stored together (better data locality). Since a collection does not impose a schema, new documents can carry new fields without changing the old ones.

A complete answer has three parts: the three reasons in the slide's words, a concrete object split over several tables, and the mechanism of the cure (embedding, one read). The order of p. 291 is the example the slide itself draws.

Lesson 13 · One object, one document: the document model and how to design it · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which statement about BSON is correct according to the lecture?

  • BSON is a text encoding of JSON that is always more compact than JSON. BSON is binary, and not always smaller: {name: "sue", age: 26} takes 28 bytes as BSON and 23 characters as JSON text. Its gain is speed of scanning.
  • BSON types are a superset of JSON types, with the exception that BSON has no universal "number" type. The words of p. 301: BSON adds types such as date and byte array, but must choose between 32-bit integer, 64-bit integer and double where JSON has one number.
  • BSON cannot store dates, so dates are stored as strings. The opposite: the slide names the date as a type JSON lacks and BSON has.
  • A BSON document can have any size; MongoDB splits large ones automatically. A document may not exceed 16 MB (p. 298). GridFS stores large files in chunks, but it is a separate mechanism you choose, not an automatic split of documents.
Show model answer

BSON types are a superset of JSON types, with the exception of not having a universal "number" type as JSON does. BSON is a binary serialization of JSON-like documents: each entry has a field name, a data type and a value, and large elements are prefixed with their length to speed up scanning.

A closed question that quotes p. 301 almost word for word. Wrong ticks may be penalised: the "always more compact" option is the tempting one, and Fig. 4 shows why it is false.

Lesson 13 · One object, one document: the document model and how to design it · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

A document has "_id": ObjectId("5ea64532a7678a3b27d34e00"), generated by MongoDB. Which statements are correct? Select all that apply.

  • The value is 12 bytes long, written as 24 hexadecimal digits. "The 12-byte ObjectId value" (p. 1420); two hexadecimal digits per byte.
  • Its first 4 bytes, 5ea64532, are the creation time in seconds since the Unix epoch. "A 4-byte timestamp value … measured in seconds since the Unix epoch": here 27 April 2020, 02:36:34 UTC.
  • It is assigned by a central counter of the server, like an auto-increment key. No central counter: a random value per process and a counter initialised to a random value make ids unique without coordination.
  • It is the value of the field that acts as the primary key of the collection. "_id field is always the primary key" (p. 297); "Every document must have an _id" (p. 303).
  • Another document of the same collection may have the same _id. The _id identifies a document within its collection, like a primary key: it must be unique there.
Show model answer

The first, second and fourth statements. An ObjectId is the default value of the mandatory _id field, the primary key of the collection. It has 12 bytes: a 4-byte timestamp (seconds since the Unix epoch), a 5-byte random value generated once per process, and a 3-byte incrementing counter initialised to a random value.

Three correct options of five. Wrong ticks may be penalised, so tick only what you can justify from p. 1420 and p. 297.

Lesson 13 · One object, one document: the document model and how to design it · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the difference between embedding and referencing documents in MongoDB, and give one situation in which each is preferable.

Show model answer

Embedding stores the related data inside the document, as a sub-document or an array of sub-documents — for example the contact and access data inside the user, or the comments inside a post. One read returns everything, without a join, and the whole document is written in one operation. Referencing stores in one document the _id of another document, which lives in its own collection — for example user_id in a contact document, or post_id in a comment. The database does not enforce the reference and does not follow it: the application needs a second query. The lecture's rule of thumb is to embed whenever possible; the exercise session says to embed data that is often queried together and to reference data that is large or accessed independently. The line items of an order are always shown with the order and belong only to it, so they are embedded. A product appears in many orders and has its own catalogue page, so it is referenced by its id: embedding it would copy it into every order, and every price change would have to update all the copies.

A complete answer gives the two definitions, the cost of each (second query against duplication and growth), the rule of thumb and a pair of situations that follow from the criteria. Naming the 16 MB limit as the reason not to embed unbounded arrays is a good addition.

Lesson 13 · One object, one document: the document model and how to design it · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam of 13 Jun 2025 (its header prints Jan. 21, 2025), exercise 3. ER scenario: a data structure for tracking concert attendances and performances. Person (Personal ID, Name, Surname, Birth Date) has the children Audience, Artistic Director, Maestro (Biography, Stage Name) and Musician (Biography), (T, E). Orchestra (Name, Foundation Date, Biography). Instrument (Name, Type (e.g., chordophones), Description). Maestro (1,1) — Direct — (1,1) Orchestra, with First Concert (Yes/No); Orchestra (10,N) — Compose — (1,2) Musician; Musician (1,N) — Play — (1,N) Instrument, with Since.

Consider the entities Orchestra, Maestro, Musician, and Instrument. How many collections would you define within a documental database (clearly state it)? How would you implement the relations between the concepts? Provide a simple documental representation. (1 PT)

Show model answer

NUMBER OF COLLECTIONS: 1 — orchestra. Direct is one-to-one, so the maestro is an embedded sub-document with First_Concert inside it. Compose is many-to-many, but a musician plays in at most two orchestras: the musicians are embedded as a sub-collection of each orchestra, and a musician is copied at most twice. Play is many-to-many with Since: the instruments are embedded in each musician, with Since inside each entry. Person is (T, E), so maestros and musicians carry Person's attributes plus their own, and there are no bare persons.

db.createCollection('orchestra');
{
  "_id": "Orchestra Aurora",            // Name: String
  "Foundation_Date": ISODate("1982-01-01"),
  "Biography": "...",                   // String
  "maestro": {                          // Direct (1:1)
    "Personal_ID": "P001",              // String
    "Name": "Claudio",                  // String
    "Surname": "Neri",                  // String
    "Birth_Date": ISODate("1960-05-05"),
    "Biography": "...",                 // String
    "Stage_Name": "Maestro Neri",       // String
    "First_Concert": true               // Boolean, of Direct
  },
  "musicians": [                        // Compose (M:N, max 2)
    { "Personal_ID": "P101",            // String
      "Name": "Fabiana",                // String
      "Surname": "Rossi",               // String
      "Birth_Date": ISODate("1994-02-11"),
      "Biography": "...",               // String
      "instruments": [                  // Play (M:N)
        { "Name": "piano",              // String
          "Type": "chordophones",       // String
          "Description": "...",         // String
          "Since": ISODate("2010-09-01") }   // Date, of Play
      ] }
  ]
}

The paper's queries — musicians named "Fabiana" who play the piano, members per orchestra and instrument, orchestras directed by "Claudio" with a member "Lorenzo" who plays the piano — all start from orchestras and run on this one collection, as a run on a real server confirmed. One consequence to say in the comments: since a musician can be stored in two orchestras, a count of musicians must count distinct Personal_IDs.

Lesson 13 · One object, one document: the document model and how to design it · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam of 30 May 2025 (its header prints June 30, 2025), exercise 5. ER scenario: a data structure for tracking puzzles' production and sales. Company (Code, Name, Address, Phone Number, Biography); Puzzle (Puzzle ID, Name, Collection Name, Number of Pieces, Width, Height, Description); Frame (Frame ID, Width, Height, Material, Color); Image (Title, Width, Height, Description) with the child Painting (Artist Name, Artist Surname, Painting Location), (P, E). Company (1,N) — Produce — (1,1) Puzzle, with Production Date; Image (1,N) — Based — (1,1) Puzzle; Puzzle (1,N) — Fit — (1,N) Frame. All sizes are in cm.

Consider the entities Puzzle, Image, Frame, and Company. How many collections would you define within a documental database (clearly state it)? How would you implement the relations between the concepts? Provide a simple documental representation. (1 PT)

Show model answer

NUMBER OF COLLECTIONS: 2 — company and frame. Puzzle is the N side of two one-to-many relationships, Produce and Based, and can be nested in only one parent: it is nested in its company, with Production_Date inside each puzzle. The image a puzzle is based on is embedded in the puzzle as a sub-document; an image used by several puzzles is copied, which is accepted because an image has few attributes and the queries filter puzzles by their image. Painting is (P, E): the painting attributes appear only when the image is a painting. Fit is many-to-many: each puzzle stores the ids of its frames, and the frames are stored once in their own collection.

db.createCollection('company');
{
  "_id": "C01",                          // Code: String
  "Name": "Tessera Puzzles",             // String
  "Address": "Via Roma 10, Pavia",       // String
  "Phone_Number": "+39 0382 555 010",    // String
  "Biography": "...",                    // String
  "puzzles": [                           // Produce (1:N)
    { "Puzzle_ID": "PZ001",              // String
      "Name": "Mona Lisa 1000",          // String
      "Collection_Name": "Museum Collection",
      "Number_of_Pieces": 1000,          // Integer
      "Width": 70, "Height": 50,         // Number (cm)
      "Description": "...",              // String
      "Production_Date": ISODate("2001-03-15"),
      "image": {                         // Based: one per puzzle
        "Title": "Mona Lisa",            // String
        "Width": 53, "Height": 77,       // Number (cm)
        "Description": "...",            // String
        "Artist_Name": "Leonardo",       // Painting only
        "Artist_Surname": "da Vinci",    // Painting only
        "Painting_Location": "Louvre" }, // Painting only
      "frames": ["F01", "F07"] }         // Fit (M:N): frame ids
  ]
}
db.createCollection('frame');
{
  "_id": "F01",                          // Frame_ID: String
  "Width": 75, "Height": 55,             // Number (cm)
  "Material": "wood",                    // String
  "Color": "black"                       // String
}

The trap of this diagram is the puzzle with two "1" sides. Choose the parent from the queries — the first counts puzzles per company — and embed the other. The second query counts distinct frames per puzzle of a Leonardo da Vinci painting in the Louvre: it needs only the frame ids, so it runs on company alone (both queries were run on a real server). Copying the frames into each puzzle would also work: say which you chose and why.

Lesson 14 · Talking to MongoDB: CRUD, filters, projections and nested documents

Lesson 14 · Talking to MongoDB: CRUD, filters, projections and nested documents · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 6 Sep 2024, exercise 3 — a restaurant. ER scenario: Person (Personal_ID, Name, Surname, Birth Date, Phone Number) with the children Owner, Customer, Waiter and Chef (Hiring Date); Head Chef (Head Chef Since) is a child of Chef. Dish (Dish_ID, Name, Price, Recipe, Description); Ingredient (Ingredient_ID, Name, Allergies (Yes/No)). Dish 1:N — Cook — 1:N Chef; Dish 1:N — Use — 1:N Ingredient; Chef 1:1 — Manage — 1:N Head Chef. N.B. "Dishes are cooked by more Chefs overall, but only one at a time."

Assumed documental representation (ours): one collection chef; a head chef is a chef document that also has head_chef_since; the dishes a chef cooked are embedded as a sub-collection, each with its ingredients: {"_id": "P1", "name": "Antonio", "hiring_date": ISODate("2019-04-01"), "dishes": [{"dish_id": "D1", "name": "Octopus with Potatoes", "price": 25.00, "recipe": "…", "description": "…", "ingredients": […]}]}.

Write a query to count the number of chefs who cooked at least one dish named “Octopus with Potatoes”, which cost 25.00€. Perform the query starting from the Chef Collection or Sub-Collection. (2 PT)

Show model answer 
db.chef.countDocuments({
  "dishes": {
    "$elemMatch": {"name": "Octopus with Potatoes", "price": 25.00}
  }
})

Both conditions describe the same dish, so they go into one $elemMatch on the sub-collection dishes; "count the number of chefs" asks for a number, so the method is countDocuments. The cost is the Price attribute, stored as a number.

This is the warm-up in a chef's apron. Written with two dotted paths, the filter would also count a chef who cooked an octopus at 30€ and some other dish at 25€: on our three test chefs the dotted form answers 2, $elemMatch answers 1. The price must be a number — 25.00 and 25 find the same value, the string "25.00" finds nothing.

Lesson 14 · Talking to MongoDB: CRUD, filters, projections and nested documents · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 13 Jun 2025 (its header prints Jan. 21, 2025), exercise 3 — concert attendances and performances. ER scenario: Person (Personal ID, Name, Surname, Birth Date) with the children Maestro (Biography, Stage Name) and Musician (Biography), among others, (T, E). Orchestra (Name, Foundation Date, Biography); Instrument (Name, Type, Description). Maestro (1,1) — Direct — (1,1) Orchestra, with First Concert (Yes/No); Orchestra (10,N) — Compose — (1,2) Musician; Musician (1,N) — Play — (1,N) Instrument, with Since.

Assumed documental representation: the one of lesson 13 — one collection orchestra; the maestro is an embedded sub-document, the musicians a sub-collection, and each musician holds his instruments as a sub-collection: {"_id": "Orchestra Aurora", "maestro": {"Name": "Claudio", …}, "musicians": [{"Personal_ID": "P101", "Name": "Lorenzo", …, "instruments": [{"Name": "piano", …, "Since": …}]}]}.

Write a query to count the number of orchestras directed by directors named “Claudio” and including at least one member named “Lorenzo” who can play the piano (2 PT)

Show model answer 
db.orchestra.countDocuments({
  "maestro.Name": "Claudio",
  "musicians": {
    "$elemMatch": {"Name": "Lorenzo", "instruments.Name": "piano"}
  }
})

The maestro is a sub-document, so a dotted path reaches his name. "At least one member named Lorenzo who can play the piano" is one musician with two properties: $elemMatch on musicians. Inside it, "instruments.Name": "piano" is a dotted path into that musician's own instruments, true if any of them is the piano.

The "directors" of the question are the maestros of Direct. Two dotted paths for the member would also count an orchestra where Lorenzo plays the cello and Fabiana the piano: on our three test orchestras the dotted form answers 2, the model answer 1.

Lesson 14 · Talking to MongoDB: CRUD, filters, projections and nested documents · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 6 Jun 2024, exercise 3 — tracking natural disasters. ER scenario: Natural Disaster (ID, Start Time, Start Date, End Time, End Date, Disaster Description) with the children Earthquake (Magnitude, Mercalli Scale), Flooding (Water Height), Landslide (Landslide Class) and Tornado (EF Scale, 0 to 5), (T, E); Location (Postal Code, City, Country, Description). Location 0:N — Occurr — 1:N Natural Disaster; Cause, recursive on Natural Disaster, with Causal Description.

Assumed documental representation (ours): one collection natural_disaster; as the hierarchy is total and exclusive, each document is one child, with a type field and its own attributes; the locations of Occurr are embedded as a sub-collection, postal codes stored as strings: {"_id": "ND1", "type": "tornado", "ef_scale": 4, "start_date": …, "locations": [{"postal_code": "23563", "city": "…", "country": "Italy", "description": "…"}]}.

Write a query to return the list of Tornadoes with an EF Scale greater than 3 that affected at least one location whose Country is Italy and whose postal code is 23563 or that affected Milan. (2 PT)

Show model answer 
db.natural_disaster.find({
  "type": "tornado",
  "ef_scale": {"$gt": 3},
  "$or": [
    {"locations": {
      "$elemMatch": {"country": "Italy", "postal_code": "23563"}
    }},
    {"locations.city": "Milan"}
  ]
})

Three conditions joined by the implicit AND: the type, the EF scale and an $or of two location clauses. "At least one location whose Country is Italy and whose postal code is 23563" is one location with two properties, hence $elemMatch; "affected Milan" is a single condition, hence a dotted path.

Read the "or" carefully: it separates the two location clauses, not the EF scale. With two dotted paths the first clause would also accept a tornado that hit location 23563 in another country and some other place in Italy. The postal code is a string because the design says so; written as the number 23563, the first clause would find nothing.

Lesson 14 · Talking to MongoDB: CRUD, filters, projections and nested documents · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 24 Jun 2024, exercise 3 — tracking Disney's digital products. ER scenario: Company Section (ID, Name, Description, Number of Employees); Digital Content (Title, Release Date, Duration, Production Cost, Minimum Age) with the children Series (Number of Episodes, …) and Movie (Sequence Number, Nominated for Award (Yes/No), …), (T, E), and Animated Movie a child of Movie, (P, O); Script (ID, Length, Duration, Title). Company Section 1:N — Produce — 1:N Digital Content, with Starting Date and End Date; Digital Content 1:N — Describe — 1:1 Script.

Assumed documental representation (ours): one collection digital_content with a type field ("series" or "movie"); the scripts are embedded as a sub-collection, Script being the N side of Describe; the producing sections are embedded as a sub-collection with the dates of Produce: {"_id": "The Pier Saga", "type": "series", "number_of_episodes": 10, "company_sections": [{"id": "908686", "name": "Digital Artist & Co", "starting_date": …, "end_date": …}], "scripts": [{"id": "S1", "length": …, "duration": …, "title": "Pier 39"}]}.

Write a query to count the number of series produced by a company section named “Digital Artist & Co” with ID “908686” and for which at least one script includes the word “Pier” in its title. (2 PT)

Show model answer 
db.digital_content.countDocuments({
  "type": "series",
  "company_sections": {
    "$elemMatch": {"name": "Digital Artist & Co", "id": "908686"}
  },
  "scripts.title": {"$regex": "Pier"}
})

The name and the ID describe one section, so they share one $elemMatch; the script condition is a single condition, so a dotted path is enough; "includes the word" is a $regex, which matches anywhere inside the title. The ID is written in quotes in the question and stored as a string.

Three families in one filter: comparison (the implicit $eq of "type": "series"), array ($elemMatch) and evaluation ($regex). $regex is case-sensitive and does not stop at word boundaries: "Pier" also matches "Pierce". If you want the word only, "\\bPier\\b" marks its boundaries — a refinement, worth one line of comment.

Lesson 14 · Talking to MongoDB: CRUD, filters, projections and nested documents · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

The session's AirBnB collection (the exercise is ours). ER scenario: Housing (ID, Name, Minimum Nights, Number of Reviews, Amenities (multi-valued)) and Host (Host_ID, Host Name, Response Rate); Housing (1,1) — Offered By — (1,N) Host; each housing has an Address with a Country. Documental representation: the session's air_bnb_collection — host and address are embedded sub-documents, amenities an array of strings, and minimum nights is stored as a string, as on p. 1472.

Write a query to return the host name and the number of reviews of the 5 housings with the highest number of reviews, among those located in Portugal, whose minimum nights is equal to 2 and that include Wifi.

Show model answer 
db.air_bnb_collection.find(
  {"address.country": "Portugal", "minimum_nights": "2",
   "amenities": "Wifi"},
  {"host.host_name": 1, "number_of_reviews": 1, "_id": 0}
).sort({"number_of_reviews": -1}).limit(5)

Three conditions in one filter, an implicit AND: a dotted path into the address, a string "2" because minimum nights is stored as a string, and "Wifi" on the array of amenities, meaning "contains". The mask keeps only what the question asks for and removes the _id; the descending sort followed by the limit gives the top 5.

On the widget's five listings this returns Ribeira (51 reviews) and River Loft (12), both hosted by Ana&Gonçalo. With the number 2 it would return Tiny Studio instead — the type trap. Forgetting "_id": 0 returns one field the question did not ask for.

Lesson 14 · Talking to MongoDB: CRUD, filters, projections and nested documents · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: in MongoDB, $nor returns the documents that do not match at least one of the query expressions in its array.

  • True This is the sentence printed on p. 1431, but it describes NOT (A AND B): a document that fails only one of the expressions would be returned, and MongoDB does not return it.
  • False $nor returns the documents that fail all the expressions of its array: NOT (A OR B).
Show model answer

False. $nor selects the documents that fail every query expression in its array: {"$nor": [A, B]} means NOT (A OR B). A document that matches A but not B is not returned.

The slide's wording is the trap, so this is the kind of statement a true-or-false question can quote. A one-line check: Mark, aged 37, is not returned by {"$nor": [{"age": 37}, {"name.first": "Bob"}]}, although he fails one of the two.

Lesson 14 · Talking to MongoDB: CRUD, filters, projections and nested documents · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain how MongoDB uses indexes: which index exists in every collection, why the order of the fields of a compound index matters, what happens when an array field is indexed, and what a unique and sparse index allows. What is the cost of an index?

Show model answer

Indexes are B+ tree structures that keep a small part of the data sorted by the indexed fields, so equality and range filters and sorts can be answered without scanning the whole collection. An index on _id, the primary key, is created automatically in every collection and keeps the _id values unique. A compound index is sorted by its first field, then by the second within equal values of the first, and so on: as in SQL, it serves queries on a prefix of its fields — for an index on (a, b, c): a; a and b; a, b and c — but not on b alone. Indexing a field that holds an array creates one index entry for every element of the array. A sparse index contains entries only for the documents that have the field; if it is also unique, duplicate values are rejected but any number of documents without the field are accepted, whereas a unique index that is not sparse would reject the second document without the field as a duplicate null. The cost: every insert and delete, and every update of an indexed field, must also update the indexes, so writes are slower, and the indexes take extra storage.

A complete answer covers five points: the automatic and unique _id index; the prefix rule with an example; one entry per array element; why unique needs sparse when the field is optional (the duplicate null); and the read/write trade-off that p. 1422 prints in green and red.

Lesson 15 · The aggregation pipeline, exam-style

Lesson 15 · The aggregation pipeline, exam-style · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam of 30 Jan 2026, exercise 3 (a hospital heating management system). ER: Hospital (Hospital_ID, Name); Building (Building_ID, Name, Year); Department (Department_ID, Specialty, N_Beds, Head_Physician_ID); Person (P_ID, Email). Has Building: Hospital (1:N) – Building (1:1); Has Dept.: Hospital (1:N) – Department (1:1); Works For, with Role: Department (1:N) – Person (1:N). Assume the documental representation of the design question: one collection, hospital; buildings and departments nested in each hospital; the people of each department nested in it, each with its Role.

"Write a query to return the total number of departments each person with the role 'maintenance' works for. Return only the top 10 people working in the highest number of departments. (2 PT) Use only the pipeline stages discussed during the practical lectures."

Show model answer

Assumed documents (as designed in the first part): { "_id": "H01", "Name": …, "buildings": [ { "Building_ID", "Name", "Year" } ], "departments": [ { "Department_ID", "Specialty", "N_Beds", "Head_Physician_ID", "people": [ { "P_ID", "Email", "Role" } ] } ] }. Role belongs to Works For, so it is read per department: a person counts the departments in which they work as maintenance.

// top 10 maintenance people by number of departments
db.hospital.aggregate([
  { "$unwind": { "path": "$departments" } },
  { "$unwind": { "path": "$departments.people" } },
  { "$match": { "departments.people.Role": "maintenance" } },
  { "$group": { "_id": "$departments.people.P_ID",
                "n_departments": { "$sum": 1 } } },
  { "$sort": { "n_departments": -1 } },
  { "$limit": 10 }
])

The two $unwind stages give one document per (department, person). The $match keeps the maintenance entries; the $group counts them per P_ID — a person appears once per department, so the count is the number of departments; $sort with -1 and $limit keep the ten highest.

Check three things in your answer: both unwinds before the condition on Role, the key with its dollar ("$departments.people.P_ID"), and $sort before $limit. The question has a second reading — all the departments of the people who are maintenance somewhere — which needs more work; say which one you answer. Tested on twelve maintenance workers spread over 12 to 1 departments: the ten with 12 down to 3 come back, in order.

Lesson 15 · The aggregation pipeline, exam-style · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam of 30 Jan 2026, exercise 3, continued. Same ER and same documents.

"Write a query to collect, for each hospital with more than 10 buildings, the number of departments with more than 25 people. Use only the pipeline stages discussed during the practical lectures. (2 PT)"

Show model answer 
// hospitals with > 10 buildings: departments with > 25 people
db.hospital.aggregate([
  { "$unwind": { "path": "$buildings" } },
  { "$group": { "_id": "$_id",
                "n_buildings": { "$sum": 1 },
                "departments": { "$first": "$departments" } } },
  { "$match": { "n_buildings": { "$gt": 10 } } },
  { "$unwind": { "path": "$departments" } },
  { "$unwind": { "path": "$departments.people" } },
  { "$group": {
      "_id": { "hospital": "$_id",
               "department": "$departments.Department_ID" },
      "n_people": { "$sum": 1 } } },
  { "$match": { "n_people": { "$gt": 25 } } },
  { "$group": { "_id": "$_id.hospital",
                "n_departments": { "$sum": 1 } } }
])

Buildings are counted with $unwind and $group, and the hospitals with more than 10 are kept. That $group would drop the departments, so they are carried through it with the aggregation function $first, which keeps the value of the first document of the group — every copy of a hospital carries the same departments array. Then people are counted per (hospital, department), the departments with more than 25 are kept, and they are counted per hospital.

A hospital with more than 10 buildings but no department over 25 people does not appear at all: no document reaches the last stage. Say so in a comment if you think a 0 is expected. A shorter answer with the same taught stages starts with { "$match": { "buildings.10": { "$exists": true } } } — an element at position 10, that is at least 11 buildings — and drops the first $unwind and $group. Both versions, tested on hospitals with 11, 10, 12 and 0 buildings, return only the first, with 2 departments (26 and 30 people; one of 5 excluded).

Lesson 15 · The aggregation pipeline, exam-style · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam of 30 May 2025 (its header prints June 30, 2025), exercise 5 (tracking puzzles' production and sales). ER: Company (Code, Name, Address, Phone Number, Biography); Puzzle (Puzzle ID, Name, Collection Name, Number of Pieces, Width, Height, Description); Produce, with Production Date: Company (1, N) – Puzzle (1, 1). Assume the documental representation of the design question: collection company, its puzzles nested in it, each with its Production_Date.

"Write a query to compute the total number of puzzles produced by each company, considering only puzzles produced after 30/04/1995 and with more than 500 pieces, and returning only the companies that produced more than 100 puzzles (1.5 PT)"

Show model answer 
// companies with more than 100 qualifying puzzles
db.company.aggregate([
  { "$unwind": { "path": "$puzzles" } },
  { "$match": {
      "puzzles.Production_Date": { "$gt": ISODate("1995-04-30") },
      "puzzles.Number_of_Pieces": { "$gt": 500 } } },
  { "$group": { "_id": "$_id", "n_puzzles": { "$sum": 1 } } },
  { "$match": { "n_puzzles": { "$gt": 100 } } }
])

One document per puzzle, the two conditions on that puzzle, a count per company, and the threshold on the count. Production_Date is assumed stored as a date, so "after 30/04/1995" is $gt that day.

The point of the exercise is the place of the first $match. Put it before the $unwind and it keeps every company with at least one such puzzle — then counts all of its puzzles: on our test data a company with 80 qualifying puzzles out of 150 was counted as 150 and passed the threshold. "More than 100" is $gt: a company with exactly 100 is out.

Lesson 15 · The aggregation pipeline, exam-style · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam of 13 Jun 2025 (its header prints Jan. 21, 2025), exercise 3 (tracking concert attendances and performances). ER: Orchestra (Name, Foundation Date, Biography); Musician, a child of Person (Personal ID, Name, Surname, Birth Date), with Biography; Instrument (Name, Type, Description). Compose: Orchestra (10,N) – Musician (1,2); Play, with Since: Musician (1,N) – Instrument (1,N). Assume the documental representation of the design question: collection orchestra; its musicians nested in it, each with the instruments they play and Since.

"Write a query to compute the number of members for each orchestra and each instrument, and return only the orchestras that include at least three musicians for at least four instruments (2.5 PT)"

Show model answer 
// orchestras with 3+ members for each of 4+ instruments
db.orchestra.aggregate([
  { "$unwind": { "path": "$musicians" } },
  { "$unwind": { "path": "$musicians.instruments" } },
  { "$group": {
      "_id": { "orchestra": "$_id",
               "instrument": "$musicians.instruments.Name" },
      "n_members": { "$sum": 1 } } },
  { "$match": { "n_members": { "$gte": 3 } } },
  { "$group": {
      "_id": "$_id.orchestra",
      "n_instruments": { "$sum": 1 },
      "instruments": { "$push": { "instrument": "$_id.instrument",
                                  "n_members": "$n_members" } } } },
  { "$match": { "n_instruments": { "$gte": 4 } } }
])

The first $group computes the members of each (orchestra, instrument); the first $match keeps the instruments with at least three; the second $group counts those instruments per orchestra and keeps their counts in a list; the last $match keeps the orchestras with at least four.

Two levels of HAVING, the pattern of the distinct count. $push is an aggregation function the slides do not show: without it the answer still returns the orchestras with their number of instruments. A musician may belong to two orchestras (Compose (1,2)) and is stored in both, but appears once inside each, so no member is counted twice per orchestra. Tested: an orchestra with three players for each of piano, violin, cello and flute is returned with 4; one with only two flautists is not.

Lesson 15 · The aggregation pipeline, exam-style · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following lists contains only stages of the MongoDB aggregation pipeline?

  • $match, $group, $unwind, $sort, $limit, $project The six stages of p. 1442: $group, $unwind, $sort, $limit, and "$match defines filters while $project defines projections".
  • $match, $elemMatch, $group $elemMatch is a query operator: it lives inside a filter, in find or in a $match. One session slide calls it a stage (p. 1438), but it never stands alone in the pipeline.
  • $group, $sum, $avg $sum and $avg are aggregation functions (accumulators, in the manual), written inside a $group.
  • $unwind, $exists, $sort $exists is an element query operator, used inside a filter.
Show model answer

The first list. A stage is an element of the array passed to aggregate(...) and transforms the stream of documents; query operators and aggregation functions are written inside stages.

A closed question where a wrong tick may cost points: the trap is the slide that calls $elemMatch a stage. Know the six names of p. 1442 by heart.

Lesson 15 · The aggregation pipeline, exam-style · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain how the $group stage of the MongoDB aggregation pipeline works: the role of _id and of the aggregation functions, the meaning of the $ in "_id": "$price", and which fields the next stage can use. How do you compute a single total over the whole collection?

Show model answer

The $group stage partitions the documents it receives by the value of the expression in _id and outputs one new document per distinct value. The other fields of the stage are aggregation functions (accumulators, in the manual), computed on the documents of each group: { "$sum": 1 } counts them, { "$sum": "$field" } adds a field, $avg, $min and $max give the mean, the minimum and the maximum. A string that starts with $ is a field path: "$price" is the value of price in each document, so there is one group per price; "price" without the dollar is a constant, and all documents fall into one group. After the stage only _id and the fields created by the aggregation functions exist; to filter on one of them, a $match is placed after the $group, like HAVING in SQL. For a total over the whole collection the _id is set to a constant such as true or null, for example { "$group": { "_id": true, "bedroom_count": { "$sum": "$bedrooms" } } }.

A complete answer covers four things: partitioning by _id, aggregation functions with at least two examples, the dollar as field path against a constant, and what survives the stage — "only the fields used to perform the aggregation or created by it" (p. 1440). The dummy _id of p. 1441 closes it. The slides say "aggregation functions"; "accumulators" is the manual's word for the same thing.

Lesson 16 · Scaling out MongoDB: shards, chunks, mongos and the CAP corner

Lesson 16 · Scaling out MongoDB: shards, chunks, mongos and the CAP corner · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

True or false: with a hashed shard key, documents with consecutive shard-key values are stored in the same chunk, so a range query on the shard key is routed to a single shard.

  • True The MD5 hash scrambles the order: consecutive values get unrelated hashes, so they scatter over the chunks (p. 332).
  • False Hash sharding distributes data "randomly within the range of MD5 values" (p. 332), so a range query on the key must be broadcast to all shards; only an equality query stays targeted.
Show model answer

False. Hash sharding applies an MD5 hash to the shard key and partitions the data by ranges of hash values. Consecutive key values have unrelated hashes, so they are spread over different chunks and shards, and a range query on the key has to be broadcast to all shards. It is range sharding that keeps neighbouring values in the same chunk.

The statement tests the price of hashing. The mirror statement — range sharding keeps neighbours together — is what makes range queries cheap and an always-growing key dangerous.

Lesson 16 · Scaling out MongoDB: shards, chunks, mongos and the CAP corner · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements about a sharded MongoDB cluster agree with the lecture? Select all that apply.

  • Sharding spreads different chunks of the data over different shards to scale, while replication keeps copies of the same data for high availability. p. 295: "Horizontal Scaling" along the row of shards, "Replication /HA" down each column.
  • The config servers store the chunk ranges of the cluster and their locations. p. 326: "Stores cluster chunk ranges and locations".
  • mongos stores a copy of the documents it routes, so adding mongos instances adds storage capacity. mongos has "No local data (persists to config database)" (p. 329); capacity grows by adding shards.
  • Geo-locality and hardware optimization require tag-aware sharding. p. 335; scale and lower recovery times take "Range or Hash".
  • A chunk that exceeds the maximum chunk size is moved, whole, to the shard with the fewest chunks. It is first split (p. 325); moving chunks is the balancer's separate decision, taken when shards are uneven.
Show model answer

The first, second and fourth statements.

A select-all question in which a wrong tick may cost points. The two false options are the classic traps: mongos holds no data, and split and migrate are two different events.

Lesson 16 · Scaling out MongoDB: shards, chunks, mongos and the CAP corner · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

Describe the roles of mongod, mongos and the config servers in a sharded MongoDB cluster. Explain the difference between a targeted and a broadcast query, and why a cluster can run several mongos instances.

Show model answer

mongod is the database instance, the main daemon that stores the documents and executes queries on them. A shard is a node of the cluster: according to the slides a single mongod or a replica set of mongod processes (one primary and secondaries); current versions require a replica set. mongos is the router: it processes all client requests, decides how many and which shards must receive each query, and collates their results before answering the client. The config servers store the cluster metadata: the chunk ranges of the shard key and the shard that holds each chunk; mongos reads and caches this map to route requests. A query whose filter contains the shard key (or a prefix of a composite shard key) is targeted to the shards owning the matching chunks; a query without it is broadcast to all shards and the partial results are merged (scatter-gather). mongos keeps no local data, since its state persists in the config database, so it can be replicated freely: one shared mongos, or one local mongos per application server to save a network hop.

A complete answer covers four roles: who stores (mongod and the shard), who routes (mongos: "how many and which", then collates), who remembers (config servers: chunk ranges and locations), and why many mongos (no local data). The slide's "1 or 3" config servers is the dated detail: today they form a replica set.

Lesson 16 · Scaling out MongoDB: shards, chunks, mongos and the CAP corner · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Define shard key and chunk. Describe what MongoDB does as a sharded collection grows, and when a new shard is added to the cluster.

Show model answer

The shard key is the field, or combination of fields, chosen by the data modeler that defines the partition space of the collection: its values are ordered like points on a line, from MinKey to MaxKey. A chunk is a contiguous range of shard-key values, a segment of that line, together with the documents whose key falls in it. A newly sharded collection starts as a single chunk. When a chunk exceeds the maximum chunk size — 64 MB by default according to the slides, 128 MB in current versions — MongoDB splits it at a shard-key value into two chunks; a split only changes the metadata on the config servers and moves no data. The balancer then migrates whole chunks from the shards that hold more to the shards that hold fewer, so that the chunks are distributed evenly; when a new shard is added, chunks are migrated to it. The application keeps working throughout, because mongos routes with the updated chunk map.

A complete answer keeps the two mechanisms apart — split (metadata only) and migration (data moves) — and gives the number. Give the slide's 64 MB, and mention the current 128 MB in one clause.

Lesson 16 · Scaling out MongoDB: shards, chunks, mongos and the CAP corner · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

A hospital heating management system stores one document per sensor reading, { deviceId, timestamp, kwh }, in a sharded MongoDB collection. Readings arrive continuously from many devices; most queries ask for the readings of one device in a time window. Compare a ranged shard key on timestamp, a hashed shard key on timestamp, and the composite shard key { deviceId, timestamp }. Which would you choose, and why?

Show model answer

A ranged key on timestamp keeps time windows together, so a time-window query is targeted, but timestamps grow monotonically: every new reading falls into the last chunk, the one bounded by MaxKey, so all inserts hit a single shard — a hot spot — and adding shards does not increase write throughput. A hashed key on timestamp applies MD5 to the value: inserts are spread evenly over chunks and shards, but neighbouring timestamps are scattered, so every time-window query must be broadcast to all shards; only an equality on one exact timestamp is targeted. The composite key { deviceId, timestamp } orders documents by device first and by time within a device: inserts from many devices spread over many chunks, and a query for one device in a time window covers one contiguous range of the key, so mongos targets one shard or a few. I would choose { deviceId, timestamp }; a query on time alone, without deviceId, would be broadcast, which is acceptable if it is rare.

This is the hook of the lesson as an exam question, and the composite key is the slide's own (p. 331). For each key name three effects — where the inserts go, which queries are targeted, which are broadcast — and the comparison writes itself.

Lesson 16 · Scaling out MongoDB: shards, chunks, mongos and the CAP corner · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

According to the lecture, which two CAP properties does MongoDB focus on? Explain how a MongoDB replica set behaves during a network partition, and which property it gives up.

Show model answer

The lecture places MongoDB on the C–P edge of the CAP triangle: it focuses on Consistency and Partition tolerance, and the course notes call it a CP architecture. Each replica set has a single primary that accepts all writes, while the secondaries replicate them. If a network partition splits a replica set, only the side that can reach a majority of the members keeps or elects a primary; a primary left on the minority side steps down and refuses writes. Clients that can reach only the minority side receive errors, so availability is sacrificed there. In exchange, only the majority side's writes survive: whatever the old primary accepted before stepping down is rolled back when the partition heals, so the data stays consistent, and the system keeps operating on the majority side despite the partition. What survives a failover therefore depends on the write concern: writes acknowledged by a majority are not lost.

Give the slide's classification first (C and P, p. 336), then the mechanism. The single primary and the majority rule are background, not slide text — they are the "why" behind the slide's label.

Module 5 · Key-value stores

19 questions · Lecture 8 and the Redis part of exercise session E05

Lesson 17 · A database with no queries: key-value stores and Redis

Lesson 17 · A database with no queries: key-value stores and Redis · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

What is a key-value store? Explain why search by ID is usually built on top of a key-value store, and what a key-value store gives up compared with a relational database.

Show model answer

A key-value store is a dictionary: every value is stored under a unique key, typically a business key such as a tweet id, a flight number, an account number or an item number, and the only way to reach a value is through its key. The store does not interpret the value; its operations are put, get and delete by key. Search by ID is built on top of a key-value store because a lookup by key is the cheapest possible access: with a hash table in memory it takes constant time whatever the size of the data, and it needs no query parsing, planning or join. Compared with a relational database, which is queried using SQL and is key-based with foreign keys, indexes and joins, a key-value store keeps only the key: no queries on the content of the values, no foreign keys, no secondary indexes, no joins. Keys are used only to retrieve a value, not to search or to map tables to each other. In exchange the store is very fast and easy to split across machines by key.

Three things make the answer complete: the definition (one key, one opaque value, access only by key), the reason (constant-time lookup with no parsing, planning or joins) and the trade-off measured against the five items of p. 346. One example from p. 345 shows you know the slide.

Lesson 17 · A database with no queries: key-value stores and Redis · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

What is Redis? Explain its definition, the three roles it can play, and what Redis is not.

Show model answer

Redis (REmote DIctionary Server) is an advanced key-value store in which the value stored under a key can be a data structure — a string, a hash, a list, a set or a sorted set — with a set of atomic operations on these data types. Unlike a plain key-value store, the values are complex data types close to the fundamental data structures and are exposed to the programmer as such, without additional abstraction layers, so the client sends an operation (append this element, add this member) and the server applies it. Redis can be used as a database, because it can persist data to disk; as a caching layer, because it is fast, the data being in memory; and as a message broker, because it is not only a key-value store (publish/subscribe, blocking queues). It is not a replacement for relational databases or document stores: it is used next to them. Increasing persistency increases latency and decreases throughput, and the dataset should fit in memory, so Redis is best used for rapidly changing data with a foreseeable size.

The three parts of p. 348, the three roles each with its reason, and the limits of p. 349. Prefer "the value of a key can be a list" to the slide's "keys can contain data structures" (the pitfall in section 2), but recognise the printed wording if a question quotes it.

Lesson 17 · A database with no queries: key-value stores and Redis · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

True or false: since all operations in Redis are atomic, a client that reads a counter with GET and then writes the incremented value back with SET performs one indivisible operation.

  • True Each of the two commands is atomic on its own, but p. 352 only guarantees that no two commands run at the same time: another client's command can run between the GET and the SET.
  • False Atomic means each single command. GET and SET are two commands; another client's SET can fall between them and an increment is lost. INCR does the read and the write in one command.
Show model answer

False. Redis is single-threaded, so no two commands run at the same time and every single command is atomic. A GET followed by a SET is two commands: another client's command can be executed between them, so an update can be lost. A single INCR (or INCRBY) is atomic.

The trap is the word "all". Lesson 18 plays this race step by step. Wrong answers may carry a penalty (the rule is announced just before the exam): tick only what you can justify.

Lesson 17 · A database with no queries: key-value stores and Redis · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Redis is a single-threaded server. Explain why this makes every command atomic, why Redis is nevertheless fast, and how it can use more than one CPU core.

Show model answer

Redis executes commands on a single thread: the commands of all clients are queued and executed one at a time, each from start to end, so no two commands can run at the same time and no client can observe or interleave with a half-executed command. Every command is therefore atomic, and no locks are needed. Redis is nevertheless fast because the data is in memory, so no disk access is on the path of a request; because most commands run in O(1) and take very little time; because a single thread spends nothing on locks or on switching between threads; and because one event loop serves many connections. The cost is that a slow command, such as an O(N) operation on a large structure, delays every other client. Redis is not designed to benefit from multiple CPU cores: to use several cores, several Redis instances are launched, each holding part of the data.

Three questions, three answers; the chain single thread, then atomic, then no locks is the core of the answer. You may add as background that since Redis 6.0 network I/O can use helper threads, while commands still execute on one thread.

Lesson 17 · A database with no queries: key-value stores and Redis · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which statements about the Redis data types are correct? Select all that apply.

  • A list may contain the same string more than once, in insertion order. A list is a "Doubly linked list of strings"; the slide's picture holds A, C, B, C.
  • A set returns its members in the order in which they were inserted. A set is an "Unordered collection of unique strings".
  • The members of a sorted set are ordered by a floating-point score. "Ordered mapping of string members to floating-point scores, ordered by score" (p. 354).
  • A geospatial index is implemented as a sorted set. "Sorted set implementation using geospatial information as the score" (p. 354).
  • A HyperLogLog returns the list of the unique items it has seen. It only estimates their number, the cardinality, in at most 12 KB; the items are not kept.
  • A string can hold an integer that Redis increments on the server. Strings contain "Integers or Floating point values" and can "increment/decrement the integers and floats" (p. 354).
Show model answer

Correct: the statements on the list, the sorted set, the geospatial index and the string. A set is unordered; a HyperLogLog only estimates the number of distinct items and cannot return them.

A select-all question where wrong ticks may cost points: the two false statements are the classic confusions, list against set and counting against storing.

Lesson 17 · A database with no queries: key-value stores and Redis · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam-style exercise, written for this notebook on the ER model of the exam of 30 May 2025 (its header prints June 30, 2025), tracking puzzles' production and sales; that paper has no Redis exercise. The part used: Puzzle (Puzzle ID, Name, Collection Name (e.g., Museum Collection), Number of Pieces, Width, Height, Description), produced by one Company (Produce, with Production Date: Company (1, N) – Puzzle (1, 1)); User (Personal ID, Name, Surname, Age, Birth Date, City, Country), who buys puzzles (Buy, with Buy Date: Puzzle (0, N) – User (0, N)). N.B. All sizes are in cm.

Exercise – Redis. For each request, state the Redis data type you use and why, in one line, then write the commands.

(1) Create a single key-value pair to store all the data of one Puzzle, with all the attributes describing the object.

(2) Store the puzzles of the "Museum Collection" sorted by their number of pieces. Then, query them to get the puzzle with the most pieces.

(3) Store the countries of the users who bought the puzzle "P1", each country only once. Then, check whether "Italy" is one of them.

Show model answer

(1) A hash: one key for the puzzle and one field for each of its attributes, so a single attribute can be read or changed on its own. The values are examples, and the attribute names are written with underscores (a field name with spaces would need double quotes).

HSET puzzle:P1 Puzzle_ID "P1" Name "Starry Night" Collection_Name "Museum Collection" Number_of_Pieces 1000 Width 70 Height 50 Description "Van Gogh, 1889"

The reply is (integer) 7, the number of fields created. One string holding the whole puzzle as JSON would also be a single key-value pair, but reading one attribute would then mean reading the whole text.

(2) A sorted set: the number of pieces is the score, so Redis keeps the puzzles in that order whatever the order of insertion. The highest score is the last element, at index -1, so the second command returns "P3".

ZADD museum:by_pieces 1000 "P1" 500 "P2" 2000 "P3"
ZRANGE museum:by_pieces -1 -1

(3) A set: each country is kept once, however many users from it bought the puzzle, and the membership test is one command. SADD replies (integer) 3, because the second "Italy" is ignored; SISMEMBER replies (integer) 1.

SADD puzzle:P1:countries "Italy" "France" "Italy" "Spain"
SISMEMBER puzzle:P1:countries "Italy"

Written for this notebook in the wording of the papers; no official solution exists for it, nor for any past paper. It trains the choice that the exam of 30 Jan 2026, exercise 5, asks on its hospital ER model (solved in lesson 18). What makes each answer complete: (1) every attribute of Puzzle under one key — a hash, or JSON in a string; (2) a structure that keeps the order by itself, the score written before the member, and the right end read: index 0 is the lowest score, -1 the highest — a list would need the puzzles pushed already in order; (3) uniqueness and membership, the two things a set gives for free and a list does not ("very quick to test the existence of a value", p. 1600).

Lesson 18 · Redis commands, exam-style

Lesson 18 · Redis commands, exam-style · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 24 Jun 2024, exercise 5 — Redis (3 PT). ER (tracking Disney's digital products), the part used: Script (ID, Length, Duration, Title) and Line (ID, Sequence Number, Text), linked by Include: Script 1:N – Line 1:1.

"Consider the Line table. Write the Redis code to create a new list of lines involving the lines "Hey you", "Watch out", and "Hello there". (1 PT)"

"Write the Redis operation to collect the first three lines in the list. (1 PT)"

"Write the Redis operations to create a new key-value pair named "Line #0001" with the value "A long time ago in a galaxy far, far away" and check its lifespan. (1 PT)."

Show model answer 
RPUSH lines "Hey you" "Watch out" "Hello there"
LRANGE lines 0 2
SET "Line #0001" "A long time ago in a galaxy far, far away"
TTL "Line #0001"

Redis answers (integer) 3; then the three lines in the order given; then OK and (integer) -1, because a key created with a plain SET has no expiry.

What to get right: RPUSH, because LPUSH would store the lines reversed and the "first three" would start with "Hello there"; the inclusive stop of LRANGE (0 2 is three elements, 0 3 would ask for four); quotes around a key that contains a space; and TTL for "check its lifespan" — saying that the answer is -1 shows that you know what it means.

Lesson 18 · Redis commands, exam-style · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 21 Jan 2025, exercise 4 — Redis (4 PT). ER (tracking Netflix's digital content), the part used: Book (ID, name, number_volumes, publication_date); Write: Book 1:1 – Writer 1:N; Inspired: Cartoon 0:1 – Book 0:N.

"Consider the Book entity and write the commands for the following operations. Create a key-value pair with "best_book", the value "La ragazza delle Arance", and TTL equal to 100 (1 PT)"

"Check the TTL of the key-value pair "best_book" and make it permanently stored (1 PT)"

"Assume there exists a set named "HP Books" that includes all the Harry Potter books besides the first one. Add the movie "Harry Potter and the Philosopher Stone" to the set. (1 PT)"

"Check if the last movie "Harry Potter and the Deadly Hallows" is part of the set (1 PT)"

Show model answer 
SET best_book "La ragazza delle Arance" EX 100
TTL best_book
PERSIST best_book
SADD "HP Books" "Harry Potter and the Philosopher Stone"
SISMEMBER "HP Books" "Harry Potter and the Deadly Hallows"

Replies: OK; the seconds left (at most 100), then (integer) 1, after which TTL best_book would answer -1; (integer) 1, one new member; (integer) 1 if the title is in the set, as it should be since the set holds every book but the first, otherwise 0. SET best_book "La ragazza delle Arance" followed by EXPIRE best_book 100 is an equally correct first answer.

What to get right: EX or EXPIRE for the TTL; TTL before PERSIST, since the item asks to check and then to make permanent; the quotes around HP Books; SISMEMBER, not SMEMBERS, for "check if". "Permanently stored" means no expiry, not a disk write. Redis compares exact strings: the paper writes "Deadly Hallows" for the Deathly Hallows, so the spelling must match the one stored in the set.

Lesson 18 · Redis commands, exam-style · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 13 Jun 2025, exercise 4 — Redis (3 PT). ER (tracking concert attendances and performances), the part used: Instrument (Name, Type (e.g., chordophones), Description); Play – Since: Musician (1,N) – Instrument (1,N).

"Consider the entity Instrument. Create a set of instruments including "Keyboard", "Violin", and "Drums" (1 PT)"

"Perform an operation to check whether "Flute" is part of the set (1 PT)"

"Perform an operation to add "Flute" to the set, with time to live equal to 10 (1 PT)"

Show model answer 
SADD instruments "Keyboard" "Violin" "Drums"
SISMEMBER instruments "Flute"
SADD instruments "Flute"
EXPIRE instruments 10

Replies: (integer) 3; (integer) 0; (integer) 1 and (integer) 1. In Redis a time to live belongs to a key, not to a member: EXPIRE makes the whole set (Keyboard, Violin, Drums and Flute) disappear after 10 seconds. If only Flute must expire, it needs a key of its own, for example SET instrument:Flute "Flute" EX 10.

No marking scheme is published, so make the sentence part of the answer: saying what EXPIRE really does shows that you know where a TTL lives. An answer that writes SADD instruments "Flute" EX 10 adds two extra members, "EX" and "10", and sets no clock at all.

Lesson 18 · Redis commands, exam-style · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 12 Sep 2025, exercise 4 — Redis (three items of 1 PT). ER (tracking farmers and their sales at local markets), the part used: Animal (Tag, Name, Age, Animal Type (e.g., Cow), Breed); House – Housing Date: Animal (1, 1) – Farm (1, N).

"Consider the Animal entity. Create a list "Farm" with two animals named "Bessy" and "Bob" (1 PT)"

"Write the code to create a new element "Dog" with value "Jerry" that will expire in 1000 seconds. (1 PT)"

"Write the code to check whether the animal "Bob" is in a list of animals named "Farm" (1 PT)"

Show model answer 
RPUSH Farm "Bessy" "Bob"
SET Dog "Jerry" EX 1000
LPOS Farm "Bob"

Replies: (integer) 2; OK; (integer) 1, the position of "Bob" ((nil) if it were missing). LPOS needs Redis 6.0.6 or later and is not in the slides; with the slides' commands, read the list with LRANGE Farm 0 -1 and look for "Bob". A set with SISMEMBER would answer this test in one step: that is what sets are for.

The list is the paper's choice, and lists are weak at membership: both answers walk the list, O(N). The sentence about sets shows that you know why. "An element that will expire in 1000 seconds" is a string with a clock: SET … EX 1000, or SET then EXPIRE Dog 1000.

Lesson 18 · Redis commands, exam-style · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 30 Jan 2026, exercise 5 — Redis (5 PT). ER (a hospital heating management system), the part used: Hospital (Hospital_ID, Name); Department (Department_ID, Specialty, N_Beds, Head_Physician_ID), with Has Dept.: Hospital 1:N – Department 1:1; Heating System (Heater_Sys_ID, Heating_Power) with the children HVAC System (Hourly_KWh_Cons, Cost_Per_KWh) and Gas Heating System (Hourly_SQM_Cons, Cost_Per_SQM), ISA (T, E), and Has H. Sys.: Hospital 1:N – Heating System 1:1.

"Create a single key-value pair to store all the data of one Gas Heater, with a TTL of 100, and with all the attributes describing the object. (1 PT)"

"Create a list to store all Department specialties, sorted by their foundation year. Then, query the list to get the older one. (2 PT)"

"Create a set to store all Department specialties for Hospital1. Then, create another set to store all Department specialties for Hospital2. Finally, combine the two sets to obtain the complete collection of specialties available across both hospitals. (2 PT)"

Show model answer 
HSET gas_heater:GH01 Heater_Sys_ID GH01 Heating_Power 24 Hourly_SQM_Cons 0.8 Cost_Per_SQM 0.12
EXPIRE gas_heater:GH01 100
ZADD specialties 1921 "Cardiology" 1950 "Neurology" 1975 "Oncology"
ZRANGE specialties 0 0
SADD hospital1:specialties "Cardiology" "Oncology"
SADD hospital2:specialties "Cardiology" "Neurology"
SUNION hospital1:specialties hospital2:specialties

The values are examples: the paper gives only the attributes. Replies: (integer) 4 and (integer) 1; (integer) 3 and "Cardiology", the lowest score; (integer) 2, (integer) 2 and the three specialties Cardiology, Oncology and Neurology, in any order.

(a) One key with all the attributes is a hash: the four attributes of a gas heating system, two of them inherited from Heating System, become fields, and EXPIRE is a second command because HSET has no EX. One string holding a JSON document, SET gas_heater:GH01 '{…}' EX 100, also keeps everything under one key. (b) Department has no foundation year in the diagram: assume the attribute, as the readme of the papers asks when a paper has a gap. The paper says "list", but only a sorted set keeps the order by itself; a list pushed in year order with RPUSH and read with LRANGE specialties 0 0 also returns the oldest — say which one you use and why. (c) SUNION returns the union; SUNIONSTORE all:specialties hospital1:specialties hospital2:specialties keeps it under a new key.

Lesson 18 · Redis commands, exam-style · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Redis offers INCR and INCRBY, although a client could read a value with GET, add one, and write it back with SET. Why are these commands needed? Explain what "atomic" means here and why Redis can guarantee it.

Show model answer

GET, +1 and SET are three separate steps: the client reads the value, computes the new one on its side and writes it back. Between the read and the write another client can read the same old value, so two concurrent increments both write 11 over 10 and one of them is lost. INCR performs the read, the increment and the write inside the server, as one command. An atomic operation is indivisible: no other client's command can run in the middle of it, so it is not affected by concurrent access. Redis can guarantee this because it is a single-threaded server that executes one command at a time — no two commands can run at the same time — so every single command is atomic without any lock, and two INCR commands always add 2. The same holds for DECR, INCRBY, DECRBY and HINCRBY.

A complete answer names three things: the lost update as the concrete problem, "atomic" defined as indivisible with respect to other clients, and the single thread as the reason. "Redis locks the key" is wrong: there is no lock, there is simply no other command running.

Lesson 18 · Redis commands, exam-style · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

A key is created with SET session "abc" EX 60. Twenty seconds later a client runs SET session "xyz". What does TTL session return right after?

  • 40 That would need the new SET to keep the old clock. A plain SET removes it (only the KEEPTTL option, not used in the course, would keep it).
  • 60 The slide's "whenever the key is SET, the TTL is reset" does not mean that the old clock starts again: it is removed.
  • -1 A plain SET replaces the value and drops the timeout: the key is now permanent.
  • -2 -2 means that there is no such key; this key exists, it was just overwritten.
Show model answer

-1. SET overwrites the value and discards any time to live unless the new SET carries its own EX; the key now never expires.

The trap is the word "reset" on p. 1595. A wrong closed answer may cost points: if you hesitate between 40 and 60, remember that a plain SET writes a brand-new value without a clock.

Lesson 19 · Keeping an in-memory database alive; caching and memcached

Lesson 19 · Keeping an in-memory database alive; caching and memcached · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which Redis topology provides both automatic failover and the distribution of data across nodes?

  • Standalone master-slave Slaves give redundancy and read offloading, but "There is no automatic failover" (p. 361), and every slave holds all the data.
  • Sentinel Automatic failover only: "Sentinel does not distribute data across nodes" (p. 362).
  • Twemproxy It distributes data by consistent hashing but has no failover: a dead instance's keys are unreachable.
  • Cluster "Cluster (automatic failover and distribute data)" (p. 360): hash slots spread the data, and a slave is promoted when its master fails (p. 364).
Show model answer

Redis Cluster. Its keyspace is divided into 16384 hash slots shared among the masters, and if a master fails one of its slaves is promoted automatically. Sentinel gives only automatic failover, Twemproxy only data distribution, and the standalone topology neither.

The two-by-two of p. 360 is the whole question. The trap to avoid is Sentinel: it is about availability, never about distribution.

Lesson 19 · Keeping an in-memory database alive; caching and memcached · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements are true? Select all that apply.

  • Redis Sentinel distributes data across the nodes of a master/slave topology. False: "Sentinel does not distribute data across nodes" (p. 362).
  • In Redis Cluster, multi-key commands are only allowed for keys in the same hash slot. True, verbatim from p. 364; hash tags put related keys in one slot.
  • By default, Redis slaves are read only. True: "Slaves are read only by default" (p. 359).
  • In the standalone master-slave topology, a slave is promoted automatically when the master fails. False: "There is no automatic failover" (p. 361); that is Sentinel's job.
  • In memcached a single item lives on one server only, and is lost if that server goes down. True: "No replication (single item in cache lives on one server only)" and "No persistency" (p. 381).
  • Twemproxy is part of Redis and performs automatic failover. False twice: "Twemproxy is a project from Twitter and is not part of redis" (p. 363), and it only distributes data.
Show model answer

True: the second, the third and the fifth. Sentinel provides automatic failover without distributing data; the standalone topology has no automatic failover; Twemproxy is an external proxy from Twitter that distributes data and has no failover.

With a possible penalty for wrong answers, tick only what a slide sentence supports. Each false statement here swaps one capability of the two-by-two for another.

Lesson 19 · Keeping an in-memory database alive; caching and memcached · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

Redis keeps its data in memory. Describe the two mechanisms it provides for persistence, what each of them can lose in a crash, and the trade-off between persistence and performance.

Show model answer

Redis provides Redis database snapshots (RDB) and append-only files (AOF). An RDB snapshot is a point-in-time copy of the whole dataset, written to disk periodically: it is compact and fast to reload, but everything written after the last snapshot is lost in a crash, typically minutes of data. The AOF appends every write command to a log that is replayed at restart: it loses at most what was not yet flushed to disk (about one second with the default once-per-second flush, nothing if the file is synced after every write), but the file is larger and slower to replay. Both can be enabled together. In general, increased persistency tends to increase latency and decrease throughput — syncing after every write makes each write wait for the disk. Redis is therefore not a replacement for relational databases or document stores: it is best used for rapidly changing data whose size is foreseeable and fits mostly in memory, often next to such a store.

The slides give the two names (p. 359) and the trade-off sentence (p. 349); the rest of the answer explains them, as a theory answer should. Three things to earn: both mechanisms, what each loses, and the persistence-versus-latency trade-off.

Lesson 19 · Keeping an in-memory database alive; caching and memcached · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

A cache served 45 cache hits and 10 cache misses. What is its hit ratio, in percent?

Show model answer

Hit ratio = hits / (hits + misses) = 45 / (45 + 10) = 0.818, about 82 %. The miss ratio is 1 − 0.818, about 18 %.

The numbers of p. 370, which prints 82 % and 18 %. Write the brackets: the slide's "hits / hits + misses", read literally, is not a ratio. If a question goes on to ask whether such a cache pays, the answer depends on the hit ratio against \(t_c / t_o\), not on 82 % being above 50 %.

Lesson 19 · Keeping an in-memory database alive; caching and memcached · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Memcached offers no persistence, no replication, no fail-over and no authentication. Explain why it is nevertheless widely used, and compare it with Redis.

Show model answer

Memcached is a free and open-source, high-performance, distributed in-memory key/value cache, intended to speed up dynamic web applications by alleviating database load. Its servers are independent: the client manages the pool and sends each key to one server (for example three servers of 64 GB form one 192 GB pool). Items are at most 1 MB, keys at most 250 characters; keys cannot be enumerated, and space is reclaimed with LRU only when needed. It can do without persistence, replication and fail-over because it is a cache: every item duplicates data whose original is stored elsewhere, usually in the database, so losing a server costs time — the next requests miss and reload from the database — but not data. Redis instead offers persistence (RDB snapshots, AOF), replication to read-only slaves, automatic failover (Sentinel, Cluster), partitioning by proxy or by Cluster, and values that are data structures. In the benchmark shown in the lecture the two are equally fast (80,000 each). Memcached fits a pure cache; Redis fits data that must survive or needs structures.

The point the examiner looks for is the link between the "No"s and being a cache. A list of features without that "why" answers only half the question.

Lesson 19 · Keeping an in-memory database alive; caching and memcached · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

An exercise in the style of the past papers' Redis exercises. Consider the ER model of a hospital heating management system (the scenario of the Exam of 30 Jan 2026): Hospital (Hospital_ID, Name) has Departments (Department_ID, Specialty, N_Beds, Head_Physician_ID), Buildings and Heating Systems; Persons (P_ID, Email) work for Departments with a Role. A dashboard shows, for each Department, a summary page computed by a slow query over its floors and heaters.

Consider the Department entity and write the Redis commands for the following operations. (a) Cache the summary of Department "D7" under a key, so that it expires after 10 minutes. (b) Check how long the cached summary will remain valid. (c) The Head Physician of "D7" changes: invalidate the cached summary immediately. (d) The dashboard runs on a Redis Cluster: store the Specialty "Cardiology" and the N_Beds 24 of "D7" under two keys that a single command can read together, and read them.

Show model answer 
SET department:D7:summary "Cardiology - 3 floors - 24 beds - 2 heaters" EX 600
TTL department:D7:summary
DEL department:D7:summary
MSET {department:D7}:specialty "Cardiology" {department:D7}:n_beds 24
MGET {department:D7}:specialty {department:D7}:n_beds

(a) A time-based invalidation: 10 minutes = 600 seconds (equivalently SET followed by EXPIRE department:D7:summary 600). (b) TTL returns the remaining seconds, at most 600, and -2 once the key has expired. (c) An event-based invalidation: DEL returns 1, and the next read misses and recomputes the summary. (d) The hash tag {department:D7} makes both keys hash the same string, so they share a hash slot and the multi-key commands are allowed; MGET returns "Cardiology" and "24".

The exercise is ours, built on the past papers' Redis wording ("Create a key-value pair … with TTL equal to …", "check its lifespan"). Three ideas are checked: TTL in seconds, invalidation on the event as an explicit DEL, and the same-slot rule of Redis Cluster with its hash tag — without the braces, MGET on two keys in different slots is refused.
