Module 6 · Columnar databases and warehouses

27 questions · Lectures 9 and 9-bis and the Cassandra part of session E05

Lesson 20 · Column stores and the Cassandra data model

Lesson 20 · Column stores and the Cassandra data model · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

True or false: Cassandra stores the values of each column of a column family together, separately from the other columns, as a column-oriented database such as Vertica does.

  • True That is the column storage of pp. 401–407. Cassandra keeps the columns of a row together under its row key: "think of cassandra as row-oriented" (p. 417).
  • False Cassandra is a wide-column store: a row, found by its key, holds all its columns together (p. 417). Storing each column apart is the analytic column store of pp. 401–407.
Show model answer

False. Cassandra is a wide-column (column-family) store: each row is uniquely identified by its key and keeps all its columns, sorted by name, together, so it should be thought of as row-oriented. Storing each column separately is the design of column-oriented databases such as Vertica or C-Store, built for OLAP: a query reads only the columns it needs, while inserts and whole-record reads touch every column. Cassandra is called columnar because of its column families and its very wide rows, and it is built for write-heavy workloads.

The trap is the word "column", shared by two different designs (section 2 and section 3). Wrong answers may carry a penalty, announced just before the exam: answer only what you can justify.

Lesson 20 · Column stores and the Cassandra data model · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain how a column-oriented database stores a table and compare it with a row-oriented database: advantages, disadvantages, and the workloads each one suits.

Show model answer

A row-oriented database stores each record contiguously; a column-oriented database stores each column contiguously, in its own storage. A query that needs k of the m columns therefore reads only those k columns in a column store, while a row store reads whole records and may read unnecessary data. The column layout also compresses better (the values of a column share a type and repeat, so techniques such as run-length encoding work well) and improves bandwidth utilisation, code pipelining and cache locality. Its disadvantages are increased disk seek time (a record is spread over many files), an increased cost of inserts (one write per column) and increased tuple reconstruction costs (records must be rebuilt by position). Column stores therefore suit read-mostly, read-intensive, large repositories — OLAP and data mining — while row stores suit OLTP, with frequent inserts and updates of whole records.

A complete answer has four parts: the two layouts, the read argument (k of m columns), the pros and cons in the words of pp. 403–404, and the workloads. A strong extra is the chart of p. 405: the column store loses only when a query selects nearly the whole tuple.

Lesson 20 · Column stores and the Cassandra data model · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

The Quarter column of a sales table, sorted by quarter, is stored with run-length encoding as (Q1, 1, 300), (Q2, 301, 350), (Q3, 651, 500), (Q4, 1151, 600). Which statements are correct? Select all that apply.

  • (Q2, 301, 350) means that Q2 is stored at the positions from 301 to 350. The third number is a length, not an end: Q2 fills the 350 positions from 301 to 650.
  • The table has 1,750 rows. The last run ends at 1151 + 600 − 1 = 1750, the same as 300 + 350 + 500 + 600.
  • Position 700 holds Q3. Q3 starts at 651 and covers 500 positions, up to 1150.
  • The Price column of the same table would shrink just as much with the same encoding. Neighbouring prices rarely repeat: each price would become a triple, three times its raw size, which is why p. 407 leaves Price raw.
  • Sorted by customer instead, the same Quarter column would usually compress far less. Runs need equal values next to each other; another sort order breaks them up.
Show model answer

The second, third and fifth statements. Each triple is (value, start_pos, run_length), so (Q2, 301, 350) covers the positions 301 to 650; the last run ends at 1151 + 600 − 1 = 1750; position 700 lies in the run of Q3, from 651 to 1150. Run-length encoding pays only when equal values are adjacent: the Quarter column is sorted and the prices are not, so the encoding is chosen column by column and Price stays raw.

The numbers are those of p. 407, so the answer can be checked against the slide. The classic slip is reading run_length as an end position. Wrong ticks may carry a penalty: tick only what you can justify.

Lesson 20 · Column stores and the Cassandra data model · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Describe the data model of Cassandra as presented in the lecture — keyspace, column family, row, column and super column. Explain why a column family is called a sparse table and what the timestamp of a column is used for.

Show model answer

A keyspace is the outermost container, roughly a database and typically one per application; some settings, such as the replication, are configured per keyspace. It contains column families, the analogue of tables, which group records of a similar but not identical kind; their settings include the comparator and the type, standard or super. A column family holds rows, each uniquely identified by its row key; Cassandra should be thought of as row-oriented, because a row's columns are stored and returned together. A column has three parts: a name (bytes; it determines the sort order, is used in queries and is indexed), a value (bytes; values are not queried) and a timestamp. A super column groups columns under a common name: it has a name and a map of columns instead of a value, and its sub-column names are not indexed. A column family is a sparse table because each row stores only the columns it has: a missing column costs nothing and is not a NULL. The timestamp (clock) is supplied with every write and resolves conflicts between replicas: the last write wins.

Give the containment chain in order and the three parts of a column with their properties (p. 420). "Sparse" and "last write wins" are the two whys the question asks for. If you quote the slide's example of a keyspace setting, the partitioner, add that it is in fact set for the whole cluster (red pen on p. 413).

Lesson 20 · Column stores and the Cassandra data model · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the difference between the domain-based model of an RDBMS and the query-based model of Cassandra. How would you support the query SELECT * FROM User WHERE city = 'Scottsdale' on a column family User keyed by UserID, and what does the solution cost?

Show model answer

An RDBMS follows a domain-based model: you model the entities and relationships of the domain, normalise them, and answer any later question by combining tables with joins ("what answers do I have?"). Cassandra follows a query-based model: rows are found by key, column values are not queried and there are no joins, so you list the questions first and build a structure for each one ("what questions do I have?" — start from queries, then design the data model). For the query, create a second column family, UserCity, keyed by city, whose column names are the IDs of the users living there, with empty values (the Valueless Column pattern): the query becomes one row read, followed by a multiget of the users from User. This is why Cassandra is called an index factory. The cost is duplication: every user is written to both column families, and the application must keep the copies consistent, with no joins and no referential integrity to help.

Two things make the answer: the why (rows found only by key, no joins) and the price (duplication, copies kept in step by the application). In CQL the same idea is a table with PRIMARY KEY ((city), user_id). The exercise session's other tool is a secondary index, CREATE INDEX; the design question of the paper of 30 Jan 2026 rules it out with "Do not use indexes".

Lesson 20 · Column stores and the Cassandra data model · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

Our exercise, in the wording of the past papers: the hotel of pp. 434–435. Consider the following ER diagram. Hotel (Hotel_ID, Name, Phone, Address, City, State, Zip); Room (Room_ID, Room Number, Rate); Guest (Guest_ID, Name, Phone, Email). Relationships: Has, Hotel (1, N) – Room (1, 1); Reserve, Guest (0, N) – Room (0, N), with the attributes Start Date and End Date.

Suppose you expect to record a large-scale, continuously increasing number of reservations, and therefore you decide to store them in a columnar database. Write the specification of the tables (column families) of a Cassandra deployment so that one can query the hotels of a state, the hotels of a city of a given state, and the reservations of a guest by phone number, the most recent first. Do not use indexes.

Show model answer

Two tables, one per access path: the hotels by location, partitioned by state and clustered by city, and the reservations by guest phone, partitioned by phone and clustered by start date, the most recent first. Each reservation carries copies of the guest, hotel and room data it shows, because Cassandra has no joins.

-- the keyspace (one per application)
CREATE KEYSPACE hotels
  WITH replication = {'class': 'SimpleStrategy',
                      'replication_factor': 3};
-- queries 1 and 2: the hotels of a state, or of a city in it
CREATE TABLE hotels_by_location (
  state text,
  city text,
  hotel_id text,
  name text,
  phone text,
  address text,
  zip text,
  PRIMARY KEY ((state), city, hotel_id)
);
-- query 3: a guest's reservations, with copies, newest first
CREATE TABLE reservations_by_guest_phone (
  guest_phone text,
  start_date date,
  reservation_id uuid,
  end_date date,
  guest_name text,
  hotel_id text,
  hotel_name text,
  room_number int,
  rate float,
  PRIMARY KEY ((guest_phone), start_date, reservation_id)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (start_date DESC, reservation_id ASC);
-- all the hotels of Arizona: one partition
SELECT * FROM hotels_by_location WHERE state = 'AZ';
-- the hotels of Scottsdale: the first clustering column fixed
SELECT * FROM hotels_by_location
  WHERE state = 'AZ' AND city = 'Scottsdale';
-- a guest's reservations, the most recent first
SELECT * FROM reservations_by_guest_phone
  WHERE guest_phone = '+1 480 555 0101';

Every reservation is one INSERT into reservations_by_guest_phone, with reservation_id from uuid() and the guest's name, the hotel's name and the room's number and rate copied in.

This is pp. 431–435 in CQL. hotels_by_location is the aggregate key state:city of p. 432: the state is the partition key and the city the first clustering column, so one table answers both location questions with no key range and no order-preserving partitioner. reservations_by_guest_phone is the Reservation of p. 435: keyed by what the application searches, with copies instead of joins; reservation_id keeps two reservations of one guest on the same day apart, since an INSERT with an existing primary key overwrites. Past papers ask the same kind of question — the farmers' paper of 12 Sep 2025, exercise 1, is solved in lesson 21, together with the CQL rules behind every line.

Lesson 21 · CQL: the key that decides everything

Lesson 21 · CQL: the key that decides everything · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 6 Sep 2024, exercise 5 (restaurant). ER: Person (Personal_ID, Name, Surname, Birth Date, Phone Number) is the parent of Owner, Customer, Waiter and Chef (Hiring Date); Head Chef (Head Chef Since) is a Chef.

"Consider the Chef table. Write a Cassandra script to perform the operations listed below. Create the Chef table, with Personal_ID as the partition key and Phone Number as the clustering key, ordering the table based on the Phone Number. (1.5 PT) Write a CQL query to collect the chef whose name is "Riccardo". If any further operation is required, write its CQL code. (1 PT) Create a Custom data type named "contact" that contains the name, surname, and phone number of the chef (1.5 PT)"

Show model answer 
CREATE KEYSPACE restaurant
  WITH replication = {'class': 'SimpleStrategy',
                      'replication_factor': 3};
USE restaurant;

CREATE TABLE chef (
  personal_id text,
  phone_number text,
  name text,
  surname text,
  birth_date date,
  hiring_date date,
  PRIMARY KEY (personal_id, phone_number)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (phone_number ASC);

-- name is not part of the key: the further operation is an index
CREATE INDEX chef_name ON chef (name);
SELECT * FROM chef WHERE name = 'Riccardo';

CREATE TYPE contact (
  name text,
  surname text,
  phone_number text
);
-- optional, to use it: a list of contacts of the chef
ALTER TABLE chef ADD contacts list<frozen<contact>>;

Without the index, SELECT * FROM chef WHERE name = 'Riccardo' ALLOW FILTERING; also works, but it scans the table and is not recommended.

The three sub-questions test three things: the bracket form with the clustering order, the index as the "further operation" for a non-key column, and the type definition. Phone numbers are text (a leading + or 0 must survive). On test data, a Riccardo with two phone numbers came back as two rows of one partition, sorted by number: here the clustering order means something.

Lesson 21 · CQL: the key that decides everything · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 11 Feb 2025, Cassandra exercise (Formula 1). ER: Person (Personal_ID, Name, Surname, Birth_Date, Gender) is the parent of Spectator (Favourite_Team), Team Owner and Team Member, ISA (T, E).

"Consider the spectator entity. Write a CQL statement to create its corresponding Cassandra Table using Favourite_Team as a clustering key and Name as an additional partition key (1 PT)." "Write a CQL statement to return a list of all the spectators whose name is Bill, whose age is over 65, and whose favourite team is "Ferrari". Besides the query, write any additional CQL statements only if it's strictly necessary. (2 PT)" "Write the CQL command to perform a set of statements by loading them from a file located in "D:\Cassandra\Scripts\statement.txt". (1 PT)"

Show model answer 
CREATE TABLE spectator (
  personal_id text,
  name text,
  favourite_team text,
  surname text,
  birth_date date,
  gender text,
  PRIMARY KEY ((personal_id, name), favourite_team)
);

-- over 65 on the day of the exam (11/02/2025): born before 11/02/1960
SELECT * FROM spectator
WHERE name = 'Bill' AND favourite_team = 'Ferrari'
  AND birth_date < '1960-02-11'
ALLOW FILTERING;

SOURCE 'D:\Cassandra\Scripts\statement.txt';

No additional statement is strictly necessary. personal_id is unknown, so the partition key is incomplete, and age is a range on a column outside the key: ALLOW FILTERING is unavoidable. An index on name (allowed on one part of a composite partition key) would narrow the scan, but the query would still need ALLOW FILTERING because of the range.

The key point is "additional partition key": name goes inside the inner brackets next to personal_id. The ER has no age, so it is computed from Birth_Date; state the assumption. Both claims were checked on Cassandra 5.0.9: without ALLOW FILTERING the query is refused, even with an index on name; with it, of five test spectators only the Bill born in 1955 who supports Ferrari comes back. The path may be written with forward slashes too; the quotes are what matters.

Lesson 21 · CQL: the key that decides everything · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 30 May 2025, exercise 1 (puzzles; the paper's header prints "June 30, 2025"). ER: User (Personal ID, Name, Surname, Age, Birth Date, City, Country) — Buy (Buy Date) — Puzzle (Puzzle ID, Name, Collection Name, Number of Pieces, Width, Height, Description); Puzzle (0, N), User (0, N).

"Suppose you expect to record a large number of purchases of puzzles and you decide to store them in a columnar database. Assume you want to run typical queries by searching by the puzzle IDs and by User City or Country. Write the specification of the tables (and other artifacts if necessary) of a Cassandra deployment that stores the required data."

Show model answer 
CREATE KEYSPACE puzzle_sales
  WITH replication = {'class': 'SimpleStrategy',
                      'replication_factor': 3};
USE puzzle_sales;

-- query 1: purchases of a puzzle, newest first
CREATE TABLE purchases_by_puzzle (
  puzzle_id text,
  buy_date date,
  personal_id text,
  puzzle_name text,
  user_name text,
  user_surname text,
  city text,
  country text,
  PRIMARY KEY ((puzzle_id), buy_date, personal_id)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (buy_date DESC, personal_id ASC);

-- query 2: purchases by country, or by country and city
CREATE TABLE purchases_by_location (
  country text,
  city text,
  buy_date date,
  puzzle_id text,
  personal_id text,
  puzzle_name text,
  user_name text,
  user_surname text,
  PRIMARY KEY ((country), city, buy_date, puzzle_id, personal_id)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (city ASC, buy_date DESC, puzzle_id ASC, personal_id ASC);

-- SELECT * FROM purchases_by_puzzle WHERE puzzle_id = 'PZ01';
-- SELECT * FROM purchases_by_location WHERE country = 'Italy' AND city = 'Milan';

Each purchase is written into both tables, in one BATCH. Cassandra has no joins, so the puzzle and user columns the queries return are copied into each table.

A complete answer shows one table per query, the searched attribute as partition key, a clustering key that keeps rows unique (otherwise two purchases overwrite each other), and denormalised columns. City is the first clustering column, so one table serves both "by country" and "by country and city"; a query by city alone is refused (checked), since a city name is not unique across countries anyway. Separate tables per city and per country are also correct. If one country's partition grows too large, a time bucket can join the partition key, for example ((country, buy_year), …).

Lesson 21 · CQL: the key that decides everything · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 12 Sep 2025, exercise 1 (farmers and local markets). ER: Product (Product ID, Name, Quantity (in grams), Expiration Date) — Sell — Market (Name and Location, both underlined, Opening Time, Closing Time, Size); Product — Buy (Buy Date, Amount) — People (Personal ID, …); every cardinality (1, N).

"Suppose you expect to record large-scale, continuously increasing number of sales of products in the market, and therefore you decide to store it in a columnar database. Write the specification of the tables (column families) of a Cassandra deployment assuming you may want to query the data by market location or product name."

Show model answer 
-- a sale: who bought which product, when and how much (Buy), at which market (Sell)
CREATE TABLE sales_by_market_location (
  market_location text,
  sale_date date,
  sale_id uuid,
  market_name text,
  product_id text,
  product_name text,
  personal_id text,
  amount int,
  PRIMARY KEY ((market_location), sale_date, sale_id)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (sale_date DESC, sale_id ASC);

CREATE TABLE sales_by_product_name (
  product_name text,
  sale_date date,
  sale_id uuid,
  product_id text,
  market_name text,
  market_location text,
  personal_id text,
  amount int,
  PRIMARY KEY ((product_name), sale_date, sale_id)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (sale_date DESC, sale_id ASC);

-- SELECT * FROM sales_by_market_location WHERE market_location = 'Lodi';
-- SELECT * FROM sales_by_product_name WHERE product_name = 'Pecorino'
--   AND sale_date >= '2025-09-01';

Both tables receive every sale (one BATCH per sale); sale_id, filled with uuid(), keeps two sales of the same day apart.

Same pattern as the puzzles: two queries, two column families, the search attribute as partition key, the date as clustering key so the newest sales come first and a date range is one slice. "Continuously increasing" is the hint about growth: without sale_id sales of one day would overwrite each other, and for a market that never stops selling a month bucket in the partition key, ((market_location, sale_month), …), keeps each partition bounded.

Lesson 21 · CQL: the key that decides everything · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 30 Jan 2026, exercise 2 (hospital heating). ER: Heating System (Heater_Sys_ID, Heating_Power) is the parent of HVAC System (Hourly_KWh_Cons, Cost_Per_KWh) and Gas Heating System (Hourly_SQM_Cons, Cost_Per_SQM), ISA (T, E); Floor (Floor_ID, Number, Has_Elevator); Department (Department_ID, Specialty, N_Beds, Head_Physician_ID). Heats: Heating System (1:N) – Floor (1:N), with Start_Time, End_Time; Placed Into: Department (1:N) – Floor (1:N).

"Consider the entities Heating System (and its child entities), Floor, and Department. Define the database structure so that one can query Heaters simultaneously by Floor number and Department specialty, sorting the results by total heating power. Write the specification of the tables of a Cassandra deployment that stores the required data. Do not use indexes. (2 PT)" "Write a CQL query to collect all the Gas Heating Systems running for "cardiology" Departments. If any further operation is required, write its CQL code. (1 PT)"

Show model answer 
-- one row per heater, for each floor number and specialty it heats;
-- (T, E): one table with a type column, the other type's columns stay empty
CREATE TABLE heaters_by_floor_and_specialty (
  floor_number int,
  specialty text,
  heating_power float,
  heater_sys_id text,
  heater_type text,
  hourly_kwh_cons float,
  cost_per_kwh float,
  hourly_sqm_cons float,
  cost_per_sqm float,
  PRIMARY KEY ((floor_number, specialty), heating_power, heater_sys_id)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (heating_power DESC, heater_sys_id ASC);
-- SELECT * FROM heaters_by_floor_and_specialty
--   WHERE floor_number = 2 AND specialty = 'cardiology';

-- further operation for the second query: a table keyed by its conditions
CREATE TABLE heaters_by_specialty_and_type (
  specialty text,
  heater_type text,
  heater_sys_id text,
  heating_power float,
  hourly_sqm_cons float,
  cost_per_sqm float,
  hourly_kwh_cons float,
  cost_per_kwh float,
  PRIMARY KEY ((specialty, heater_type), heater_sys_id)
);
SELECT * FROM heaters_by_specialty_and_type
WHERE specialty = 'cardiology' AND heater_type = 'Gas';

"Simultaneously by Floor number and Department specialty" makes both a composite partition key; "sorting by heating power" makes it the first clustering column, DESC assumed (state it); heater_sys_id keeps two heaters of equal power apart. The second query lacks the floor number, so on the first table it would need ALLOW FILTERING over everything (it runs, checked); "Do not use indexes" points to the query-first answer, a second table. The ISA (T, E) becomes a type column in one table, empty columns costing nothing in Cassandra.

Lesson 21 · CQL: the key that decides everything · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the roles of the partition key and of the clustering key of a Cassandra table. Which conditions can the WHERE clause of a SELECT contain without ALLOW FILTERING, and why does Cassandra impose these rules?

Show model answer

The primary key has two parts. The partition key, the first element (or the columns in the inner brackets), is hashed into a token that decides on which nodes the row is stored: all rows with the same partition key form one partition on the same replicas. The clustering key, the remaining columns, decides how the rows are sorted inside the partition (WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY sets ASC or DESC). Without ALLOW FILTERING, a SELECT that restricts the partition key must restrict every one of its columns with = or IN, so Cassandra can compute the token and go to the right node. Clustering columns may be restricted only as a prefix in their declared order, with a range only on the last one, because then the result is one contiguous slice of the sorted partition; OR does not exist. Any other column needs a secondary index and an equality condition, and such a query may omit the partition key, at the price of asking every node. ALLOW FILTERING lifts the rules, but makes Cassandra read and discard rows, so its cost grows with the size of the table.

A complete answer names both roles, the three legal kinds of condition and the reason behind each: the hash for the partition key, the sorted storage for the clustering prefix, the per-node lookup for the index. Quoting the slide's definitions (p. 1560) is safe.

Lesson 21 · CQL: the key that decides everything · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which statements about CQL are true? Select all that apply.

  • The clustering key decides on which node a row is stored. That is the partition key; the clustering key sorts the rows inside a partition (p. 1560).
  • With PRIMARY KEY ((a, b), c) and no index, the clause WHERE a = 1 is rejected unless ALLOW FILTERING is added. Rule 1 of p. 1569: "WHERE clauses must include ALL components of the partition key"; half a composite key gives no token.
  • A secondary index makes range conditions (<, >) on the indexed column legal without ALLOW FILTERING. An index answers equality only: "most comparison operators require the additional statement ALLOW FILTERING" (p. 1568), and price > 100000 still needs it after CREATE INDEX car_price.
  • An INSERT fails if a row with the same primary key already exists. INSERT is an upsert: it overwrites the listed columns without an error.
  • With PRIMARY KEY (a, b, c) and no index, the clause WHERE a = 1 AND c = 2 is rejected unless ALLOW FILTERING is added. c is restricted while the preceding clustering column b is not (rule 2).
  • A DELETE that names only the partition key removes every row of that partition. p. 1571: the clustering key is "not strictly required"; without it the whole partition goes.
Show model answer

True: the second, the fifth and the sixth.

Wrong answers may carry a penalty at the real exam (it is announced just before it), so tick only what you can justify. The fourth option is the SQL reflex; the third is the most common slip about secondary indexes.

Lesson 22 · Inside Cassandra: ring, gossip, replicas, quorums; HBase

Lesson 22 · Inside Cassandra: ring, gossip, replicas, quorums; HBase · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Describe step by step what a Cassandra replica node does when it receives a write, and explain why reads can be slower than writes.

Show model answer

The replica first appends the write to the commit log on disk, a sequential append that makes it durable, and then applies it to the memtable, an in-memory structure of key-value pairs sorted by key; then it can acknowledge. No read, no disk seek and no lock are needed. When the memtable is full or old, it is flushed to disk as an SSTable (Sorted String Table): an immutable file of key-value pairs sorted by key, with an index file of (key, position) pairs and a Bloom filter. Because SSTables are never modified, new versions and deletes (tombstones) accumulate in newer SSTables, and compaction periodically merges them, keeping the newest version of each key and removing deleted items. A read, instead, must look in the memtable and possibly in several SSTables — skipping those whose Bloom filter says the key is absent — and merge the versions by timestamp; the coordinator may also contact several replicas and start a read repair. This is why reads may be slower than writes.

A complete answer gives the order (commit log, then memtable), the words SSTable, flush, compaction and Bloom filter, and the reason for the asymmetry. The slide of p. 446 says reads "touch log and multiple SSTables": write memtable, and mention the log only for restarts.

Lesson 22 · Inside Cassandra: ring, gossip, replicas, quorums; HBase · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

A keyspace has replication factor N = 3. Which write/read consistency-level pairs guarantee that a read returns the most recent acknowledged write? Select all that apply.

  • W = ONE, R = ONE 1 + 1 = 2 ≤ 3: the read may ask a replica the write has not reached.
  • W = QUORUM, R = QUORUM 2 + 2 = 4 > 3: the two majorities share at least one replica.
  • W = ANY, R = ALL ANY can be satisfied by a hint on a coordinator that is not a replica: until the hint is delivered, no replica has the value, so even reading all three can miss it.
  • W = ALL, R = ONE 3 + 1 = 4 > 3: every replica has the value.
  • W = ONE, R = ALL 1 + 3 = 4 > 3: the read asks every replica, including the one that acknowledged.
  • W = ONE, R = QUORUM 1 + 2 = 3, not greater than 3: the two replicas read may both be stale.
Show model answer

QUORUM/QUORUM, ALL/ONE and ONE/ALL. The condition is W + R > N (here 2 + 2, 3 + 1 and 1 + 3, all greater than 3): it forces the replicas that acknowledged the write and the replicas that answer the read to overlap in at least one node, and the coordinator returns the value with the most recent timestamp. ONE/ONE and ONE/QUORUM fail the inequality; ANY guarantees no replica at all, because a hint stored on the coordinator counts as a successful write.

Three correct options out of six. ANY/ALL is the trap: it looks like the "read everything" corner, but W counts only real replicas. Wrong ticks may be penalised, so tick only what the inequality proves.

Lesson 22 · Inside Cassandra: ring, gossip, replicas, quorums; HBase · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: a Bloom filter may report that a key is absent from an SSTable although the key is stored in it.

  • True That would be a false negative, which a Bloom filter never gives: the bits set when the key was inserted are never cleared.
  • False No false negatives; only false positives — an absent key whose bits were all set by other keys.
Show model answer

False. When a key is inserted, all its k hashed bits are set, and bits are never reset, so a stored key always finds its bits set and the filter answers "maybe present". The filter can only err the other way: an absent key may find all its bits set by other keys (a false positive). The rate is low; the lecture's example has k = 4 hash functions, 100 items and 3200 bits, with a false-positive rate of 0.02 %.

A true/false question that tests the direction of the error. Write the reason, not just the word: "bits are never cleared".

Lesson 22 · Inside Cassandra: ring, gossip, replicas, quorums; HBase · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain how the nodes of a Cassandra cluster learn which nodes are alive without a master. Describe the membership list and how a node decides that another node is down.

Show model answer

Cassandra is peer to peer: no master knows the membership, so each node keeps its own membership list with, for every node, its address, the latest heartbeat counter heard and the local time at which that counter was received. Each node increments its own counter and periodically gossips its list to a few peers (up to three per round), with a three-message exchange (GossipDigestSyn, GossipDigestAck, GossipDigestAck2). On receipt, for every address the node keeps the larger counter and stamps it with its own local time, because the clocks are not synchronised: node 2 at local time 70 receives 10120 and 10098 for nodes 1 and 3, larger than its 10118 and 10090, and stores (10120, 70) and (10098, 70), while it keeps its own entry and the equal one. The traffic per node stays constant and the views converge (anti-entropy). A node whose counter stops increasing becomes suspect: the accrual failure detector computes a suspicion value PHI, minus the base-10 logarithm of the probability that a heartbeat arrives later than the silence observed so far, from the past inter-arrival times; the application sets the threshold (on the slide, PHI = 5 gives a detection time of 10–15 seconds).

A complete answer has three parts: no master and gossip with few peers; the merge rule with local times; suspicion as a value with a threshold rather than a fixed timeout. The numbers of p. 451 make the merge rule concrete.

Lesson 22 · Inside Cassandra: ring, gossip, replicas, quorums; HBase · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Cassandra "prefers availability over consistency", while HBase "prefers (strong) consistency over availability". Explain where this difference comes from in the two architectures.

Show model answer

In Cassandra every row has N equal replicas on a masterless ring. Any node can coordinate a write and sends it to all replicas; with levels such as ONE or ANY, and with hinted handoff, a write is accepted even while replicas are down, so the replicas may hold different versions for a while; they are reconciled later by timestamps, read repair and compaction (eventual consistency, BASE). In HBase a table is split into regions, and each region is served by exactly one HRegionServer at a time, assigned by the HMaster through ZooKeeper. Every write of a row goes through that single server, which appends it to its write-ahead log (HLog) before applying it to the MemStore, so every read of the row sees the latest write. The copies of the data are kept by HDFS at the file level, not by several servers answering for the same row. When a region server fails, its regions are unavailable until the HMaster reassigns them and the HLog is replayed into the new MemStores: HBase gives up availability during recovery to keep strong consistency.

The core is "N equal replicas" against "one owner per region". The write-ahead log alone gives durability; consistency comes from the single owner. Mention BASE for Cassandra, and the unavailability during recovery for HBase.

Lesson 22 · Inside Cassandra: ring, gossip, replicas, quorums; HBase · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

A keyspace uses the Simple Strategy with replication factor 4. Writes are performed at consistency level QUORUM. What is the smallest number of replicas a read must wait for, so that it is guaranteed to return the latest acknowledged write?

Show model answer

QUORUM with N = 4 is ⌊4/2⌋ + 1 = 3 replicas, so W = 3. A fresh read needs W + R > N, that is R > 4 − 3 = 1, so R = 2 replicas. Of the lecture's levels, ONE is not enough and QUORUM (3) is more than needed.

The two steps are the quorum size and the inequality. With N = 4 the slide's N/2 + 1 gives 3 with or without rounding; with an odd N only the rounded-down form is a whole number, so always write the floor.

Lesson 22 · Inside Cassandra: ring, gossip, replicas, quorums; HBase · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

ER scenario of the exam of 12 Sep 2025 (the exercise itself is exam-style, not from the paper): a data structure for tracking farmers and their sales at local markets. Market (Name, Location, Opening Time, Closing Time, Size) and Product (Product ID, Name, Quantity, Expiration Date) are linked by the relationship Sell, with cardinality (1, N) on both sides.

The sales are stored in Cassandra, partitioned by market location, on one datacenter of six nodes n1–n6 with tokens 10, 25, 40, 55, 70 and 85; the token of the partition 'Milano' is 62. Write the CQL statement that creates the keyspace markets so that every row is stored three times. Say which nodes hold the copies of 'Milano', and choose consistency levels for writes and reads so that a read always returns the latest acknowledged sale while one replica may be down.

Show model answer 
CREATE KEYSPACE markets
  WITH replication = {
    'class': 'SimpleStrategy',
    'replication_factor': 3};

With the Simple Strategy the first copy goes to the first node clockwise from the token 62, that is n5 (token 70), and the other two to the next nodes clockwise, n6 (85) and n1 (10, after wrapping around the ring). Writes and reads at QUORUM wait for ⌊3/2⌋ + 1 = 2 replicas each: 2 + 2 > 3, so every read overlaps the last acknowledged write, and either operation still succeeds with one replica down. (ONE/ALL or ALL/ONE would also be consistent, but a single replica down would block the reads or the writes.)

The keyspace line opens every Cassandra script of the exercise session (p. 1553); here it is joined to the placement and quorum rules of this lesson. The wrap-around from n6 to n1 is where answers usually slip.

Lesson 23 · Data warehouses and Snowflake

Lesson 23 · Data warehouses and Snowflake · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: a data warehouse always contains the most current information available in the operational systems.

  • True The warehouse is a copy integrated in advance and refreshed by loads: between two loads it lags behind the sources.
  • False The first advantage of p. 491 comes with its price: "High query performance — But not necessarily most current information".
Show model answer

False. In the warehousing approach information is integrated in advance and copied into the warehouse, which is refreshed by periodic batch loads. Queries are fast and do not interfere with the sources, but the data is not necessarily the most current: it is as fresh as the last load. For rapidly changing information the query-driven approach is still better.

The trap is the word "always". A good justification names the price of copying in advance — staleness — and the case in which the lazy, query-driven approach wins.

Lesson 23 · Data warehouses and Snowflake · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

According to the lecture, which of the following characterise a data warehouse (OLAP) rather than an operational OLTP database? Select all that apply.

  • Mostly reads p. 489, warehouse column.
  • Many small transactions The OLTP column of p. 489: the till's short transactions.
  • History rather than the current snapshot p. 489: "History" against "Current snapshot"; p. 505: "Historic data".
  • Index/hash on the primary key That is how OLTP finds one row by key; the warehouse does "Lots of scans".
  • Lots of scans p. 489: analytical queries read large parts of the data.
  • Normalized table structure, many tables with few columns p. 505 gives this to the operational system; the warehouse is "De-normalized … few tables, many columns".
  • Batch updates p. 505: the warehouse is loaded in batches, the operational system has "Continuous updates".
Show model answer

Mostly reads, history, lots of scans and batch updates. Many small transactions, an index or hash on the primary key and a normalized structure with many narrow tables characterise the OLTP database.

A select-all question built from the two comparison slides, pp. 489 and 505. Every option is a row of one of them: learn each row as a pair, and the wrong ticks become easy to avoid.

Lesson 23 · Data warehouses and Snowflake · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Give Inmon's definition of a data warehouse and explain each of its four properties with an example. Then explain how the warehousing approach differs from the query-driven approach to integrating heterogeneous sources.

Show model answer

Inmon defines a data warehouse as "a subject-oriented, integrated, time-varying, non-volatile collection of data that is used primarily in organizational decision making". Subject-oriented: data is organised by business subject, such as customer, product or sales, not by the application that produced it. Integrated: data from heterogeneous sources is reconciled into one consistent format and meaning. Time-varying: history is kept and every record is tied to a time — all transactions ever made at a bank, not only the current balance. Non-volatile: data is loaded in batches and then not updated, possibly append-only. In the warehousing approach information is integrated in advance: an extractor/monitor at each source feeds an integrator that loads the warehouse, and clients query the warehouse directly, with high performance and no interference with the sources, but not necessarily the most current data. In the query-driven approach (lazy, on demand) an integration system decomposes each query and sends it through wrappers to the live sources; it remains better for rapidly changing information or sources, truly vast amounts of data and clients with unpredictable needs.

A complete answer has three parts: the four adjectives with a concrete example each, the eager-versus-lazy contrast, and at least one case where each approach wins. Inmon's book writes "time-variant"; the slide prints "time-varying": use the slide's word and recognise the other.

Lesson 23 · Data warehouses and Snowflake · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

A chain of pharmacies records its sales in an operational database whose ER diagram has these entities (primary key first): Sale Line (Line_ID, Date, Quantity, Amount), Medicine (Medicine_ID, Name, Category, Producer), Pharmacy (Pharmacy_ID, Name, City, Region) and Campaign (Campaign_ID, Description). Each sale line refers to exactly one medicine (1,1), exactly one pharmacy (1,1) and at most one campaign (0,1); a medicine, a pharmacy or a campaign can appear in any number of sale lines (0,N). Management wants to analyse quantities and amounts by medicine category, region and month.

(a) Design a star schema with one fact row per medicine, pharmacy, campaign and day: give the fact table with its key and its measures, and the dimension tables with their attributes, with the (min,max) cardinalities. (b) Say which tables are normalised and why. (c) Show how the Pharmacy dimension changes in a snowflake schema. (d) Write the SQL query that returns the total amount by region and month.

Show model answer

(a) Fact Sale(Medicine_ID, Pharmacy_ID, Campaign_ID, Time_ID, Quantity, Amount): the key is composed of the four dimension keys, the measures are Quantity and Amount, and each row aggregates all the sale lines of that combination in that day. Dimensions: Medicine(Medicine_ID, Name, Category, Producer), Pharmacy(Pharmacy_ID, Name, City, Region), Campaign(Campaign_ID, Description), with a "no campaign" row for sales without one so that the key stays complete, and Time(Time_ID, Day, Month, Year). Every relationship is (1,1) on the Sale side and (0,N) on the dimension side.

(b) The fact table is in BCNF: its only functional dependency goes from the whole key to the measures. The dimensions are not normalised: Pharmacy_ID → City → Region and Day → Month → Year are transitive dependencies, kept on purpose because the warehouse is loaded in batches and not updated in place, and every attribute stays one join away from the fact.

(c) In a snowflake schema the hierarchy becomes separate entities: Pharmacy (1,1)—(0,N) City (1,1)—(0,N) Region, that is Pharmacy(Pharmacy_ID, Name, City_ID), City(City_ID, Name, Region_ID), Region(Region_ID, Name).

(d)

SELECT ph.Region, t.Year, t.Month, SUM(s.Amount) AS Amount
FROM Sale s
  JOIN Pharmacy ph ON s.Pharmacy_ID = ph.Pharmacy_ID
  JOIN Time t ON s.Time_ID = t.Time_ID
GROUP BY ph.Region, t.Year, t.Month;

The design reasoning of pp. 509–512 applied to a new scenario. A complete answer has the composed key, the measures in the fact and the descriptions in the dimensions, the normalisation argument of p. 510, and a query that joins only the dimensions it needs — grouped by year and month, or April of two different years would fall into one group. The "no campaign" row is the detail that keeps the key complete: the slide's star puts Promo (1,1) on every sale, which needs exactly such a row for sales without a promotion.

Lesson 23 · Data warehouses and Snowflake · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the cube Store × Product × Time with measure Quantity. Define drill-down, roll-up, slice and dice, give an example of each, and say which clause of the SQL star query each operation changes.

Show model answer

Drill-down adds one analysis dimension (disaggregation): from sales by product and quarter to sales by product, quarter and store. Roll-up removes one analysis dimension (aggregation): from sales by product, quarter and store back to sales by product and quarter, each cell being the sum over the stores; climbing a hierarchy, such as from month to year or from city to region, is also a roll-up. A slice fixes one dimension to one value and leaves a sub-cube with one dimension less, for example the sales of one product over all stores and periods. A dice selects ranges of values on several dimensions and leaves a smaller cube, for example a category of products in a region over a mid-term time span: two products, two stores and two quarters, 8 cells. In the star query, roll-up and drill-down change the GROUP BY (fewer or more grouping attributes, or a coarser or finer level of a hierarchy); slice and dice add conditions on dimension attributes to the WHERE.

Give the slide's definitions (p. 513) first, word for word, then the hierarchy form as a complement. The link to SQL shows that you understand the operations rather than remember the pictures.

Lesson 23 · Data warehouses and Snowflake · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which statement describes the architecture of Snowflake?

  • Shared-disk: every compute node works on one common disk system, and all queries compete for it Half of the picture: Snowflake keeps one central storage, like shared-disk, but runs its queries on independent MPP clusters (virtual warehouses) that do not compete with each other.
  • Shared-nothing: every node owns a partition of the data on its own disks Nodes do process data in parallel, but the data is not owned by them: it sits in one central storage.
  • A hybrid: a central columnar storage readable by all compute nodes, independent MPP compute clusters (virtual warehouses), and a cloud services layer that coordinates them The three layers of pp. 524–527: Database Storage, Query Processing, Cloud Services.
  • A single large server with columnar storage, scaled by moving to a bigger machine Virtual warehouses are MPP clusters of many nodes "allocated by Snowflake from a cloud provider" (p. 526).
Show model answer

The third. Snowflake is a hybrid of the two traditional architectures: like shared-disk it keeps the data in one central repository that every compute node can access, stored in an optimized, compressed, columnar format; like shared-nothing it processes queries on MPP clusters, the virtual warehouses, which share no compute resources with each other. A cloud services layer handles authentication, infrastructure, metadata, query parsing and optimization, and access control.

The slides draw the two traditional architectures (p. 523) and Snowflake's layers (p. 524) without the word "hybrid"; Snowflake's documentation uses it. The question checks that you can place Snowflake against both, not just name its layers.

Lesson 23 · Data warehouses and Snowflake · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the separation between compute and storage in Snowflake and why the lecture presents it as a key feature. Then explain how resizing a virtual warehouse differs from a multi-cluster warehouse, and write the statement that creates a warehouse of size XSMALL that runs between 1 and 3 clusters and suspends after 5 minutes of inactivity.

Show model answer

In Snowflake the data is stored once, in a central cloud storage layer, in a compressed columnar format managed by Snowflake, while queries are processed by virtual warehouses: independent MPP compute clusters that read that storage and share no compute resources with each other. Other databases combine compute and storage, so they must be sized for the largest workload and that cost is paid all the time. With the two separated, each workload gets its own warehouse on the same data without interference, and a warehouse can be suspended when idle (AUTO_SUSPEND) and resumed automatically (AUTO_RESUME), so compute is paid only while it is used. Resizing a warehouse — scaling up, for example from XSMALL to LARGE — gives each query more compute and makes large, complex queries faster. A multi-cluster warehouse — scaling out — keeps the size but runs between MIN_CLUSTER_COUNT and MAX_CLUSTER_COUNT identical clusters, started automatically when queries queue: it serves more concurrent users but does not make a single query faster, and it requires the Enterprise Edition.

CREATE WAREHOUSE AS_WH
WITH WAREHOUSE_SIZE = 'XSMALL'
MIN_CLUSTER_COUNT = 1
MAX_CLUSTER_COUNT = 3
SCALING_POLICY = 'STANDARD'
AUTO_SUSPEND = 300
AUTO_RESUME = TRUE;

Two traps: the slides title multi-cluster warehouses "Scale Up" (p. 544), so name both moves and say what each one does; and AUTO_SUSPEND is in seconds, so five minutes is 300.

Module 7 · Search: information retrieval and Elasticsearch

Lesson 24 · Search is not a WHERE clause: relevance, TF-IDF and the inverted index

Lesson 24 · Search is not a WHERE clause: relevance, TF-IDF and the inverted index · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which pairing of a search query with its intent type agrees with the definitions of the lecture?

  • "buy new iPhone 16 now" — commercial/investigational The user is ready to complete an action, buying: transactional.
  • "Facebook login page" — informational The user wants to go to a specific website: navigational.
  • "best laptop for students" — commercial/investigational The user is considering a purchase and wants to compare options or research products (p. 553).
  • "How does user intent work?" — navigational The user wants to learn something: informational.
Show model answer

The third. A user searching "best laptop for students" is considering a purchase and wants to compare options or research products: commercial/investigational intent. The other three queries are transactional ("buy new iPhone 16 now"), navigational ("Facebook login page") and informational ("How does user intent work?").

These are the slide's own four examples, shuffled. The trap is telling commercial (still comparing) from transactional (ready to act). Wrong answers may carry a penalty: tick only what you can justify.

Lesson 24 · Search is not a WHERE clause: relevance, TF-IDF and the inverted index · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

True or false: with the normed TF-IDF vectors of the lecture, ranking documents by their Euclidean distance from the query vector gives the same order as ranking them by cosine similarity.

  • True For unit vectors the squared distance is 2 − 2 cos θ: it shrinks exactly when the cosine grows.
  • False That would hold for raw vectors of different lengths, where a long document is far from everything; normalisation removes the length.
Show model answer

True. After normalisation every vector has length 1, so

\[ \lVert \vb{q} - \vb{d} \rVert^2 = \vb{q} \cdot \vb{q} - 2\, \vb{q} \cdot \vb{d} + \vb{d} \cdot \vb{d} \] \[ = 2 - 2\cos\theta . \]

A smaller distance means a larger cosine, so both criteria give the same ranking, and the score to compute is the dot product of the normed vectors.

If the question comes as an open one, write the one-line derivation, not only the verdict. Without normalisation the two orders can differ: a long document is far from every short query even when it points the same way.

Lesson 24 · Search is not a WHERE clause: relevance, TF-IDF and the inverted index · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

Consider the Dish entity of the restaurant ER diagram of the exam of 6 Sept 2024: Dish (Dish_ID, Name, Price, Recipe, Description). Three dishes are named D1 "grilled salmon", D2 "salmon tartare with avocado" and D3 "grilled octopus". Treat each name as a document (lower-case, no stemming, every word kept) and use the formulas of the lecture: TF = log(count + 1), IDF = log((#docs + 1) / #docs-the-term-occurs-in), then normalise each vector to length 1. Which dish is the most similar to D1? Enter its similarity score with three decimals.

Show model answer

#docs = 3. df: grilled 2, salmon 2, tartare 1, with 1, avocado 1, octopus 1. IDF: df 2 gives log(4/2) = log 2, df 1 gives log 4 = 2 log 2. Every count is 1, so TF = log 2 for every term, and each raw weight is (log 2)² times 1 (df 2) or times 2 (df 1); the common factor cancels in the normalisation.

D1 is proportional to (grilled 1, salmon 1): 0.707 each. D2 is proportional to (salmon 1, tartare 2, with 2, avocado 2), length √13: salmon 0.277. D3 is proportional to (grilled 1, octopus 2), length √5: grilled 0.447.

sim(D1, D2) = 0.707 × 0.277 = 0.196; sim(D1, D3) = 0.707 × 0.447 = 0.316. D3 is the most similar, with 0.316.

The scenario comes from the paper; the question does not — no past paper asks for a TF-IDF computation. Show the order TF, IDF, normalisation, and let the dot product run over the shared terms only. Both rivals share one word with D1; the shorter name keeps more weight per word — T4's advantage on p. 563 again. The base of the logarithm cancels.

Lesson 24 · Search is not a WHERE clause: relevance, TF-IDF and the inverted index · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Using the formulas of the lecture (TF = log(count + 1), IDF = log((#docs + 1) / #docs-the-term-occurs-in), then normalisation), explain why the word "data" has weight 0.316 in the title "Data Mining Your Website" but 0.187 in "Mastering Data Mining: The Art and Science of Customer Relationship Management". What would the second weight become if the word "the" were removed from that title?

Show model answer

In both titles "data" occurs once, so TF = log 2, and it occurs in 3 of the 8 titles, so IDF = log(9/3) = log 3: the raw weight is the same, log 2 × log 3 = 0.7615. The difference comes from the normalisation, which divides each weight by the Euclidean length of the whole title vector: 2.408 for the four-term title, 4.074 for the eleven-term one. So 0.7615 / 2.408 = 0.316 and 0.7615 / 4.074 = 0.187: the same word counts for more in a short document. Removing "the" removes one raw weight of 1.523 from the sum under the square root: the length drops to √(16.598 − 2.320) = 3.779, and "data" rises to 0.7615 / 3.779 = 0.202, although its TF and IDF do not change.

Three things to say: same TF, same IDF, different length. The trap in the second part is to think the weight of "data" cannot change because nothing about "data" changed — normalisation couples every word of a document.

Lesson 24 · Search is not a WHERE clause: relevance, TF-IDF and the inverted index · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

What is an inverted index? Explain how a search engine uses it to answer a query with several terms, and why, according to the lecture, its posting lists are compressed and sorted from the most important documents to the least.

Show model answer

An inverted index is the building block of a search engine: a mapping from each term (TermID) to the list of the documents that contain it (DocumentIDs), its posting list. It is called inverted because it turns the natural direction, document to words, into word to documents. To answer a query with several terms, the engine finds all documents containing the set of terms by computing a join over their posting lists — the intersection for AND, the union for OR — without reading the documents. Search engines must respond in tenths of a second, so the lists are compressed with integer compression algorithms that allow fast decompression, take the statistics of text into account and are organised in blocks for easy reading; the reason is to reduce the space in memory, so that the index is kept and read in RAM. Sorting each posting list from the most important documents (highest term counts) to the least allows early termination: the engine computes bounds on the scores that the remaining documents could reach and drops lower-ranked documents as early as possible, while index pruning removes entries that would never be retrieved for a query.

Four things to cover: the mapping, the join, why compression (memory, hence speed) and early termination. With time to spare, add the hidden assumption of the slide's example: "fox" -> D1 only, because the index did not stem "foxes".

Lesson 24 · Search is not a WHERE clause: relevance, TF-IDF and the inverted index · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam of 24 June 2024, exercise 1. Consider the ER diagram representing a data structure for tracking Disney's digital products. Company Section (ID, Name, Description, Number of Employees); Digital Content (Title, Release Date, Duration, Production Cost, Minimum Age), with the sub-entities Series (Number of Episodes, …) and Movie (Sequence Number, Nominated for Award (Yes/No), …), (T, E), and Animated Movie (…), a sub-entity of Movie, (P, O); Character (Name, Nickname, Age); Actor (Personal ID, Name, Surname, Age, Birth Date, Career Description), with the sub-entity Voice Actor (Voice Tone, Voice Strength (from -5 to 5)), (P, O); Script (ID, Length, Duration, Title); Location (ID, Name, Description, Furniture (Text), Indoor (Yes/No)); Line (ID, Sequence Number, Text). Relationships: Play, Character (1:1) – Actor (1:N); Involve, Character (1:N) – Digital Content (1:N); Produce, Company Section (1:N) – Digital Content (1:N), with Starting Date and End Date; Describe, Digital Content (1:N) – Script (1:1); Include, Script (1:N) – Line (1:1); Perform, Actor (1:N) – Line (1:1), with Recording Date; Located, Script (1:N) – Location (1:1).

Which DB technology would be the best to store the entities/rel. of the ER diagram? [you may not need all the rows of the table] (1 PT) — ENTITIES / REL. | DB TYPE | MOTIVATION

Show model answer
ENTITIES / REL.DB TYPEMOTIVATION
Character, Actor, Voice Actor, Line; Play, PerformGraph DB (Neo4j)Many links traversed and counted (lines an actor performed, characters an actor played); the (P, O) Voice Actor is a second label on the same node; Recording Date is an edge property.

Its exercise 4 asks for the Locations whose description includes "Stadium" and "Basketball", "assigning a higher score" to those whose furniture includes "Planet" and description "Space": a ranking, not a WHERE clause. That is the motivation the third row must give — free text, an inverted index, a relevance score.

Lesson 25 · Inside an Elasticsearch index — and the rest of ELK · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 11 Feb 2025, Elasticsearch exercise, first sub-question. ER scenario: a data structure for tracking Formula 1 races. Person (Personal_ID, Name, Surname, Birth_Date, Gender) has the children Spectator, Team Owner and Team Member, (T, E); Team Member has the children Engineer, Mechanic and Pilot, (P, E). Pilot - (...), Championships_Won, Career (Textual). The second sub-question will ask for male pilots who won at least 10 championships and whose career must include the words "Champion" and "Car".

Consider the Pilot entity. Provide the complete mapping of the index for the considered entity (i.e., field name, field type, the structure of the mapping, etc.) (1 PT)

Show model answer 
PUT /pilots
{
  "mappings": {
    "properties": {
      "personal_id": { "type": "keyword" },
      "name": { "type": "text" },
      "surname": { "type": "text" },
      "birth_date": { "type": "date", "format": "yyyy-MM-dd" },
      "gender": { "type": "keyword" },
      "championships_won": { "type": "integer" },
      "career": { "type": "text" }
    }
  }
}

A pilot is a person and a team member, so its documents carry the inherited attributes as well. The identifier and the gender are keywords (exact match), names and career are full text, the number of championships is an integer and the birth date a date with its format.

The paper prints "Career (Textual), Career (Textual)" — one field is enough. The question tests the structure mappings → properties → one entry per attribute, and types that fit the queries that follow: "male" is an exact value of gender, "at least 10" a range on an integer, "Champion" and "Car" words in a text field. Forgetting the inherited attributes leaves the next query without a gender to filter on. That query is solved in lesson 26.

Lesson 25 · Inside an Elasticsearch index — and the rest of ELK · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

An index of restaurant inspections answers the request below with "hits" : [ ], although many of its documents contain "BORO" : "Queens". Which explanation is correct?

GET /nyc-restaurants/_search
{ "query": { "match": { "BORO": "queens" } } }
  • A match query is always case-sensitive. On a text field with the standard analyzer both sides are lower-cased, and "queens" finds "Queens".
  • The index has a single shard, so only part of the documents is searched. A search reaches every shard of the index; with one shard, that shard holds every document.
  • A match query cannot be used on fields that hold strings. The same match with "Queens" returns hits (p. 597).
  • BORO is mapped as keyword, which has no analyzer, so the query term "queens" must equal the stored term "Queens" exactly. The words of p. 598: "Borough (BORO) was mapped as a keyword. A keyword does not have any analyser!"
Show model answer

BORO is mapped as keyword. A keyword field has no analyzer: the stored value "Queens" is indexed as one exact term, and the query text "queens" is also used as one exact term. The two terms differ in case, so no document matches. With a text field and the standard analyzer, both would be lower-cased and the query would match.

A closed question built on pp. 597–598. Wrong ticks may be penalised: the tempting wrong answer is the first, which holds only for keyword fields and for analyzers that do not lower-case.

Lesson 25 · Inside an Elasticsearch index — and the rest of ELK · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the difference between primary shards and replica shards in Elasticsearch. How are write and read operations routed, and which of the two makes an index resistant to the failure of a node?

Show model answer

An index is partitioned into primary shards: each document belongs to exactly one primary shard, and the shards are distributed over the nodes of the cluster, so the data and the work are spread over several machines. A replica shard is a copy of a primary shard, always stored on a different node from its primary. A write operation is performed on the primary shard and then copied to its replicas, so all copies stay identical; a read operation can be routed to the primary or to any replica, so replicas also increase the capacity to serve reads. Resistance to faults comes from the replicas: when a node fails, a replica of each primary that was on it is promoted to primary and no data is lost, while without replicas the shards of the failed node are lost. Both numbers are set per index with number_of_shards and number_of_replicas.

The slides (p. 578) list resistance to faults under shards; the session (p. 1621) repeats it and counts replicas as one of "two types of shards". An answer that names the mechanism — a copy on another node — is right under every reading and shows that you know why.

Lesson 25 · Inside an Elasticsearch index — and the rest of ELK · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: once an index contains documents, the type of one of its existing fields can be changed in place with PUT /index_name/_mapping.

  • True The terms of that field were indexed for the old type. PUT /index_name/_mapping can add new fields, but not retype an existing one.
  • False To change the type you create a new index with the new mapping and reindex the documents into it.
Show model answer

False. The mapping of an existing field cannot be changed once documents are indexed: to change its type, create a new index with the correct mapping and reindex the data. PUT /index_name/_mapping can add new fields, but it cannot retype an existing one.

The statement is false both by the slide's printed sentence (p. 580) and by the precise rule — safe either way. Had it read "No field can be added to an index that has documents", the slide's wording and the facts would part ways (p. 588 adds one).

Lesson 25 · Inside an Elasticsearch index — and the rest of ELK · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which request lets Elasticsearch generate the identifier of the new document, so that sending it twice stores two documents?

  • PUT /blog/_doc/1 It creates or replaces the document with id 1: sent twice, the index still holds one document, with _version 2.
  • GET /blog/_doc/1 GET reads a document; it stores nothing.
  • POST /blog/_doc "POST doesn't require the ID of the resource → duplicates" (p. 583): each call gets a new generated _id.
  • PUT /blog It creates the index blog, not a document.
Show model answer

POST /blog/_doc. Without an id, Elasticsearch creates and assigns one, so repeating the request creates a duplicate. PUT /blog/_doc/1 requires the id and creates or updates that same document.

The pair POST /index/_doc against PUT /index/_doc/id is the whole of p. 583; the other two options test whether you can read a verb and an endpoint.

Lesson 25 · Inside an Elasticsearch index — and the rest of ELK · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Describe the logical flow of data inside a Logstash pipeline. What are the queue and the workers for, and which delivery guarantee does Logstash offer?

Show model answer

A Logstash pipeline receives events from one or more inputs, for example Beats, TCP, UDP or HTTP listeners, or a JDBC input that polls a database. All inputs write into a single queue that buffers the events, either in memory or persistently on disk; the queue decouples the inputs from the processing and absorbs bursts. Worker threads take batches of events from the queue and run them through the filters, which structure, transform, normalize and enrich the events (for example mutate or geoip), and then through the outputs, for example Elasticsearch. Several workers run in parallel, which gives server-side scalability. Codecs attached to inputs and outputs deserialize and serialize the data. Logstash offers at-least-once delivery: in most conditions an event is delivered exactly once, but after an unclean shutdown it may be delivered twice; this requires the persistent queue. Events that cannot be processed go to the dead letter queue, so that they are not lost and the pipeline does not halt.

The figure of p. 624 is the skeleton: Input → Queue → Worker (Filter → Output). Naming the two kinds of queue and the condition of at least once shows that you know why the guarantee holds, not only its name.

Lesson 26 · Asking Elasticsearch: match, filter, bool and aggregations

Lesson 26 · Asking Elasticsearch: match, filter, bool and aggregations · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 6 Jun 2024, exercise 4 — Elasticsearch (4 PT). ER (tracking natural disasters), the part used: Location (Postal Code, City, Country, Description); Occurr: Location 0:N – Natural Disaster 1:N.

"Consider the Location entity. 4.1. Provide the complete mapping of the index (i.e., field name, field type, the structure of the mapping, etc.) (1 PT) PUT …"

"Write a query to return the list of all the locations whose description includes the words "Beautiful" and "Town", removing the ones whose name includes the word "Milano". The condition on the word "Town" should not affect the final score. (1.5 PT)"

"Write a query to return the count of the locations for each country. (1.5 PT)"

Show model answer 
PUT /locations
{
  "mappings": {
    "properties": {
      "postal_code": { "type": "keyword" },
      "city": { "type": "text" },
      "country": { "type": "keyword" },
      "description": { "type": "text" }
    }
  }
}
GET /locations/_search
{
  "query": {
    "bool": {
      "must": [
        { "match": { "description": "Beautiful" } }
      ],
      "filter": [
        { "match": { "description": "Town" } }
      ],
      "must_not": [
        { "match": { "city": "Milano" } }
      ]
    }
  }
}
GET /locations/_search
{
  "size": 0,
  "aggs": {
    "locations_per_country": {
      "terms": { "field": "country" }
    }
  }
}

Location has no Name attribute: I assume the paper means the city.

Three choices decide this exercise. Postal code and country are keyword — one matched whole, one counted by — and the description is text. "Town … should not affect the final score" sends that word to filter, where a match is still analysed but scores nothing; "Beautiful" stays in must. The readme of the papers warns that an exercise may name something its diagram lacks and tells you to "consider all the necessary assumptions to solve the exercise": write yours in one line, as above. Add "size": 250 inside terms if every country must appear, not only the ten largest.

Lesson 26 · Asking Elasticsearch: match, filter, bool and aggregations · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 24 Jun 2024, exercise 4 — Elasticsearch (4 PT). ER (tracking Disney's digital products), the part used: Location (ID, Name, Description, Furniture (Text), Indoor (Yes/No)); Located: Script 1:N – Location 1:1.

"Consider the Location entity. 4.1. Provide the complete mapping of the index (i.e., field name, field type, the structure of the mapping, etc.) (1 PT) PUT …"

"Write a query to return the list of Locations whose description includes the words "Stadium" and "Basketball", assigning a higher score to those including the words "Planet" in their furniture and "Space" in their description. (1.5 PT)"

"Write a query to return the count of indoor and outdoor locations. (1.5 PT)"

Show model answer 
PUT /locations
{
  "mappings": {
    "properties": {
      "id": { "type": "keyword" },
      "name": { "type": "text" },
      "description": { "type": "text" },
      "furniture": { "type": "text" },
      "indoor": { "type": "boolean" }
    }
  }
}
GET /locations/_search
{
  "query": {
    "bool": {
      "must": [
        { "match": { "description": "Stadium" } },
        { "match": { "description": "Basketball" } }
      ],
      "should": [
        { "match": { "furniture": "Planet" } },
        { "match": { "description": "Space" } }
      ]
    }
  }
}
GET /locations/_search
{
  "size": 0,
  "aggs": {
    "indoor_and_outdoor": {
      "terms": { "field": "indoor" }
    }
  }
}

The aggregation returns two buckets, one for true (indoor) and one for false (outdoor), each with its doc_count; a boolean bucket prints its key as 1 or 0, with "key_as_string" "true" or "false".

Indoor is a Yes/No attribute: boolean, and its terms aggregation has exactly two buckets. "Includes the words "Stadium" and "Basketball"" needs both words: two match clauses in must, or one with "operator": "and" — a compact match with both words would accept either. The two bonuses sit on two different fields, so they are two should clauses: one match cannot name two fields.

Lesson 26 · Asking Elasticsearch: match, filter, bool and aggregations · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 6 Sep 2024, exercise 4 — Elasticsearch (4 PT). ER (a restaurant), the part used: Dish (Dish_ID, Name, Price, Recipe, Description); Cook: Dish 1:N – Chef 1:N; Use: Dish 1:N – Ingredient 1:N.

"Consider the Dish entity. 4.1. Provide the complete mapping of the index (i.e., field name, field type, the structure of the mapping, etc.) (1 PT) PUT …"

"Write a query to return the list of all the dishes whose description and name include the word "salmon", prioritising the one whose recipe includes the words "stir" or "boil". (1.5 PT)"

"Write a query to return the list of all the dishes whose price exceeds 10.00€, whose name is "focaccia ligure", and whose descriptions do not include the word "butter". The condition on the description attribute must not affect the final score. (1.5 PT)"

Show model answer 
PUT /dishes
{
  "mappings": {
    "properties": {
      "dish_id": { "type": "keyword" },
      "name": { "type": "text" },
      "price": { "type": "double" },
      "recipe": { "type": "text" },
      "description": { "type": "text" }
    }
  }
}
GET /dishes/_search
{
  "query": {
    "bool": {
      "must": [
        { "match": { "description": "salmon" } },
        { "match": { "name": "salmon" } }
      ],
      "should": [
        { "match": { "recipe": "stir boil" } }
      ]
    }
  }
}
GET /dishes/_search
{
  "query": {
    "bool": {
      "must": [
        { "range": { "price": { "gt": 10.00 } } },
        {
          "match": {
            "name": {
              "query": "focaccia ligure",
              "operator": "and"
            }
          }
        }
      ],
      "must_not": [
        { "match": { "description": "butter" } }
      ]
    }
  }
}

Query 1: "description and name include the word" is two fields, so two match clauses in must; "stir" or "boil" is one match, OR by default, in should. Query 2: the description condition is a negation, and must_not never scores — that already satisfies "must not affect the final score". With name mapped as text, "name is "focaccia ligure"" is a match with "operator": "and"; had you mapped it as keyword, it would be a term on the exact stored value, capitals included. Whatever you choose, the query must agree with your own mapping.

Lesson 26 · Asking Elasticsearch: match, filter, bool and aggregations · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 13 Jun 2025, exercise 5 — Elasticsearch (3 PT). (The paper's header misprints its date as "Jan. 21, 2025".) ER (tracking concert attendances and performances), the part used: Track (Title, Duration (minutes), Composer (e.g., Mozart), Music Type (e.g., classical)); Perform: Orchestra (0,N) – Track (1,N).

"Consider the Track entity. Provide the complete mapping of the index for the considered entity (i.e., field name, field type, the structure of the mapping, etc.) (1 PT) PUT /"

"Write a query to return the documents whose composer is "Bach", whose duration is greater than 8 minutes, and whose music type is classical, prioritizing those with duration greater than 11 minutes. The condition on the composer must not affect the final score (2 PT)"

Show model answer 
PUT /tracks
{
  "mappings": {
    "properties": {
      "title": { "type": "text" },
      "duration": { "type": "integer" },
      "composer": { "type": "keyword" },
      "music_type": { "type": "keyword" }
    }
  }
}
GET /tracks/_search
{
  "query": {
    "bool": {
      "must": [
        { "range": { "duration": { "gt": 8 } } },
        { "term": { "music_type": "classical" } }
      ],
      "filter": [
        { "term": { "composer": "Bach" } }
      ],
      "should": [
        { "range": { "duration": { "gt": 11 } } }
      ]
    }
  }
}

The composer goes to filter, as the paper demands. The 8-minute threshold and the music type are required: must (in filter they would be correct too — the paper only forbids scoring the composer). The 11-minute range in should adds 1.0 to the long tracks, which therefore come first: "prioritizing". Composer and music type are keywords so that term finds "Bach" and "classical" whole; with text fields you would write match.

Lesson 26 · Asking Elasticsearch: match, filter, bool and aggregations · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 12 Sep 2025, exercise 3, second sub-question — Elasticsearch (2 PT). ER (tracking farmers and their sales at local markets), the part used: People (Personal ID, Name, Surname, Age, Address, State) and its child Farmer ((…), Biography, Experienced (Yes/No)), ISA (P, O); Cultivate: Farmer (1, N) – Crop (1, N).

The first sub-question, the complete mapping of the Farmer index (1 PT), is solved in lesson 25; this one uses its field names.

"Write a query to return the farmers whose name is "Bill", whose biography contains the words "cactus" and "aloe", and does not contain the word "potato". Additionally, if the "crop" word is found, assign a higher score to the farmer. (2 PT)"

Show model answer 
GET /farmers/_search
{
  "query": {
    "bool": {
      "must": [
        { "match": { "name": "Bill" } },
        { "match": { "biography": "cactus" } },
        { "match": { "biography": "aloe" } }
      ],
      "must_not": [
        { "match": { "biography": "potato" } }
      ],
      "should": [
        { "match": { "biography": "crop" } }
      ]
    }
  }
}

Every word condition is on a text field, hence match: "contains the words "cactus" and "aloe"" — both required, two clauses in must; "does not contain" — must_not; "if the "crop" word is found, assign a higher score" — should. Mapping the name as text lets "Bill" match whatever its case.

Lesson 26 · Asking Elasticsearch: match, filter, bool and aggregations · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the difference between query context and filter context in Elasticsearch. Which clauses of a bool query run in each context, and when would you place a condition in filter context?

Show model answer

In query context a clause answers the question "how well does this document match?": it decides whether the document matches and also computes a relevance score, and the hits are sorted by that score. A leaf query placed directly under query, and the must and should clauses of a bool query, run in query context. In filter context a clause answers only "does this document match?": it decides membership, but no score is computed. The filter and must_not clauses of a bool query run in filter context. A condition belongs in filter context when it must hold but should not influence the ranking — an exact category, a date range, or any condition that the task says "must not affect the final score". Filtering is also cheaper: no scoring is done, and the yes/no result can be cached and reused by later queries.

A complete answer names the two questions (how well / does), says that the score is computed in one and not in the other, places all four clauses — must_not is in filter context too, which is often forgotten — and gives a reason to filter: ranking versus a pure condition, and the cache.

Lesson 26 · Asking Elasticsearch: match, filter, bool and aggregations · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

A bool query with a must clause (a range on the inspection date) and a must_not clause returns 8923 hits, all with score 1.0. A should clause, a term on the borough "Queens", is added. Which statement describes the new response?

  • Fewer hits: only the inspections in Queens. That is what a second must or a filter would do. Next to a must, a should clause is optional.
  • More hits: those in the date range or in Queens. A should clause cannot bring in documents that fail the must: every hit must still match the range.
  • The same 8923 hits; the Queens inspections get a higher score and move to the top. With a must present, should only adds its score to the documents that match it: the lecture prints 8923 hits and a best score of 2.487945 (p. 606).
  • The same hits with the same scores. A matching should clause "will contribute to the score" (p. 1653): the Queens inspections gain 1.4879451 each.
Show model answer

The same 8923 hits. Because the query has a must clause, the should clause is optional: it removes and adds no document, but it adds its score to the inspections in Queens, which rise to the top (best score 1.0 + 1.4879451 = 2.487945).

This is the lecture's own example (pp. 604–606). The slide labels should "OR", and the trap is the answer "more hits". Wrong closed answers may be penalised at the exam, so if you hesitate, remember that should is an OR only when it is alone.
