Module 8 · Vectors and features

20 questions · Lectures 11a and 11b and exercise session E07

Lesson 27 · Meaning as coordinates: embeddings, similarity and the search for neighbours

Lesson 27 · Meaning as coordinates: embeddings, similarity and the search for neighbours · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements are true? Select all that apply.

  • Cosine similarity always takes values between 0 and 1. False: it ranges from −1 (opposite vectors) through 0 (orthogonal) to 1 (same direction) (p. 655). Only vectors without negative components stay within [0, 1].
  • Locality-sensitive hashing differs from conventional hashing in that hash collisions are maximized, not minimized. True, almost verbatim from p. 667: similar items should land in the same bucket.
  • A K-D tree answers nearest-neighbour queries in O(log n) whatever the dimensionality of the vectors. False: only under the hypothesis \(n \gg 2^k\) (p. 661); with high-dimensional data "the efficiency is no better than exhaustive search".
  • If all vectors are normalised to unit length, ranking by Euclidean distance gives the same order as ranking by cosine similarity. True: on unit vectors \(\norm{A-B}^2 = 2 - 2\cos\theta\), a decreasing function of the cosine.
  • HNSW guarantees to return the exact nearest neighbour. False: "HNSW does not guarantee an exact nearest neighbor search" (p. 670); a greedy walk can stop at a local minimum.
  • In IVFPQ, the vector reconstructed from its product-quantization code is identical to the original vector. False: "the reconstructed vector is not identical to the original vector" (p. 674); quantization is lossy.
Show model answer

True: the second and the fourth. Cosine similarity ranges from −1 to 1; the O(log n) of a K-D tree holds only when n ≫ 2^k; HNSW and IVFPQ are approximate methods, and product quantization loses information.

With a possible penalty for wrong answers, tick only what a slide sentence supports. Each false statement here bends one: a wrong range, a dropped condition ("whatever the dimensionality"), or "approximate" turned into "exact".

Lesson 27 · Meaning as coordinates: embeddings, similarity and the search for neighbours · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Compute the cosine similarity between the vectors \(A = (2, 1)\) and \(B = (0, 2)\), to three decimals. Are the two vectors similar, orthogonal or opposite?

Show model answer

\(A\cdot B = 2\cdot 0 + 1\cdot 2 = 2\); \(\norm{A} = \sqrt{5} \approx 2.236\); \(\norm{B} = 2\).

\[ \cos\theta = \frac{A\cdot B}{\norm{A}\,\norm{B}} = \frac{2}{2.236 \cdot 2} \approx 0.447 \]

The angle is about 63°: the vectors are moderately similar. The cosine is positive, so they are not opposite (cosine −1), and it is clearly above 0, so they are not orthogonal, but it is well below 1, the value of vectors pointing the same way.

The vectors of the session's Comparison notebook, which prints 0.4472. The usual slips: forgetting a square root in a norm, or dividing by the sum of the lengths instead of their product.

Lesson 27 · Meaning as coordinates: embeddings, similarity and the search for neighbours · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain what an embedding is and why a vector database can retrieve documents that a keyword search based on an inverted index would miss. Use the query "Rainy days make me sad" as an example.

Show model answer

Vectorization or embedding is the process of converting data, such as text or images, into dense numerical vectors that capture their semantics, using an embedding model: an encoder (BERT for text, CNNs or ViTs for images, CLIP for both) trained with metric learning so that similar items are close together and dissimilar ones far apart. A vector database stores these high-dimensional vectors; a query is embedded with the same model, and the database returns the stored vectors most similar to it, typically by cosine similarity, which compares the directions of the vectors. An inverted index only finds documents that contain the query's terms. "Colds are unpleasant." shares no word with "Rainy days make me sad", yet in the lecture's example its cosine similarity with the query is 0.419, the second best after "Rainy days can be gloomy." (0.764), because both sentences express discomfort. Vector search is contextual search that goes beyond keyword matching.

A complete answer has four parts: the definition, the role of the model (it creates the meaning; the database stores and compares), the similarity measure, and the contrast with keyword matching — with an example that has no shared word.

Lesson 27 · Meaning as coordinates: embeddings, similarity and the search for neighbours · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Why do vector databases not rely on K-D trees or R-trees to find the nearest neighbours of embedding vectors? What do they use instead, and at what price?

Show model answer

A K-D tree is a binary tree whose nodes split the space with axis-aligned hyperplanes at the median, cycling through the dimensions. Its search costs O(log n) only under the hypothesis n ≫ 2^k, where k is the dimensionality; embeddings have hundreds of dimensions (2^384 ≈ 3.9 × 10^115), so the hypothesis never holds, and a nearest-neighbour search must examine most of the tree: "the efficiency is no better than exhaustive search". An R-tree groups nearby objects into minimum bounding rectangles and skips every subtree whose rectangle the query does not intersect; in high dimension the rectangles overlap and cover much empty space, so little can be skipped. This is the curse of dimensionality. Vector databases therefore use Approximate Nearest Neighbor algorithms — LSH, HNSW, IVFPQ — which are much faster but may miss the true nearest neighbour: they sacrifice a small amount of accuracy for speed.

A complete answer names the hypothesis \(n \gg 2^k\) with its meaning, the phrase "curse of dimensionality", and the trade-off of ANN. Naming the R-tree's pruning rule shows you know what stops working.

Lesson 27 · Meaning as coordinates: embeddings, similarity and the search for neighbours · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Compare HNSW and IVFPQ: how each organizes the vectors, how a query is answered, and what each trades for speed.

Show model answer

HNSW builds a navigable small-world graph in which every vector is a node linked to its near neighbours, organized in layers, each with fewer nodes than the layer below; layer 0 contains all the vectors. The top layer of a new vector is chosen at random, and the vector is linked to its closest neighbours in that layer and in the layers below. A query starts at the entry point in the top layer, moves greedily to the linked node closest to the query, and descends a layer whenever no neighbour is closer, until layer 0. It needs few comparisons but stores the full vectors plus the links, and the result is approximate. IVFPQ first partitions the space with k-means (coarse quantization): every vector is filed in the inverted list of its nearest coarse centroid. Product quantization then compresses each vector by cutting it into sub-vectors and replacing each with the id of its nearest centroid in a small codebook. A query first finds the nearest centroids and searches only those clusters (coarse search), then compares itself with the compressed codes inside them (refined search). It is memory-efficient and fast but approximate twice, since the true neighbour may sit in a cluster that was not searched and distances computed on codes are estimates; its accuracy is tuned through the number of clusters searched and the precision of the quantization.

A good answer keeps the two indexes in parallel — structure, search, trade-off. It also avoids two slide slips: the upper HNSW layers are random, not "representative points", and HNSW is fast, not small.

Lesson 27 · Meaning as coordinates: embeddings, similarity and the search for neighbours · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

An exercise in the style of the past papers' "Which DB technology" table. Consider the ER model of a system tracking Formula 1 races (the scenario of the Exam of 11 Feb 2025): Person (Personal_ID, Name, Surname, Birth_Date, Gender) with the subtypes Spectator (Favourite_Team), Team Member and Team Owner (Total_Assets, Biography (Textual)), an ISA hierarchy (T, E); Team Member has the subtypes Engineer and Mechanic (Role_Description (Textual)) and Pilot (Championships_Won, Career (Textual)), an ISA hierarchy (P, E); Team, Race, Track and Ticket complete the model.

The organizers want a "find similar pilots" feature: given a free-text description such as "a young driver who wins on wet tracks", return the pilots whose career is most similar in meaning, even when it uses different words. Which DB technology would be the best to store the entities and attributes involved? Fill in one row of the table ENTITIES / REL. · DB TYPE · MOTIVATION, and say what is stored for each pilot.

Show model answer

Lesson 28 · Vector database systems: FAISS, Pinecone, Milvus — and the exam fill-in

Lesson 28 · Vector database systems: FAISS, Pinecone, Milvus — and the exam fill-in · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Exam of 11 Feb 2025, exercise 2 — Vector DB (1 PT). ER (tracking Formula 1 races), the part used: Pilot, a child of Team Member (P, E), itself a child of Person (T, E) — Person (Personal_ID, Name, Surname, Birth_Date, Gender); Pilot adds Championships_Won and Career (Textual).

"Complete the following Zilliz query that considers the pilots whose name starts with "Mic", and computes how many pilots won each given number of championships. The results will be in the form: (NumberOfWonChampionships, NumberOfWinningPilots). Return only the first 5 results."

pilots = client.search (
    collection_name = "pilot_collection",
    data = [...],
    search_params = {"metric_type": "IP", "params": {"level": 1}},
    ___________ = _____________________________________________
    ___________ = _____________________________________________
    ___________ = _____________________________________________
)
Show model answer 
    filter = 'name like "Mic%"',
    group_by_field = "championships_won",
    limit = 5

The filter keeps the pilots whose name starts with "Mic" (like with the % wildcard), group_by_field returns one result for each distinct number of championships won, and limit = 5 keeps the first five groups. A grouping search returns one representative pilot per value, not the number of pilots: the count per value would need client.query with output_fields = ["count(*)"] and a filter on each value.

What the grader most plausibly looks for: the three keyword names as pymilvus spells them, a like pattern with % inside a Python string, field names that match the collection, and 5. No marking scheme is published, so the one-sentence caveat is cheap insurance: it shows that you know grouping returns one pilot per value rather than the count the text asks for. The other line a grader might have in mind is output_fields = ["championships_won"], which prints the value next to each hit; but in place of group_by_field it groups nothing, and in place of limit it ignores "Return only the first 5 results". Filter, grouping and limit match the three sentences one to one.

Lesson 28 · Vector database systems: FAISS, Pinecone, Milvus — and the exam fill-in · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: "FAISS is a vector database."

  • True FAISS has no server, no access control, no replication and no metadata: the slides file it under "Libraries for vector search and comparisons" (p. 678).
  • False It is a library that indexes and searches vectors inside your program; a vector database adds distribution, scalability, access control, fault tolerance and monitoring (p. 696).
Show model answer

False. FAISS is a library for efficient similarity search and clustering of dense vectors: it stores vectors in an index in memory and returns the ids and distances of the nearest neighbours. A vector database such as Pinecone or Milvus wraps an index with distribution, scalability, access control, fault tolerance and monitoring.

A true/false item may carry a penalty when wrong, so the reason matters before the click: "library" against "database" is exactly the line drawn on p. 678 and p. 696.

Lesson 28 · Vector database systems: FAISS, Pinecone, Milvus — and the exam fill-in · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

In FAISS, an IndexIVFFlat is built with nlist = 100 and searched first with nprobe = 1, then with nprobe = 10. Explain what nlist and nprobe are, how search time and accuracy change when nprobe grows, and which of the two can be changed without rebuilding the index.

Show model answer

nlist is the number of Voronoi cells into which IndexIVFFlat partitions the space: training runs k-means on the database vectors to place nlist centroids, and each vector is stored in the list of its nearest centroid. nprobe is the number of cells, out of nlist, that a search visits: the query is compared with the centroids, and only the vectors of the nprobe nearest cells are compared with it; the default is 1. The cost per query is about nlist + nprobe × nb / nlist distance computations, so the search time grows roughly linearly with nprobe, while the accuracy — the recall, the share of the true nearest neighbours that are found — rises towards that of exact search. Only nprobe can change without rebuilding: it is an attribute set between searches, whereas a new nlist means new centroids, a new training and adding every vector again.

Three things earn the marks: the two definitions, the direction of the trade-off (more probes, more time, higher recall), and which knob is a search-time setting. The slide calls both "parameters to the search method"; say clearly that nlist is fixed at training. A number makes the trade-off concrete if you remember one: running the slide's code, recall@4 goes from 0.654 with one probe to 1.000 with ten (computed for this notebook, not printed on the slides).

Lesson 28 · Vector database systems: FAISS, Pinecone, Milvus — and the exam fill-in · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the difference between a vector index such as FAISS and a vector database such as Pinecone or Milvus. Describe the scatter-gather pattern and explain why every shard must return k results.

Show model answer

A vector index such as FAISS is a library: it keeps vectors in memory inside a program and returns the nearest neighbours of a query, with ids that are row numbers; there is no server, no metadata, and persistence is a file. A vector database wraps an index with the capabilities production needs — distribution, scalability, access control, fault tolerance and monitoring — and offers an API, persistence and post-processing of the results, such as re-ranking and filters. Its data is sharded across nodes. Since no key tells which shard holds a query's neighbours, the query is sent to all shards (scatter), each shard returns its local top k, and the results are merged into the global top k (gather). Each shard must return k results because a vector of the global top k can be anywhere in the top k of its own shard; with fewer results per shard a true neighbour could be lost. Replication keeps copies of the data on other nodes, so a failed node is replaced.

Name at least four of the five capabilities in the slide's words, describe scatter-gather as two moves, and give the reason for k per shard. Linking scatter-gather to the broadcast query of MongoDB shows that you see the general pattern.

Lesson 28 · Vector database systems: FAISS, Pinecone, Milvus — and the exam fill-in · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

In Milvus, what are a growing segment, a sealed segment and the write-ahead log? Which design principles of Milvus do they implement, and why are fresh vectors not indexed with IVF or HNSW straight away?

Show model answer

Every insert is first written by a proxy to the write-ahead log, Kafka or Pulsar — a to-do list for the data nodes. A growing segment is an in-memory segment that replays the shard's data from the log broker; it uses a FLAT index, that is brute force, so that fresh data is searchable at once and can still be appended. When it is full it is sealed: it becomes immutable, is flushed to object storage, and an index node builds an efficient index for it, such as IVF or HNSW. A search scans the growing segment, uses the indexes of the sealed segments, and merges the results. This implements "Separate Streaming and historical data" and "Log As data", and relies on "Disaggregate storage and computation", since nodes rebuild their state from the log and from object storage. Fresh vectors are not indexed straight away because such indexes are costly to build and hard to update: they are built once, on data that no longer changes.

The examiner checks the two segment kinds with their index (FLAT against an efficient one), the role of the log as the first and primary copy, and the "why": an index needs data that stopped changing. The comparison with Cassandra's commit log, memtable and SSTables is a welcome extra.

Lesson 28 · Vector database systems: FAISS, Pinecone, Milvus — and the exam fill-in · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

In Milvus Lite you need every entity of demo_collection with a price above 50 in the category 'books', and you have no query vector. Which call fits?

  • client.get(collection_name="demo_collection", ids=[123, 456]) get selects by primary key; here you have a condition, not ids.
  • client.search(collection_name="demo_collection", data=[query_vector], filter="price > 50 and category == 'books'", limit=5) search needs a query vector and returns only the limit most similar matches, not all of them.
  • client.query(collection_name="demo_collection", filter="price > 50 and category == 'books'") The scalar query: no vector, and it "Returns all entities that match the filter conditions" (p. 1706).
  • client.delete(collection_name="demo_collection", filter="price > 50 and category == 'books'") The same filter, but it removes the entities instead of returning them.
Show model answer

client.query with the filter: a scalar query needs no vector and returns all the entities that match the condition. client.search would need a query vector and rank the matches, client.get selects by id, and client.delete removes.

Search, query, get: the three verbs of p. 1706. The key distinction is whether a vector is involved; "all entities that match" is the scalar query.

Lesson 29 · Feature stores

Lesson 29 · Feature stores · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Define training-serving skew as presented in the lecture. Explain why it arises in machine-learning systems, how a feature store prevents it, and how it differs from data drift.

Show model answer

Training-serving skew, also called online/offline skew, is the situation in which a model performs poorly because the features used in production differ from the ones used during training. It arises because features are required both in the modelling phase and in the production (deployment) phase of the lifecycle: when each phase has its own feature code, the code is duplicated and the two implementations can silently diverge, for example in the time window, in the handling of refunds or in the default for missing values. A feature store prevents it by keeping one registered and versioned definition per feature and one transformation pipeline, whose results are stored in an offline store, which provides historical values for training, and in an online store, which provides the latest values with low latency for inference, both accessed through one serving API. In this way the same feature transformations are applied in training and in production. Data drift is different: the distribution of the input data changes over time while the computation stays the same; a feature store can detect drift by monitoring the features, but the remedy is retraining.

A complete answer has three parts: the definition in the slide's words (p. 734, with the synonym of p. 743), the cause — two code paths for one feature (p. 743) — and the mechanism: one definition, two stores. The contrast with drift shows understanding rather than memory.

Lesson 29 · Feature stores · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

In a feature store, which component typically stores only the latest feature values for each entity and is usually eventually consistent?

  • The offline store It keeps "months' or years' worth of feature data for training purposes" (p. 765): the history, not only the latest values.
  • The registry The registry holds definitions and their metadata (p. 769), not feature values.
  • The online store "They typically only store the latest feature values for each entity" and "Online stores are usually eventually consistent" (p. 765).
  • The data source In Feast it is the raw data the users own; "Feast does not manage any of the raw underlying data" (p. 759).
Show model answer

The online store. It persists the latest feature value of each entity for low-latency lookups during inference, usually in a key-value store such as Redis or DynamoDB, and it is usually eventually consistent. The offline store keeps months or years of history for training.

Every distractor is another component of the same architecture; the two properties in the question are the ones p. 765 gives the online store.

Lesson 29 · Feature stores · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: in Feast, for offline use cases that rely only on batch data, the data must first be ingested into the feature store.

  • True The slide says the opposite: "Feast does not need to ingest data and can query your existing data" (p. 757).
  • False Ingestion is for online use cases: materialization from batch sources and pushing of streaming features (p. 757).
Show model answer

False. For offline use cases that rely only on batch data, Feast does not need to ingest data and queries the existing data in place. Data are ingested only for online use cases: batch features are materialized into the online store, and streaming features are pushed to make them available both offline and online.

A true/false item is easy to build by flipping one slide sentence. With a possible penalty for wrong answers, answer only what the sentence of p. 757 supports.

Lesson 29 · Feature stores · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

In Feast, what are an entity, a feature view and an entity key? Give an example of a feature view with zero entities, one with one entity and one with several entities, and explain why Feast uses a time-series data model.

Show model answer

An entity is a collection of semantically related features, defined by the users to map to the domain of their use case, for example a driver or a customer. A feature view is a collection of features, bound to its entities and to a data source; it maps to zero or more entities. A collection of entities for a feature view is called an entity key; in the data, the entity key of a row is the tuple of its entity values, for example (customer_A, driver_1001). Zero entities: a global feature such as num_daily_global_transactions. One entity: a user feature such as user_age or last_5_bought_items. Several entities, a composite key: num_user_purchases_in_merchant_category, for a user and a merchant category. Feast uses a time-series data model, in which every row carries a timestamp, the entity values and the feature values, in order to build training datasets and to materialize features into an online store: the timestamps let a training row receive the latest feature values at or before its own time, without values from the future, and they tell materialization which value is the latest.

The definitions are p. 758 almost word for word, the examples too; the composite key is the part that is easiest to forget. The last sentence goes beyond the slides (point-in-time correctness is in the Feast documentation): it shows why p. 759 insists on time.

Lesson 29 · Feature stores · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following features can only be produced by an on-demand transformation? Select all that apply.

  • User country Data at rest: a batch transform (p. 767).
  • Number of views per listing in the past hour Computed continuously from a stream (Kafka, Kinesis, PubSub): a streaming transform.
  • Whether the user is currently in a supported location It depends on "data that is only available at the time of the prediction" — where the user is now (p. 767).
  • The similarity score between a listing and the search query The query exists only when the request arrives: "These features cannot be pre-computed" (p. 767).
  • Product category A property of data at rest: a batch transform.
Show model answer

The third and the fourth. On-demand transformations produce features from data that is only available at the time of the prediction, coming from the user-facing application, so they cannot be pre-computed. User country and product category come from batch transformations of data at rest; views per listing in the past hour come from a streaming transformation.

The examples are p. 767's own, so a question can use them verbatim. The test to apply: does the input exist before the request arrives? If it does, the feature can be precomputed.

Lesson 29 · Feature stores · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the ER scenario of the exam of 12 Sep 2025, tracking farmers and their sales at local markets: People (Personal ID, Name, Surname, Age, Address, State) Buy Products (Product ID, Name, Quantity, Expiration Date), with a Buy Date and an Amount; Products are sold at Markets (Name, Location, Opening Time, Closing Time, Size).

A cooperative wants two models: (a) every night, predict for each product the amount that will be bought the next day; (b) at the moment a person pays, decide whether the purchase is fraudulent. Using a feature store, propose for each model its entity key and features of the three transformation types where they fit, say which store serves each prediction, and which Feast retrieval method is used for training and for prediction.

Show model answer

(a) Entity Product, entity key Product ID. Batch features computed from the history of Buy: the total Amount bought in the last 7 days, the number of days to the Expiration Date. Training: get_historical_features with an entity dataframe of (Product ID, date) rows labelled with the amount of the next day, so that each row is joined with the feature values at or before its date. The nightly prediction for all products is a batch prediction: it also uses get_historical_features on the offline store; no low latency is needed.

(b) Entity Person, entity key Personal ID. Batch feature: Age. Streaming feature: the number of purchases of this person in the last 10 minutes, computed from the stream of Buy events and pushed to both stores. On-demand feature: whether the market of the payment is open at the time of the payment, computed at request time from the request data and the market's Opening Time and Closing Time; it cannot be precomputed. Training: get_historical_features on past purchases labelled fraudulent or not. Prediction: get_online_features for the Personal ID, served by the online store with the latest values and low latency, plus the on-demand transformation at request time.

Ours, not a past-paper exercise: the scenario is borrowed to practise the vocabulary in context. The trap is (a): a nightly prediction is offline, exactly as "Predicting user churn for all users on a daily basis" on p. 762, so it uses get_historical_features, not the online store.

Module 9 · Hadoop: MapReduce, HDFS and the ecosystem

26 questions · Lectures 11 and 12

Lesson 30 · MapReduce: the juice factory

Lesson 30 · MapReduce: the juice factory · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the scenario of the exam of 12 Sep 2025, which tracks farmers and their sales at local markets: Product (Product ID, Name, Quantity, Expiration Date) is sold at Market (Name, Location, Opening Time, Closing Time, Size) through the relationship Sell, with cardinality (1, N) on both sides. Each market stall logs the name of every product it sells, one word per sale, in a text file. Two stalls produced the files "milk cheese milk" and "cheese milk eggs", and each file is one input split.

Exemplify MapReduce with a word count over these files: give the output of each mapper, the input of the reducer after shuffle and sort (one reducer), and the output of the job. Then say how many pairs cross the network with and without a combiner.

Show model answer

Mapper 1 (split "milk cheese milk") emits one pair per word: <milk, 1> <cheese, 1> <milk, 1>. Mapper 2 (split "cheese milk eggs"): <cheese, 1> <milk, 1> <eggs, 1>.

Shuffle and sort: all pairs with the same key go to the same reducer, which receives them sorted by key and grouped: <cheese, [1, 1]> <eggs, [1]> <milk, [1, 1, 1]>.

Reduce (sum of the values of each key): <cheese, 2> <eggs, 1> <milk, 3>. Check: the counts add up to 6, the number of words, and there is one output pair per distinct word.

Without a combiner, 6 pairs cross the network. With the reducer used as combiner, mapper 1 sends <milk, 2> <cheese, 1> and mapper 2 sends <cheese, 1> <milk, 1> <eggs, 1>: 5 pairs. The reducer then receives <milk, [2, 1]> and still outputs <milk, 3>.

A complete answer shows three stages, each in its own form: one <word, 1> per occurrence (no counting in the mapper), groups <word, [values]> after shuffle and sort, one sum per key. Writing <milk, 2> as a map output is the classic mistake — it is a combiner's output.

Lesson 30 · MapReduce: the juice factory · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the scenario of the exam of 12 Sep 2025: People (Personal ID, Name, Surname, Age, Address, State) buy Products (Product ID, Name, Quantity, Expiration Date) through the relationship Buy, with attributes Buy Date and Amount. The purchases are exported as a large text file with one line per purchase: "PersonalID,ProductID,BuyDate,Amount".

Using the pseudo-code notation of the lecture, write map and reduce functions that compute, for each product, the total amount bought and the average amount per purchase. Can the reduce function also be used as the combiner? If not, write a combiner.

Show model answer 
map(String key, String line):
    // key: offset of the line
    // line: "PersonalID,ProductID,BuyDate,Amount"
    (person, product, date, amount) = split(line, ",")
    EmitIntermediate(product, (ParseFloat(amount), 1));

combine(String product, Iterator pairs):
    // pairs: (partial sum, partial count)
    s = 0; c = 0
    for each (a, n) in pairs:
      s += a; c += n
    EmitIntermediate(product, (s, c));

reduce(String product, Iterator pairs):
    s = 0; c = 0
    for each (a, n) in pairs:
      s += a; c += n
    Emit(product, (s, s / c));

The map emits, for every purchase, the product as key and the pair (amount, 1) as value. The reduce adds up amounts and counts and emits the total and the average. The reduce cannot be the combiner: its output (total, average) has another meaning than its input (sum, count), and an average of partial averages is wrong when the parts differ in size — the average of 10 and 20 is 15, the average of 15 and 60 is 37.5, while the average of 10, 20 and 60 is 30. The combiner above emits partial (sum, count) pairs of the same type as the map output, so the framework may run it zero, one or several times without changing the result.

A "Hadoop simple example" in the style of p. 810: the key is the grouping attribute, the value carries what the reducer needs. The combiner trap — averages do not combine — is the point of the question; for the total alone the reducer itself could be the combiner.

Lesson 30 · MapReduce: the juice factory · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: when a worker fails, the map tasks it had already completed must be executed again, while the reduce tasks it had already completed are not re-executed.

  • True Completed map output lives on the failed worker's local disk and is lost; completed reduce output is already in the distributed file system (p. 821).
  • False The asymmetry is real: "In-progress and Completed map tasks are re-scheduled", "Completed reduce tasks are not re-scheduled" (p. 821).
Show model answer

True. A failure is detected by missing heartbeats. The completed map tasks of the failed worker stored their output on its local disk, which is no longer reachable, so they are re-scheduled together with the in-progress ones, and the workers executing reduce tasks are notified to read from the new location. Completed reduce tasks wrote their output to the distributed file system, so they are not re-executed.

The "why" is where the output lives: local disk for map output, the replicated file system for reduce output. A true/false question invites a careless "false" from anyone who reasons "completed means safe".

Lesson 30 · MapReduce: the juice factory · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which component of a MapReduce job decides to which reducer an intermediate key-value pair is sent?

  • The combiner The combiner pre-aggregates one mapper's output; it does not choose a reducer.
  • The input splitter It cuts the input into chunks for the mappers, before any intermediate pair exists.
  • The partitioner It "Decides which pairs are sent to which reducer", by default with Key.hashCode() % numOfReducers, that is hash(key) mod R (pp. 798, 825).
  • The output committer It commits the reduce output to a file, after the reducers have run.
Show model answer

The partitioner (also called the shuffler): by default it computes hash(key) mod R, so all pairs with the same key reach the same reducer. It can be replaced, for example to obtain a total order of the output or a more uniform load.

Each wrong option is a real component in the wrong place of the pipeline: know the order splitter, mapper, combiner, partitioner, reducer, committer.

Lesson 30 · MapReduce: the juice factory · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

What is a combiner in MapReduce? Why does it save network bandwidth, and which conditions must it satisfy to be used safely? Give one reduce function that can serve as a combiner and one that cannot.

Show model answer

A combiner is an optional "intermediate reducer" that runs on the output of each mapper, on the map side, before the shuffle. When the map output contains many repeated keys — in a word count, thousands of <the, 1> — it merges the values of each key locally, so far fewer pairs are written, sorted and sent over the network to the reducers. Conditions: its input and output types must be the same as the map output types, because the reducer must accept both combined and uncombined pairs; it must have no side effects, because the framework may run it zero, one or several times; and the aggregation must give the same final result however the values are grouped, as for sums, counts of ones, maximum and minimum. The word-count reducer can be the combiner (conf.setCombinerClass(Reduce.class)); a reducer that computes an average cannot, unless the values travel as (sum, count) pairs and the division happens only in the reducer.

Three conditions — same types, no side effects, an aggregation that does not care about grouping — and the two examples. The slide's own wording "intermdiate number of times" means an indeterminate number: write "zero, one or more times".

Lesson 30 · MapReduce: the juice factory · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the principle "move the code, not the data" in MapReduce. How does the Master use HDFS to schedule map tasks, and which data still crosses the network?

Show model answer

Transferring a peta-scale input from where it is stored to the machines that run the map function would saturate the network, while the map code is tiny; so the framework sends the code to the machines that store the data. The Master asks the HDFS NameNode where the chunks (blocks) of the input are and assigns each map task to a worker that holds a copy of its split, which then reads from its local disk; if no such worker is free, it uses one close to the data, for example on the same network switch. The number of map tasks follows the block size, one split per block, to exploit this locality. The input then does not cross the network, but the intermediate data still does, in the shuffle: every reducer reads its partition from every map worker. Combiners and compression of the map output reduce this traffic.

A full answer gives the mechanism (the Master asks the NameNode for block locations), the fallback (same switch) and the honest limit (the shuffle); stopping at the slogan leaves all three out.

Lesson 30 · MapReduce: the juice factory · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements about MapReduce agree with the lecture? Select all that apply.

  • The number of map tasks M is specified by the user, while the number of reduce tasks R is determined by the system. The other way round: the system determines M from the splits, the user specifies R (p. 808).
  • Speculative execution runs extra copies of slow tasks near the end of a job, and the output of the copy that finishes first is used. pp. 818 and 824: back-up tasks as the job nears completion, first to complete wins.
  • By default, Hadoop schedules jobs with the Fair scheduler, developed by Facebook. The default is FIFO (p. 829); Fair and Capacity are the alternatives.
  • Each reduce task produces one output file. p. 823, and the reason why R also fixes the number of output files.
  • A combiner may change the types of the mapper output, since the reducer converts them back. "Cannot change the type of its input": its input and output types must be equal (p. 799).
Show model answer

The second and the fourth statements.

A select-all question where a wrong tick may cost points. The false options swap two roles (M and R), name the wrong default (FIFO, not Fair) and break the combiner's type rule.

Lesson 31 · Hadoop and HDFS: blocks, replicas, one very important server, YARN

Lesson 31 · Hadoop and HDFS: blocks, replicas, one very important server, YARN · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

True or false: in HDFS, a client that writes a file sends each block to the NameNode, which then stores it on three DataNodes.

  • True The NameNode never receives file data: it registers the file (create, complete) and gives the client a list of DataNodes for each block (pp. 862, 868).
  • False The client writes each block once, to the first DataNode of a pipeline, which forwards it to the second, and the second to the third; acknowledgements flow back (p. 862).
Show model answer

False. The NameNode handles only metadata. The client calls create() and the NameNode adds the file to the namespace; for each block the client receives a list of DataNodes chosen by the placement policy. The client streams the block, in packets, to the first DataNode only; the first DataNode forwards the data to the second and the second to the third (data pipelining), and acknowledgements travel back along the pipeline. When all replicas are written the client moves on to the next block; at the end it closes the stream and the NameNode is told that the file is complete. Keeping the NameNode out of the data path is what gives HDFS its high aggregate bandwidth.

The trap is the NameNode placed next to the word "stores". A full answer adds the pipeline: one upload per block, three copies, acks flowing back.

Lesson 31 · Hadoop and HDFS: blocks, replicas, one very important server, YARN · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements agree with the lecture's comparison between an RDBMS and Hadoop? Select all that apply.

  • Hadoop targets petabytes of data, an RDBMS gigabytes. The "Data size" row of p. 840.
  • Hadoop supports both interactive and batch access. "Interactive & Batch" is the RDBMS column; Hadoop's is "Batch".
  • Hadoop follows a write-once, read-many-times model for updates. The "Updates" row, and the "write-once-read-many" model of pp. 837 and 851.
  • "Integrity: Low" means that HDFS often corrupts the data it stores. It means no schema, constraints or transactions; HDFS checksums every block with CRC32 (p. 860).
  • Hadoop scales linearly, an RDBMS non-linearly. The "Scaling" row: each machine stores and processes its own share.
Show model answer

The first, third and fifth statements.

A select-all question where a wrong tick may cost points. The two false options are the classic misreadings: the access column swapped, and "low integrity" read as "loses data".

Lesson 31 · Hadoop and HDFS: blocks, replicas, one very important server, YARN · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

A hospital heating management system stores its sensor readings in HDFS as one file of 200 MB. With the defaults printed on the slides — block size 64 MB, replication factor 3 — how many block replicas does the cluster store for this file?

Show model answer
\[ n = \left\lceil \frac{200}{64} \right\rceil = 4 \] \[ 4 \times 3 = 12 \]

Four blocks (64 + 64 + 64 + 8 MB), three replicas each: 12 block replicas, which occupy 3 × 200 MB = 600 MB of disk.

Two traps: rounding 3.1 down to 3 blocks, and computing the disk as 4 × 64 × 3 = 768 MB — the last block holds only its 8 MB.

Lesson 31 · Hadoop and HDFS: blocks, replicas, one very important server, YARN · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Describe the architecture of Hadoop and its main components, distinguishing the core of Hadoop from its ecosystem.

Show model answer

Hadoop is a software platform for storing and processing very large data sets in batch on clusters of commodity machines; it is an open-source implementation of Google's GFS and MapReduce. Its core has four parts: Hadoop Common (shared libraries), HDFS (storage), YARN (resource management, since Hadoop 2) and MapReduce (batch processing). HDFS has a master/worker architecture: one NameNode keeps the namespace in memory — the blocks of each file and the DataNodes of each block — while the DataNodes store the blocks (64 MB each on the slides, replicated three times), send heartbeats and block reports, and serve data directly to clients. YARN has one Resource Manager for the cluster's resources, a NodeManager on every machine that launches and monitors containers, and one Application Master per application that negotiates containers and schedules and monitors its tasks; in Hadoop 1 both roles belonged to the JobTracker, with a TaskTracker per node. Every worker runs a DataNode and a NodeManager, so computation can be moved to the data. Around the core, the ecosystem adds separate projects: Hive (SQL queries), Pig (scripting), HBase (a column store for random access), ZooKeeper (coordination), Sqoop (data exchange with relational databases), Flume (log collection), Oozie (workflows), Mahout (machine learning), Hue (web interface) and Ambari (cluster management), packaged by distributions such as Cloudera's CDH or the Hortonworks Data Platform.

The question uses the guideline's own words. Give the core first, with the role of each master and worker, then the ecosystem by role; listing names without roles is the weak answer.

Lesson 31 · Hadoop and HDFS: blocks, replicas, one very important server, YARN · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

What metadata does the HDFS NameNode keep, and where? What are the transaction log and the Secondary NameNode for? Why is the NameNode a single point of failure in HDFS 1, and how does Hadoop 2 address it?

Show model answer

The NameNode manages the file-system namespace: it maps each file name to its list of blocks and each block to the DataNodes holding its replicas, and keeps file attributes such as creation time and replication factor. The entire metadata is in main memory, with no demand paging, so every lookup is fast and the number of files is bounded by RAM. Every change to the namespace, such as a file creation or deletion, is recorded in a transaction log, while the FsImage is the saved snapshot of the namespace. The Secondary NameNode periodically copies the FsImage and the transaction log, merges them into a new FsImage and uploads it to the NameNode, whose log is then purged: it is a checkpointing helper that keeps the log short and the restart fast, not a standby. In HDFS 1 there is a single NameNode, so when it fails the whole file system is unavailable; storing the transaction log in several directories, one on a remote NFS/CIFS file system, only prevents losing the namespace. Hadoop 2 adds an Active and a Standby NameNode: the active one writes every edit to a shared edit log, the standby replays it, and the DataNodes report to both, so the standby can take over.

Four things are checked: the two maps in RAM, the log and the FsImage, the Secondary as a checkpointer — the most common wrong answer calls it a backup NameNode — and the standby of Hadoop 2.

Lesson 31 · Hadoop and HDFS: blocks, replicas, one very important server, YARN · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

What were the limitations of Hadoop 1.0, and how does YARN overcome them?

Show model answer

In Hadoop 1.0 a single JobTracker managed the resources of the whole cluster and also scheduled and monitored every task of every job, while a TaskTracker on each node ran the tasks in map and reduce slots. Scalability was limited to about 4000 nodes and 40000 concurrent tasks, because of coarse synchronization in the JobTracker; the JobTracker was a single point of failure whose failure killed all queued and running jobs, which users had to resubmit; restarting it was tricky because of its complex state; and only MapReduce could run. YARN splits the two functions of the JobTracker: a global Resource Manager does only cluster resource management, and a per-application Application Master, itself running in a container, negotiates resource containers from the scheduler and schedules and monitors its own job; the TaskTracker is replaced by a per-node NodeManager that launches containers, monitors their CPU, memory, disk and network use and reports to the Resource Manager. As a result clusters reach 6,000–10,000 machines and more than 100,000 concurrent tasks; the Resource Manager saves its state in ZooKeeper and restarts the Application Masters automatically, and MapReduce applications resume from state saved in HDFS; protocols are wire-compatible, allowing rolling upgrades; and other paradigms — Tez, Spark, Giraph, Storm, HBase — run on the same cluster through their own Application Masters, while existing MapReduce jobs keep working.

Pair each limitation of p. 842 with its answer: scalability with the split and the numbers of p. 884; the single point of failure and the tricky restart with the small Resource Manager state saved in ZooKeeper, the restarted Application Masters and the MapReduce state in HDFS; MapReduce-only with paradigm-specific Application Masters. Wire compatibility and rolling upgrades are the bonus point.

Lesson 32 · The Hadoop ecosystem: HBase, Pig, Hive, Impala

Lesson 32 · The Hadoop ecosystem: HBase, Pig, Hive, Impala · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Describe the data model of HBase, using the zoo example of the slides. How is a cell identified, how is a table stored, and what does "nulls are free" mean?

Show model answer

HBase is modelled on Google's Bigtable: a key-valued row/column store on HDFS for billions of rows and millions of columns. A table is a sparse map in which each cell is addressed by its row key, its column — written family:qualifier — and a timestamp, and holds an untyped byte array. Column families are declared with the table (in the zoo example, animal: and repairs:), while qualifiers such as animal:type and animal:size can be added by any row. Every write creates a new version: enclosure1 has animal:type = lion at t1 and zebra at t2, and a read returns the newest version, zebra. Each column family is stored separately, as a list of (row, timestamp, column) → value entries: three in animal: and one in repairs: for the example. A cell that was never written has no entry at all, so it takes no space — that is "nulls are free"; space grows with the cells written, not with rows times columns. Access is only by key: get a cell or a row, or scan a range of row keys; there is no SQL and no join.

A complete answer covers four things: the cell address (row key, family:qualifier, timestamp) with an untyped value; versions, newest returned; storage per family as (row, timestamp, column) → value entries; the absent entry as the meaning of "nulls are free". Use the zoo: it is the slides' own example. If you mention "column-oriented", add that it means column families, not a column store.

Lesson 32 · The Hadoop ecosystem: HBase, Pig, Hive, Impala · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

The Pig Latin script below finds the top 5 most visited pages by users aged 18–25. Into how many MapReduce jobs is it compiled, which statements belong to each job, and why that number?

Users = load 'users' as (name, age);
Filtered = filter Users by age >= 18 and age <= 25;
Pages = load 'pages' as (user, url);
Joined = join Filtered by name, Pages by user;
Grouped = group Joined by url;
Summed = foreach Grouped generate group, COUNT(Joined) as clicks;
Sorted = order Summed by clicks desc;
Top5 = limit Sorted 5;
store Top5 into 'top5sites';
Show model answer

Three MapReduce jobs. A MapReduce job has at most one shuffle, so Pig starts a new job for every operator that needs tuples with equal keys brought together across the cluster: the join on name, the group on url and the order by clicks. Job 1 loads users and pages and filters the users by age in the map phase, shuffles both inputs on the name and joins them in the reduce phase. Job 2 reads the joined tuples, shuffles them on url, and counts each group in the reduce phase (COUNT can also run as a combiner). Job 3 shuffles the (url, clicks) pairs on clicks, so they arrive sorted; the reduce side keeps the first five and stores them. LOAD, FILTER, FOREACH … GENERATE and LIMIT need no shuffle of their own and ride along in the map or reduce phase of these jobs; the outputs of jobs 1 and 2 are written to HDFS and read by the next job.

The number alone does not answer the "why": give the rule — one job per shuffle — and the statements assigned to jobs as on p. 904. The tempting wrong answers are nine (one per line) and one ("it is one script"). If you know that real Pig adds a small sampling job before the ORDER, say so after the three jobs of p. 904, never instead of them.

Lesson 32 · The Hadoop ecosystem: HBase, Pig, Hive, Impala · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

A Hive table page_views is PARTITIONED BY (dt STRING, country STRING) and holds 365 days of data for 4 countries, one partition per (dt, country) pair. How many partition directories does Hive read to answer the query below?

SELECT * FROM page_views
WHERE dt = '2008-03-31' AND referrer_url LIKE '%xyz.com';
  • 1 That would need a condition on country too. With dt alone, every country of that day is read.
  • 4 dt = '2008-03-31' selects one day; country is unconstrained, so the 4 directories of that day are read, and the LIKE filters rows inside them.
  • 124 31 days × 4 countries: the whole of March — what the printed query of p. 911 selects, not this one.
  • 1460 The whole table: that happens only without the dt condition. The LIKE on referrer_url prunes nothing.
Show model answer

4 directories out of 365 × 4 = 1460. Partition pruning matches the condition on the partition column dt against the partitions listed in the Metastore before the job starts, so only the directories dt=2008-03-31/country=… become input. The condition on referrer_url concerns a column inside the files: it is evaluated row by row in the mappers and does not reduce the data read.

Two ideas in one question: a partition is a directory chosen by value before the job runs, and a condition on an ordinary column prunes nothing.

Lesson 32 · The Hadoop ecosystem: HBase, Pig, Hive, Impala · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Impala and Hive can query the same tables. Explain why Impala answers interactive queries much faster than Hive, what it gives up in exchange, and when Hive remains the better choice.

Show model answer

Hive compiles a HiveQL query into MapReduce jobs: every job pays a start-up cost, and the output of every job is written to HDFS before the next one reads it. Impala is a massively parallel processing (MPP) engine integrated into the Hadoop stack on the same level as MapReduce, not above it: long-running daemons on the DataNodes plan the query, split it into fragments that run in parallel on every node, read the HDFS blocks locally and stream intermediate rows to each other in memory, and its scanners push predicates down into the read. It shares Hive's Metastore, so it uses the same tables and files without moving any data — the engine is brought to the data. The slides claim it is always at least 10 times faster than Hive. In exchange it has no fault tolerance within a query (the slide says "inter-query"): if a node fails, the whole query is aborted and must be restarted, whereas MapReduce re-runs only the failed tasks because their inputs and outputs are on disk. In the slides' version Impala also could not join several big tables, had no Java UDFs and was not elastic in the cloud. Hive therefore remains the choice for long batch jobs over very large tables, where a restart would be expensive, and Impala for short interactive queries.

The core of the answer is the trade-off, not the speed: the mechanism (MPP daemons, no MapReduce, in-memory streaming, local reads, predicate pushdown), the price (a node failure kills the query — within a query, whatever the printed "inter-query" says) and the consequence (long jobs on Hive, interactive ones on Impala). The other points of p. 921 are dated: say "in the slides" when you use them.

Lesson 32 · The Hadoop ecosystem: HBase, Pig, Hive, Impala · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements are true? Select all that apply.

  • Pig and Hive run their queries as MapReduce jobs, whereas Impala runs SQL without MapReduce. True: Pig and Hive sit on top of MapReduce; Impala sits on the same level and processes data "without using MapReduce" (pp. 915–916).
  • HBase stores a NULL marker for every unwritten cell of a row. False: an unwritten cell has no entry at all — "nulls are free" (pp. 893, 895).
  • Hive keeps the schemas of its tables in the Metastore and their data as files in HDFS directories. True: "Maintains list of table schemas" (p. 906); warehouse directory, subdirectories and flat files (p. 909).
  • Tables created in Hive must be redefined in Impala before Impala can query them. False: "Impala shares metastore with Hive, which enables very simple adoption" (p. 919).
  • A Hive bucket spreads the rows of a partition over a fixed number of files by hashing a column. True: "Hash partitions within ranges (useful for sampling, join optimization)" (p. 908).
  • "Can call Hadoop Streaming scripts from HiveQL" means that Hive processes real-time data streams. False: Hadoop Streaming runs mappers and reducers written in any language over standard input and output; stream processing is Storm.
Show model answer

True: the first, the third and the fifth. HBase does not store anything for an unwritten cell; Impala shares the Hive Metastore, so Hive's tables are visible to it at once; Hadoop Streaming is a way of writing MapReduce code in any language, not stream processing.

With a possible penalty, tick only what a slide supports. Each false statement swaps a word: NULL marker for no entry, redefine for share, streaming scripts for stream processing.

Lesson 32 · The Hadoop ecosystem: HBase, Pig, Hive, Impala · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

An exercise of ours, on the ER diagram of the Exam of 12 Sep 2025 (tracking farmers and their sales at local markets): People (Personal ID, Name, Surname, Age, Address, State) buy Products (Product ID, Name, Quantity, Expiration Date) through the relationship Buy (Buy Date, Amount); Products are sold at Markets (Name, Location, Opening Time, Closing Time, Size) through the relationship Sell.

Suppose you record a large, continuously increasing number of purchases of products at the markets as files in HDFS, and analyse them with Hive, mostly one day at a time. (a) Write the Hive table for the purchases, with the attributes of the ER model you need, partitioned so that a query on one day reads only that day's data. (b) Write a HiveQL query that returns, for each market location, the number of distinct people who bought a product on 20/03/2025 and the total amount. (c) How many partition directories does your query read, and why?

Show model answer 
CREATE TABLE purchases(product_id STRING, product_name STRING,
                       market_name STRING, market_location STRING,
                       personal_id STRING, amount DOUBLE)
COMMENT 'One row per purchase of a product at a market'
PARTITIONED BY(buy_date STRING)
STORED AS SEQUENCEFILE;
SELECT market_location, COUNT(DISTINCT personal_id), SUM(amount)
FROM purchases
WHERE buy_date = '2025-03-20'
GROUP BY market_location;

(a) The partition column buy_date is declared in PARTITIONED BY and not in the column list: each day becomes a directory such as /user/hive/warehouse/purchases/buy_date=2025-03-20/. Dates are stored as yyyy-mm-dd strings, which sort in date order. (b) WHERE comes before GROUP BY; COUNT(DISTINCT personal_id) counts people, not purchases. (c) One directory: buy_date is a partition column, so the compiler matches the WHERE against the partitions listed in the Metastore before the job starts and reads only buy_date=2025-03-20; the GROUP BY then needs one shuffle, on market_location.

The exercise is ours: Hive is not among the topics of the practical part (Neo4j, MongoDB, Elasticsearch, Redis, SparkQL), but the paper's own first exercise asks for a columnar design of the same sales. It checks what the theory part can ask about Hive: partition columns declared apart and turned into directories, the WHERE before the GROUP BY, and COUNT(DISTINCT …) counting people. Only attributes of the ER model are used, as the MongoDB exercise of the same paper demands ("Do not add variables besides the ones included in the ER model").

Lesson 33 · Streams into Hadoop: Storm, Flume and Sqoop

Lesson 33 · Streams into Hadoop: Storm, Flume and Sqoop · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

A Storm topology counts the occurrences of each word in a stream of sentences. A bolt splits the sentences and emits one tuple per word to a counting bolt with several tasks, each keeping a counter per word. Which stream grouping lets the counting tasks share the work while the count of each word stays exact in a single task?

  • Shuffle grouping It shares the work, but the tuples of one word reach random tasks: each task holds a partial count, and no task holds the total.
  • All grouping Every task receives every tuple and counts everything: the counts are exact but computed in every task, so no work is shared.
  • Fields grouping on the field "word" The task is chosen by hashing the word ("consistent hashing on a subset of tuple fields", p. 933): the same word always reaches the same task, and different words spread over the tasks.
  • Global grouping All tuples go to the task with the lowest id: the counts are exact, but one task does all the work.
Show model answer

Fields grouping on "word". The tuple is routed by a hash of the chosen field, so all tuples with the same word reach the same task, which therefore holds the exact count for that word, while different words are spread over the tasks. Shuffle grouping would split each word's count over several tasks; all grouping would make every task count everything; global grouping would send everything to one task.

The point of the question is the link between grouping and state: a bolt that keeps state per key needs the tuples of that key in one place. Each wrong option is right about something else — shuffle for stateless bolts, all for broadcasts, global for one final total — which is what makes them tempting.

Lesson 33 · Streams into Hadoop: Storm, Flume and Sqoop · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Describe the main concepts of Apache Storm — stream, spout, bolt, topology — and the components of a Storm cluster. In what does a Storm topology differ from a MapReduce job?

Show model answer

Storm is a distributed system for realtime computation, developed at BackType, which Twitter acquired. A stream is an unbounded sequence of tuples, a tuple being a named list of values. A spout is a source of streams: it reads data from an external source and emits tuples. A bolt processes one or more input streams and produces new streams; it can implement filters, aggregations, joins or any function. A topology is a network of spouts and bolts — a directed graph — and every component runs as many tasks across the cluster; a stream grouping (shuffle, fields, all, global) decides which task receives each tuple. The cluster has a master node, Nimbus, similar to Hadoop's JobTracker, which distributes the code, assigns tasks to machines and monitors failures; a ZooKeeper cluster used for coordination, which keeps the cluster state; and Supervisors on the worker machines, which run the worker processes. A MapReduce job reads a bounded input and finishes; a topology processes an unbounded stream with low latency and runs until it is killed.

A complete answer has four definitions, three components and the one difference "finishes / never finishes". If there is room, add why ZooKeeper sits between Nimbus and the Supervisors: they keep no state of their own, so either can be restarted, and the topologies keep running while Nimbus is down (Storm tutorial).

Lesson 33 · Streams into Hadoop: Storm, Flume and Sqoop · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain how Apache Flume moves events from a source to a sink within an agent, and from one agent to the next, without losing them. What does this mechanism not guarantee?

Show model answer

A Flume agent hosts a source, a channel and a sink. The source accepts incoming data, turns it into events (headers with metadata, used for routing, and a body with the content) and writes them into the channel inside a transaction, which commits only when the channel has accepted the whole batch. The sink polls the channel and removes a batch inside its own transaction, and commits only after a successful transfer — when the destination, such as HDFS, has written it, or when the source of the next agent has committed it into its own channel. On any failure the transaction rolls back and the events stay in the channel to be retried. An event therefore leaves a channel only when the next hop holds it, and no event is lost in transit between agents. Not guaranteed: durability with a memory channel, which is not persistent — its events are lost if the agent process dies, so only a file (disk) channel survives a restart; exactly-once delivery, since a batch retried after a failed commit can arrive twice; and strict ordering, since Flume gives only a weak ordering guarantee: events arrive in the order received in the absence of failures.

The slides print "This ensures no data loss" (p. 949): repeat it, then add its conditions — the red pen of this lesson. The question asks for both halves: the mechanism first, then the three limits.

Lesson 33 · Streams into Hadoop: Storm, Flume and Sqoop · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: Apache Flume guarantees that events always reach the destination in the order in which the source received them.

  • True Only "in the absence of failures": page 986 calls it a "weak ordering guarantee". If an agent dies and its web servers fail over to the second agent (p. 955), the events held in the first agent's file channel arrive after newer ones.
  • False Page 986: "Flume provides weak ordering guarantee, i.e., in the absence of failures the data will arrive in the order it was received". "Always" is what makes the statement false.
Show model answer

False. Flume provides only a weak ordering guarantee: in the absence of failures, events arrive in the order in which they were received. After a failure — for example an agent that dies and whose producers fail over to another agent — events can arrive out of order.

Look for the absolute word: the slide's guarantee holds only without failures. Page 951's "flow ordering*" carries the same restriction in its asterisk.

Lesson 33 · Streams into Hadoop: Storm, Flume and Sqoop · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the farmers' market system of the Exam of 12 Sep 2025: farmers sell their products at local markets, and every sale is sent as an event to a tier of Flume agents that writes into HDFS. In the worst case the tier ingests 1,500 events per second, and HDFS may be unavailable for up to 40 minutes. Following the rule of thumb of the slides, what channel capacity does the tier need, in millions of events?

Show model answer

Channel capacity = worst-case ingest rate × worst-case downstream outage = 1,500 events/s × 2,400 s = 3,600,000 events, i.e. 3.6 million events, spread over the agents of the tier. At this size a file channel is needed, on several disks, with more disk space than the raw data.

The rule of p. 963, as worded on the summary of p. 964; the scenario is the paper's, the question is ours. The usual slip is to leave the minutes unconverted: 1,500 × 40 = 60,000 events cover only 40 seconds of outage.

Lesson 33 · Streams into Hadoop: Storm, Flume and Sqoop · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements about Apache Flume and Apache Sqoop are true? Select all that apply.

  • In a Flume agent, a source may write to several channels, while a sink reads from exactly one channel. True: "Source – Channel: many-to-many", "Channel– Sink: one-to-many (sink reads only from one channel)" (p. 966).
  • The Flume channel pushes events to the sink as soon as they arrive. False: "Source pushes events", "Sink polls for events" (p. 946); the channel is passive.
  • With a memory channel, the events waiting in the channel survive a crash of the agent process. False: the memory channel is "Not persistent" (p. 968); only the file (disk) channel keeps events across a restart.
  • The multiplexing channel selector routes each event to channels according to the value of one of its headers. True: "routes events to channels based on their headers" (p. 981), e.g. header show = 1 to ch1 (p. 982).
  • Sqoop is pull-based: it reads the relational database in bulk when the import runs. True: "PULL-based (no streams)", "Bulk imports" (p. 988).
  • Sqoop streams every new row of a table into HDFS as soon as the row is inserted. False: there are "no streams" (p. 988); an incremental job fetches the new rows only when it is executed.
Show model answer

True: the first, the fourth and the fifth. The sink polls the channel; a memory channel is not persistent; Sqoop imports in bulk when it is run, not as a stream.

Each false statement swaps one word of a slide: push for poll, survives for "Not persistent", streams for "no streams". With a possible penalty, tick only what a slide sentence supports.

Lesson 33 · Streams into Hadoop: Storm, Flume and Sqoop · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

An exercise in writing a configuration and a command. Consider the ER model of the hospital heating management system of the Exam of 30 Jan 2026: a Hospital (Hospital_ID, Name) has Buildings (Building_ID, Name, Year), Departments (Department_ID, Specialty, N_Beds, Head_Physician_ID) and Heating Systems (Heater_Sys_ID, Heating_Power), which heat Floors. Every heating system posts a reading each minute as a JSON event over HTTP, with a header building that holds its Building_ID. The Department table, whose primary key is the integer Department_ID, lives in the hospital's MySQL database hospitaldb.

(a) Write the properties file of a Flume agent heatagent that receives the readings on port 8989 and writes them as plain text files into HDFS under /hospital/readings, one folder per building, without losing the readings held by the agent if its process restarts. (b) Write the Sqoop command that copies the Department table into the HDFS directory /hospital/departments with two mappers.

Show model answer 
heatagent.sources = http-source
heatagent.sinks = hdfs-sink
heatagent.channels = ch

heatagent.sources.http-source.channels = ch
heatagent.sinks.hdfs-sink.channel = ch

heatagent.sources.http-source.type = org.apache.flume.source.http.HTTPSource
heatagent.sources.http-source.bind = 0.0.0.0
heatagent.sources.http-source.port = 8989

heatagent.sinks.hdfs-sink.type = hdfs
heatagent.sinks.hdfs-sink.hdfs.path = /hospital/readings/%{building}
heatagent.sinks.hdfs-sink.hdfs.filePrefix = readings
heatagent.sinks.hdfs-sink.hdfs.fileSuffix = .log
heatagent.sinks.hdfs-sink.hdfs.inUsePrefix = _
heatagent.sinks.hdfs-sink.hdfs.fileType = DataStream

heatagent.channels.ch.type = file
sqoop import \
--connect jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/hospitaldb \
--username=admin \
--password=password \
--table=Department \
--target-dir /hospital/departments \
-m 2

(a) The agent is started with flume-ng agent --conf-file heatagent.properties --name heatagent. %{building} puts each reading in the folder of its header value; DataStream writes plain text; the file channel keeps the waiting readings on disk across a restart, which a memory channel would not. (b) The two mappers split the rows by ranges of the primary key Department_ID, so no --split-by is needed; each writes one file into the new directory.

The exercise is ours, in the setting of a past paper; no paper so far asks for Flume or Sqoop code. It tests three ideas: the wiring keys (channels on the source, channel on the sink), header bucketing in the HDFS path, and the file channel as the answer to "without losing". bind = 0.0.0.0 accepts posts from other machines; the slide's localhost accepts only local ones.
