Lesson 34 · Getting data from the web: APIs and scraping

Lesson 34 · Getting data from the web: APIs and scraping · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: "As an end-user, the most used requests for APIs are GET requests."

  • True p. 1511, word for word. An end-user mostly consumes data, and GET is the method that reads.
  • False POST, PUT and DELETE create, update and delete; a person collecting data mostly reads, and reading is GET.
Show model answer

True. APIs are invoked through HTTP methods depending on the kind of operation; an end-user mostly retrieves data, and retrieval is a GET request, which can be written as a single URL (as in the iTunes and ViaggiaTreno examples).

The sentence is quoted from the slide, so a true/false item can reuse it as it stands. If it comes back as an open question, add the reason: the method carries the intent of the operation.

Lesson 34 · Getting data from the web: APIs and scraping · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the request https://itunes.apple.com/search?term=maroon&entity=allArtist&attribute=allArtistTerm&limit=10, shown in the exercise session on APIs. Identify the scheme, the host, the path and the query parameters, explain what limit=10 does, and describe the structure of the answer.

Show model answer

It is a GET request to a web API. The scheme is https, the host is itunes.apple.com (the server that answers) and the path is /search (the service called). After the question mark comes the query string: four key=value pairs separated by ampersands — term=maroon, the text searched for; entity=allArtist, the kind of object returned (artists); attribute=allArtistTerm, the field the term is matched against (the artist's name); limit=10, the maximum number of results. The answer is a JSON object with resultCount, here 10 because of the limit, and results, an array of artist objects with named fields such as artistName, artistId and primaryGenreName — the first two are Maroon 5 and Maroon. Records of the same answer may have different fields. Special characters inside a value must be percent-encoded, for example %20 for a space.

Name all four parts of the URL with their terms, then show that you understand each parameter as an instruction to the server. Saying that the answer is "a web page" loses the point of an API: it returns data, JSON, for programs.

Lesson 34 · Getting data from the web: APIs and scraping · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

In the Trenitalia ViaggiaTreno example, the autocompletion for train 798 returns the lines 798 - SALERNO - 04/11/25|798-S09818-1762210800000 and 798 - SALERNO - 05/11/25|798-S09818-1762297200000. Explain the structure of these lines, what the number 1762210800000 represents, why the two numbers differ by 86400000, and how the result is used in the next request.

Show model answer

Left of the vertical bar is a label for people: train number, origin station and date. Right of it is a key for programs: train number, station code (S09818, Salerno) and departure day, separated by dashes. The day is written as epoch time in milliseconds, the number of milliseconds since 1 January 1970, 00:00 UTC: 1762210800000 is the midnight of 4 November 2025 in Italian time (23:00 UTC on 3 November). One day lasts 24 × 3600 × 1000 = 86 400 000 ms, so the second key is the next day, 5 November. The parts of the key are then placed in the path of the second call, andamentoTreno/S09818/798/1762210800000 (station, train, day), which returns the status of that run of the train in JSON — for example category ICN, duration 14:35, arrived. It is a two-step workflow in which the first call provides the identifiers needed by the second, and the parameters travel in the path rather than in a query string.

The three things to get right: the number is a day, not a time of departure; the unit is milliseconds, not seconds; and the key feeds the next call. The slides print the numbers without explaining them — the derivation in this lesson is ours, and it checks digit for digit.

Lesson 34 · Getting data from the web: APIs and scraping · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

What is web scraping? How does it differ from web crawling, and when is scraping the appropriate way to collect data?

Show model answer

Web scraping is the automated process of extracting data from websites and organizing it into a structured format, such as a spreadsheet or a database. Programs called scrapers pull information such as text, prices or images from the HTML code of web pages, for later analysis. Web crawling, used by search engines to index web content, is about visiting pages by following links; scraping is about extracting data from them. The two can be combined: Scrapy both crawls a website and extracts data. Scraping is appropriate for websites with valuable content and no official API — social networks, retailers, events, train schedules — and it should be a last resort: when an API exists it provides structured, documented data, while scrapers break when the HTML changes and websites may detect and block them.

The definition of p. 1523 is the core; the contrast with crawling is the classic follow-up. The "last resort" argument (maintenance, protections) shows that you know why an API is preferred.

Lesson 34 · Getting data from the web: APIs and scraping · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

What is the Document Object Model (DOM), what does it define, and why does it matter for web scraping?

Show model answer

The W3C DOM is a platform- and language-neutral interface that allows programs and scripts to dynamically access and update the content, structure and style of a document — a standard for how to get, change, add or delete HTML elements. When a web page is loaded, the browser creates the DOM of the page. It defines the HTML elements as objects, the properties of all HTML elements, the methods to access them and their events. It is a tree: the document, the root element html, the elements head and body, their child elements, and text nodes as leaves; attributes such as href are attached to their element. For scraping it matters because parsers such as BeautifulSoup build the same tree from the HTML, and selectors (CSS selectors, XPath) are paths in it; and because scripts can update the DOM after loading, the HTML that is downloaded can differ from what the browser shows.

Quote the definition, list the four things the DOM defines (p. 1525), and add the link to scraping — the last sentence is what turns a memorised definition into understanding.

Lesson 34 · Getting data from the web: APIs and scraping · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Why may downloading and parsing the HTML of a page not be sufficient to extract its content? Which tool does the exercise session propose, and why that tool?

Show model answer

Most websites have dynamically generated content: the HTML returned by the server (and downloaded with curl or requests) is the page before its JavaScript runs, and the content may be loaded by scripts only after the page has loaded, after a click on a specific field, or while scrolling (for example Facebook and Instagram posts); content may also be split over pages in sequence (pages of reviews on Amazon or TripAdvisor). A plain download does not contain that content and cannot click or scroll. The session proposes Selenium, a module developed for automating website testing: it drives a real browser and simulates clicks, drags, scrolls and any other interaction, so it retrieves data that is not loaded with the source page and navigates several pages. The rendered page is then passed to BeautifulSoup, the parser that extracts the data after the page is fully loaded.

The decisive word is "browser": Selenium works because the browser runs the scripts. An answer that says only "use Selenium" without saying why the download is empty misses the mechanism the examiner is checking.

Lesson 34 · Getting data from the web: APIs and scraping · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements about the scraping tools presented in the exercise session are correct? Select all that apply.

  • BeautifulSoup is an HTML (and XML) parser, used to extract data after the page is fully loaded; it does not download pages itself. p. 1533: it navigates a document someone else has fetched.
  • Selenium was developed to parse HTML documents faster than BeautifulSoup. Selenium was developed for automating website testing: it simulates clicks, drags and scrolls in a browser (p. 1535). Parsing is BeautifulSoup's job.
  • Scrapy is a Python framework that both crawls a website and extracts data. p. 1534: a framework for web crawling and data extraction, for building scrapers and spiders.
  • curl and requests extract data from a page by means of CSS selectors. They retrieve the page (p. 1530); extracting with CSS selectors, regular expressions or XPath is the separate parsing step.
  • Selenium is used to retrieve data that is not loaded with the source page, or to navigate several pages of a site. p. 1535, almost word for word.
  • Once a scraper works, it keeps working as long as the website is online. The HTML changes over time, especially the classes, so the scraper must be kept updated (p. 1537); sites may also detect and block it (p. 1528).
Show model answer

The first, third and fifth statements are correct. In the pipeline of the session, Selenium or Scrapy obtains the HTML page from the Internet, BeautifulSoup parses it, and the result is the parsed data.

Two wrong options swap the roles of two tools or of two steps — browser automation versus parsing, retrieve versus parse; the last one forgets that the HTML changes and that sites detect scrapers. Wrong answers may carry a penalty, so tick only what you can justify with a slide.

Lesson 35 · Data wrangling: quality, cleansing and understanding data

Lesson 35 · Data wrangling: quality, cleansing and understanding data · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Give the conventional definition of data quality presented in the course, with its five dimensions, and explain the four problems the course attributes to it.

Show model answer

The conventional definition describes data quality along five dimensions: accuracy (the data was recorded correctly), completeness (all relevant data was recorded), uniqueness (entities are recorded once), timeliness (the data is kept up to date, with time consistency guaranteed) and consistency (the data agrees with itself). The course lists four problems with this definition. It is unmeasurable: accuracy and completeness are extremely difficult, perhaps impossible, to measure, because the true values and the missing records are not in the data. It is context independent: it does not account for what is important for the use of the data — for example, aggregates can tolerate a lot of inaccuracy. It is incomplete: it says nothing about interpretability, accessibility, metadata or analysis. And it is vague: it gives no guidance towards practical improvements of the data.

The five names and the four problems are the skeleton; each needs its meaning, not just the word. One clause on why accuracy is unmeasurable — no reference inside the data — shows that the list is understood and not only recalled.

Lesson 35 · Data wrangling: quality, cleansing and understanding data · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

"If you are computing aggregates, you can tolerate a lot of inaccuracy." Which problem of the conventional definition of data quality does the course illustrate with this example?

  • Unmeasurable That problem says accuracy and completeness are hard to measure; the example is about how much inaccuracy matters for a given use.
  • Context independent The definition makes no account of what is important: the same inaccuracy is harmless in an aggregate and serious in a single record.
  • Incomplete That problem is about dimensions the definition leaves out: interpretability, accessibility, metadata, analysis.
  • Vague That problem is the lack of guidance towards practical improvements of the data.
Show model answer

Context independent. The conventional definition does not account for what is important for the use of the data: random errors in single records largely cancel out in sums and averages, so an aggregate tolerates an inaccuracy that a single record, such as a customer's bill, cannot.

"Unmeasurable" is the tempting wrong option, because both lines of the slide mention accuracy. The key words are "what is important": quality depends on the use.

Lesson 35 · Data wrangling: quality, cleansing and understanding data · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Describe the vicious cycle of bad data. Why is it a cycle rather than a chain, and where can it be broken?

Show model answer

Bad data leads to incorrect analysis, incorrect analysis to invalid insights, invalid insights to wrong decisions, and wrong decisions to poor outcomes; poor outcomes in turn produce more bad data. It closes into a cycle because the outcomes of wrong decisions are recorded and become the data of the next analysis, and because poor outcomes reduce the budget and the attention available to fix the data. A better analysis method cannot break it, since the analysis is incorrect because of its input. The cycle is broken at the data itself, by investing in its quality through data wrangling and cleansing, which turns it into the chain of the course's goal: good data, insights, better decisions, better outcomes.

The five stages in the slide's order are the minimum. The slide (p. 1077) draws the closing arrow without explaining it, so the reason the loop closes is the part that shows understanding; the last sentence connects it to "Making a Wrong Right".

Lesson 35 · Data wrangling: quality, cleansing and understanding data · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

What is data wrangling? Place it in the data science pipeline, list its steps, and explain how it relates to ETL.

Show model answer

Data wrangling is the process of transforming raw data into data that can be analyzed to generate valid actionable insights. In the data science pipeline — data acquisition, data preparation, data analysis, data visualization, results interpretation — it is the data preparation stage, after acquisition and before analysis, and it usually takes more of a data scientist's time than the analysis itself. It is an iterative process with the steps obtain, understand, explore, transform, augment and shape; the result is visualized and checked, and the process goes back to understanding when new problems appear. ETL (Extract-Transform-Load) is the batch-oriented information architecture pattern used to feed data warehouses: data is extracted from the sources, transformed into the target schema and loaded. The course lists "(good old) ETL" among the other names of data wrangling: wrangling is essentially the transform step of ETL under a newer name, not a new technique.

Give the definition close to the slide's words: raw data in, analysable data out, valid actionable insights as the purpose. Because the slide's diagram and list disagree on the last step, naming the six boxes and adding that the result is visualized covers both readings.

Lesson 35 · Data wrangling: quality, cleansing and understanding data · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following does the course list among the reasons why data is "dirty"? Select all that apply.

  • Dummy values On the list: placeholders typed to get past a mandatory field, such as ZIP 99999.
  • Denormalised tables Not on the list. Redundant copies can end up contradicting each other through update anomalies (lesson 9), but the slide names the symptom, "Contradicting Data", not this design choice.
  • Reused primary keys On the list: an identifier given to a new entity, so old records point at the wrong one.
  • Shared field usage On the list: one field used by two departments for different purposes.
  • Replica lag in a distributed database Not on the list: a consistency issue of distributed systems (lesson 5), not of how data is entered or combined.
  • Non-unique identifiers On the list: a "key" that does not identify, such as the same customer number given to two people.
Show model answer

Dummy values, reused primary keys, shared field usage and non-unique identifiers. The full list of the course: dummy values, absence of data, multipurpose fields, cryptic data, contradicting data, shared field usage, inappropriate use of fields, violation of business rules, reused primary keys, non-unique identifiers and data integration problems.

Wrong answers may be penalised, so ticking a plausible-sounding impostor can cost you. Learning the eleven causes by kind — values, misuse of structure, rules and keys, integration — makes the impostors easy to spot.

Lesson 35 · Data wrangling: quality, cleansing and understanding data · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Name, in order, the five activities of data cleansing in practice and explain what each does, using the example of the course where it helps. Why can matching only be done after the first three?

Show model answer

Parsing locates and identifies the individual data elements of the source and isolates them in target fields: the label "Beth Christine Parker, SLS MGR, …, 12800 Lake Calumet, Hedgewisch, IL" becomes first name, last name, title, number, street, city and so on, with the values unchanged. Correcting fixes the parsed components with sophisticated algorithms and secondary data sources: a postal reference turns Lake Calumet, Hedgewisch into South Butler Drive, Chicago and adds the ZIP code 60633-2398. Standardizing converts the values into their preferred and consistent format with standard and custom business rules and coherent measurement units: "SLS MGR" becomes "Sales Mgr.", the street "S. Butler Dr.", and the pre-name "Ms." and the first-name match standards (Elizabeth, Bethany, Bethel) are added. Matching searches records within and across sources for duplicates by predefined business rules, grading each field as exact, very close, close or blank: Beth Parker matches Elizabeth Parker-Lewis of a second source. Consolidating identifies the relationships between the matched records and merges them into one representation that keeps the best value of every field. Matching compares field values, so it needs the first three steps: without parsing there are no fields, without correcting the two sources spell the same address differently, and without standardizing "Beth" and "Elizabeth" never compare as equal.

The order is half the answer, and the reason for the order is the other half. The example is optional but makes every definition concrete; the values are those of pp. 1088–1097.

Lesson 35 · Data wrangling: quality, cleansing and understanding data · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: when two records are recognised as duplicates, consolidating keeps the more complete of the two and discards the other.

  • True That would be deletion. The consolidated record of the course takes values from both records.
  • False Consolidating merges the matched records into ONE representation: title from the first source, suite, phone and fax from the second, both first names kept.
Show model answer

False. Consolidating analyses and identifies the relationships between matched records and merges them into one representation. In the course's example the consolidated record takes the title from the first source and the suite number, phone and fax from the second, and keeps both first names: Ms. Beth (Elizabeth) Christine Parker-Lewis.

The course notes (§5.2.2) end with "one of them is discarded"; the slides show the merge, and the slides are the reference.

Lesson 36 · Holes, caps and spikes: missing values, censoring and outliers

Lesson 36 · Holes, caps and spikes: missing values, censoring and outliers · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: in the terminology of the lecture, data munging denotes well-specified, lossless transformations of a data set.

  • True The lecture says the opposite: munging consists of "Potentially lossy" transformations and of "Vague" steps that are "not yet completely clear".
  • False Munging is defined as "Potentially lossy transformations applied to a piece of data or a file" and "Vague data transformation steps that are not yet completely clear", e.g. removing punctuation or HTML tags, data parsing, filtering and transformation.
Show model answer

False. Data munging is defined as potentially lossy transformations applied to a piece of data or a file, and as vague transformation steps that are not yet completely clear, for example removing punctuation or HTML tags, data parsing, filtering and transformation.

Two words carry the point: "lossy" and "vague". With a possible penalty for a wrong answer, answer only if you remember both.

Lesson 36 · Holes, caps and spikes: missing values, censoring and outliers · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

A common way to handle missing values is to discard every record that contains one. Explain why the lecture warns against this policy. Support your answer with a computation: a table has 10 attributes, and each attribute is missing in 5 % of the records, independently of the others.

Show model answer

A record survives only if all of its attributes are present. With independent holes this happens with probability \((1 - 0.05)^{10} = 0.95^{10} \approx 0.60\), so about 40 % of the records would be discarded although only 5 % of each attribute is missing. The lecture reports that in federated data between 30 % and 70 % of the data points have at least one missing attribute, so ignoring them causes data wastage. Moreover, the remaining data is seriously biased: the complete records are representative only if values are missing completely at random, while in practice the holes often depend on the source or on the values themselves. The consequence is a lack of confidence in the results. Finally, the pattern of the missing data is itself useful, since it unearths data integrity issues. The alternative is imputation, keeping in mind that imputed values are useful in aggregates but cannot be trusted individually.

A complete answer names the slide's three consequences — wastage, bias, lack of confidence — and shows why 5 % becomes 40 %. Independence is part of the computation: state it.

Lesson 36 · Holes, caps and spikes: missing values, censoring and outliers · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Describe the techniques for imputing missing values presented in the lecture, in increasing order of sophistication, with the assumption each one makes. What does the lecture say about how far imputed values can be trusted?

Show model answer

1. Standalone imputation fills each hole with a point estimate of its attribute, such as the mean or the median; it assumes that the missing values are distributed like the non-missing ones, ignores the relationships between attributes and introduces bias, but it is convenient and easy to implement.

2. Better imputation uses the attribute relationships and assumes a monotone pattern: all prior attributes are populated. The regression method (parametric) sweeps left to right with linear regressions, X3 = a + b·X2 + c·X1, then X4 = d + e·X3 + f·X2 + g·X1, where a missing X3 is estimated from the first equation. The propensity score method (nonparametric) estimates the probability that Xj is missing from X1 … X(j−1) by logistic regression, groups the records by this score and fills the missing Xj within each group from the known ones with the approximate Bayesian bootstrap.

3. For an arbitrary missing pattern, Markov Chain Monte Carlo assumes multivariate Normal data with parameter Θ and alternates simulating the missing values given Θ and re-computing Θ from the filled-in data until stable; it is expensive and mostly used to induce monotonicity.

Imputed values are useful in aggregates but cannot be trusted individually.

What makes the answer complete is the order of the methods and the assumption of each. Monotonicity is easy to name and forget to explain: say what it means, "all prior attributes are populated".

Lesson 36 · Holes, caps and spikes: missing values, censoring and outliers · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following is the lecture's example of truncated data?

  • Call durations above 20 are recorded as 20. That is the lecture's example of censored data: the record stays, and its value is bounded but not precise.
  • Days 11, 12 and 16 are missing from a chart of visitors per day because of system failures. Those are random missing values, "System failures" and "Complete miss", not the effect of a bound.
  • Customers with less than 2 minutes of calling per month are not in the data. Truncated: "Data point dropped if it exceeds or falls below a certain bound". The records below the bound are gone.
  • A spike of accounts on one value because timestamps were reset to defaults. The lecture reads such spikes as censored time intervals caused by default timestamps; truncation shows as a dip, not a spike.
Show model answer

Customers with less than 2 minutes of calling per month are not in the data. Truncated data: a data point is dropped if it exceeds or falls below a certain bound. Censored data keeps the record but bounds its value, e.g. call durations above 20 recorded as 20.

Censored against truncated is the classic confusion. Capped, or thrown out.

Lesson 36 · Holes, caps and spikes: missing values, censoring and outliers · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Define an outlier as the lecture does. Should outliers be removed before an analysis? List the families of approaches the lecture gives for detecting outliers.

Show model answer

An outlier is a departure from the expected, so every detection approach is a way of defining what is expected. In the lecture's example, 3, 4, 7, 4, 8, 3, 9, 5, 7, 6, 92, the value 92 is suspicious. Outliers should not be removed automatically: they are potentially legitimate; often they are data or model glitches; but they could also be a data miner's dream, such as highly profitable customers. Each must be investigated with knowledge of the meaning of the data. The approaches are: error bounds and tolerance limits (control charts); model-based approaches (regression depth, analysis of residuals); geometric approaches; distributional approaches; and time-series outliers.

A complete answer has the definition in the slide's words, the three possible natures of an outlier and the five approaches. A sentence on why a plain 3-sigma rule can miss an outlier — the outlier inflates the standard deviation that judges it — shows understanding beyond the list.

Lesson 36 · Holes, caps and spikes: missing values, censoring and outliers · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Two positively correlated variables are monitored with a separate control chart each (component-wise limits). A point lies inside both univariate control bands but outside the ellipse of the bivariate Normal distribution. For the component-wise charts this point is:

  • a Type II error (a missed alarm) The point is out of control jointly, but each univariate chart accepts it: the alarm is missed.
  • a Type I error (a false alarm) A Type I error is an alarm raised for a point that is in control; here the component-wise charts raise no alarm at all.
  • correctly in control Outside the ellipse, the pair of values contradicts the correlation: the point is out of control.
  • correctly out of control The component-wise charts do not flag it, so they cannot be "correctly" signalling it.
Show model answer

A Type II error: the point is accepted by both component-wise limits, but it is out of control for the bivariate distribution, so the alarm is missed. The points at the tips of the ellipse that cross a univariate limit are the opposite case, Type I errors (false alarms). This is why the lecture says that component-wise limits lead to Type I and II errors.

Picture the rectangle and the ellipse, and the answer reads off the picture.

Lesson 36 · Holes, caps and spikes: missing values, censoring and outliers · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

A Shewhart control chart for kilogram calibrations prints its two control limits as "UCL = mean + 3s = −19.39 mg" and "LCL = mean − 3s = −19.57 mg". Compute s, in mg.

Show model answer

The two limits are 6s apart:

\[ s = \frac{-19.39 - (-19.57)}{6} \] \[ s = \frac{0.18}{6} = 0.03 \ \text{mg} \]

The mean is \((-19.39 - 19.57) / 2 = -19.48\) mg. The chart follows the main steps of a control chart: define the expected value and the departure (here the mean and 3s), compute the aggregate of each sample, plot the aggregates against the expected value and the error bounds, and declare "out of control" any point outside the bounds.

The only trap is the 6: the distance between the limits is twice 3s.

Lesson 37 · Not all tables are created equal: tidy data, keys, duplicates and tools

Lesson 37 · Not all tables are created equal: tidy data, keys, duplicates and tools · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the difference between the rows and columns of a table and its observations and variables. Then explain, with an example, the lessons learnt "Never values as columns!" and "Each variable belongs to (only!) one column!".

Show model answer

Rows and columns are the physical layout of a table; observations and variables are what its values mean. A variable is a property measured on every unit (school, year, student count); an observation is the set of values measured on one unit (one school in one year). A table is well shaped when each variable is one column and each observation one row. "Never values as columns!" forbids headers that are values of a variable: in School | 2012 | 2013 | 2014 the years are values of the variable year, so one row holds three observations, and a query cannot filter or group by year without listing columns by hand. Melting the headers gives School | Year | Student Count, one row per school and year. "Each variable belongs to (only!) one column!" forbids spreading one variable over several columns (Q1 | Q2 | Q3) and gluing two variables into one: Comedy-Q1 holds a category and a quarter, and must be split into a Category and a Quarter column.

A good answer has two parts: the physical-versus-semantic distinction, and one concrete repair for each lesson. Quote the two lessons exactly as printed (pp. 1140–1141); a closed question may reuse that wording.

Lesson 37 · Not all tables are created equal: tidy data, keys, duplicates and tools · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Consider the ER diagram of the exam of 21 January 2025 ("tracking Netflix's digital content"): Digital Content (ID, title, duration, description, language, original), with the children Film, TV Series and Cartoon, ISA (T, E). An analyst exports the number of hits of each kind of content per quarter as the following table.

YearFilm-Q1TV Series-Q1Cartoon-Q1Film-Q2TV Series-Q2Cartoon-Q2
20241273954
202515461182

(a) Explain why the total number of film hits in 2024 is hard to compute on this table. (b) Restructure the table following the lessons learnt on tables. (c) Write the query that computes the total number of film hits in 2024 on the new table, and give its result.

Show model answer

(a) The headers are values: each one glues a kind of content and a quarter (Film-Q1). The query has to list the film columns by hand (Film-Q1 + Film-Q2) and must be rewritten whenever a quarter is added; neither the quarter nor the kind of content can be filtered or grouped, because they are not in any cell.

(b) One column per variable and one row per observation — one kind of content in one quarter of one year:

Content_TypeQuarterYearHits
FilmQ1202412
TV SeriesQ120247
CartoonQ120243
FilmQ220249
TV SeriesQ220245
CartoonQ220244
… and the six rows of 2025, 12 rows in all

(c) The query, which returns 12 + 9 = 21 and does not change when Q3 or a new kind of content arrives:

SELECT SUM(Hits)
FROM content_hits
WHERE Content_Type = 'Film'
  AND Year = 2024;

In a SparkQL exercise, where SQL syntax is not allowed, the same filter and sum is written with the PySpark DataFrame API (after from pyspark.sql import functions as F):

# one row: Total_Hits = 21
(content_hits
    .filter((F.col("Content_Type") == "Film")
            & (F.col("Year") == 2024))
    .agg(F.sum("Hits").alias("Total_Hits"))
    .show())

The scenario is the paper's; the table and its numbers are ours — that paper's SparkQL exercise works on Director and Film tables instead. The DataFrame form is the one a SparkQL exercise demands: the exam of 30 Jan 2026 prints "Note that you are not allowed to use SQL syntax. Use PySpark instead." (lesson 39). A complete answer has all three parts: the reason (values in the headers), the tidy header with an example row, and a query that names no quarter.

Lesson 37 · Not all tables are created equal: tidy data, keys, duplicates and tools · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following is NOT one of the data source selection criteria listed in the lecture?

  • Provenance Listed (p. 1131): where the data comes from and through which transformations it went.
  • Currency Listed: it means up-to-dateness — not money, which is Cost.
  • Popularity Not in the list: a popular source can still be inaccurate, outdated or illegal to use.
  • Verifiability Listed: can the values be checked against something else?
Show model answer

Popularity. The twelve criteria are Credibility, Completeness, Accurateness, Verifiability, Currency, Accessibility, Compliance, Cost, Legal issues, Security, Storage and Provenance.

Wrong answers may be penalised, so guessing among intruders is costly. Learn the twelve words in the three groups of the table in section 1: the content, getting it, rules and trust.

Lesson 37 · Not all tables are created equal: tidy data, keys, duplicates and tools · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

True or false: with schema on read, a record that does not match the schema is rejected when it is written to the store.

  • True That is schema on write: traditional DBMSs "enforced writing only data consistent with a pre-designed schema".
  • False With schema on read the data is stored as it comes; the schema is applied "as it is pulled out of a stored location, rather than as it goes in".
Show model answer

False. Rejection at write time is schema on write. With schema on read every record is stored, and a plan or schema is applied when the data is read; the reader must then deal with the records that do not fit, so the wrangling moves from the load to the read. The lecture's reason for the shift: "Today data modeling at design time is a luxury".

The three lines of p. 1142 are short enough to learn word for word; an open variant of this question would ask for them plus the "why".

Lesson 37 · Not all tables are created equal: tidy data, keys, duplicates and tools · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Describe the "Key" (matching) problem and the "Duplicates" problem presented in the lecture. Why can they be solved only "to certain degree of accuracy"? Give an example of each.

Show model answer

Inside one database a key identifies each record exactly. When data comes from many databases, each has its own keys — a Facebook id, a Twitter handle — and nothing says which records denote the same real entity: aligning them is the key (matching) problem, for example matching the same user across social networks. The duplicates problem is the same question inside one data set: which records are the same observation recorded twice, for example the same post published on several networks, or a customer registered twice with a typo. Because no shared key exists, records are compared on their values (name, address, age) with a similarity measure, and a pair above a chosen threshold is declared a likely identity or a likely duplicate. The decision can be wrong in two ways: a low threshold merges different entities (false matches), a high one misses true duplicates. Exact-duplicate removal such as DISTINCT finds only identical copies.

A good answer separates aligning records of different sources (key problem) from finding repeated records in one data set (duplicates problem), says why no exact solution exists — no shared key, comparison by similarity against a threshold — and names the two kinds of error.

Lesson 37 · Not all tables are created equal: tidy data, keys, duplicates and tools · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which statements about the data wrangling tools and the "Hands on Data Wrangling" checklist of the lecture are correct? Select all that apply.

  • Trifacta Wrangler profiles every column (data type, distribution of values) and records each transformation as a step of a script. p. 1146: histograms and summaries above the columns, the script in the right pane.
  • Fuzzy/Exact match is listed under Data Cleansing. It is under Data Augmenting, next to "Aggregate data sources": you match records in order to combine sources.
  • OpenRefine keeps an undo/redo history of the operations applied to a project. p. 1147 shows a twelve-step Undo / Redo history, from "Create project" to "Rename column".
  • stringr, dplyr, tidyr and lubridate are Python libraries for data wrangling. They are R libraries (p. 1149, "R Libraries for Data Wrangling"); in Python, pandas plays their role.
  • Tidying Data belongs to the Data Shaping phase. p. 1148: Data Shaping — Tidying Data.
Show model answer

The first, third and fifth statements. The five phases of the checklist are Data Ingestion, Data Exploration, Data Shaping, Data Cleansing and Data Augmenting.

The trap is the place of matching: it serves augmenting (joining sources), not cleansing. With possible penalties, tick only what you can place on a slide.

Module 11 · Spark

Lesson 38 · Spark: RDDs, transformations and actions

Lesson 38 · Spark: RDDs, transformations and actions · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

What is an RDD in Spark? Explain the meaning of each word of the name and the main properties of RDDs, and say why Spark evaluates transformations lazily.

Show model answer

An RDD (Resilient Distributed Dataset) is the core abstraction of Spark: an immutable collection of records split into partitions that are spread over the nodes of a cluster and processed in parallel. It is created from data in stable storage, such as a file in HDFS, or from other RDDs through deterministic transformations such as map, filter or join. Resilient: it is fault-tolerant, because Spark records its lineage (the parent RDDs and the function applied) and recomputes lost partitions instead of replicating them. Distributed: its partitions live on different worker nodes, and each partition is processed by one task. Dataset: the programmer handles it as one collection of data. RDDs are immutable (a transformation returns a new RDD), lazily evaluated (transformations only build the lineage; an action such as count or collect triggers the computation) and cacheable (cache() keeps the computed partitions in the executors' memory for later actions). Laziness lets Spark see the whole chain before running it: nothing is computed that no action needs, consecutive narrow transformations run in one pass over each partition, and the plan can be cut into stages and sent to where the data is.

The examiner looks for the three words tied to their mechanisms — lineage, partitions — and the three adjectives of p. 1208; for "why lazy", the reason the slide gives on p. 1210: with a large dataset you do not compute transformations until you are sure an action needs them. "Resilient because it is replicated" is the classic wrong answer.

Lesson 38 · Spark: RDDs, transformations and actions · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following Spark operations are actions? Select all that apply.

  • count() It returns the number of elements to the driver and triggers execution (p. 1211).
  • map() A transformation: it returns a new RDD and runs nothing.
  • collect() It returns all the elements to the driver: an action (pp. 1211–1212).
  • reduceByKey() A (wide) transformation: it returns a new RDD of pairs. Do not confuse it with reduce(), which is an action.
  • saveAsTextFile() It exports the data to storage: the "save" of p. 1211.
  • filter() A transformation: a new RDD with the records that pass the test.
Show model answer

count(), collect() and saveAsTextFile(). The others are transformations: they return a new RDD and are only executed when an action is called.

A select-all question where a wrong tick may cost points. The trap is reduceByKey: it sounds like reduce, but it returns an RDD.

Lesson 38 · Spark: RDDs, transformations and actions · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which statement about Spark DataFrames is correct?

  • A DataFrame can be modified in place, row by row. DataFrames are immutable: every operation returns a new DataFrame (p. 1216).
  • A DataFrame is an immutable distributed collection with named columns, built on RDDs, whose operations Spark can optimize. The definition of p. 1216, with "Improved performance via optimizations (Tungsten and Catalyst)".
  • DataFrames are available only in Scala and Java. The slide of p. 1216 says "Uniform APIs across languages (Scala, Java, Python, R, and SQL)".
  • A DataFrame query in Python is about twice as slow as the same query in Scala. That holds for RDD code (about 9.5 s against 4.2 s on the chart of p. 1218); DataFrame code takes about 2 s in every language.
Show model answer

The second: a DataFrame is immutable data with named columns, built on RDDs, with a uniform API across languages and performance improved by the Catalyst optimizer and Tungsten.

Each wrong option is a known confusion: the pandas habit of editing in place, the typed Dataset of p. 1219 (Scala and Java only), and "Python is slow", which is true only of RDD code.

Lesson 38 · Spark: RDDs, transformations and actions · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the difference between narrow and wide transformations in Spark, give two examples of each, and explain how Spark uses this difference when it executes a job.

Show model answer

A transformation is narrow when each partition of the parent RDD is used by at most one partition of the child RDD: every output partition can be computed from its input partition(s) on the same node, without moving data. Examples: map, filter, union, coalesce. It is wide when a partition of the parent may be needed by many partitions of the child, so the records must be regrouped by key across the cluster with a shuffle. Examples: groupByKey, reduceByKey, distinct, repartition, or a join whose inputs are not co-partitioned. When an action is called, the DAGScheduler splits the DAG of RDDs into stages at the wide dependencies. Inside a stage the narrow transformations are pipelined into one task per partition; a stage that follows a shuffle can start only when all the tasks of its parent stage have written their shuffle output. Wide dependencies are therefore more expensive (disk and network), and a lost partition after a shuffle may need data from all the parent partitions.

The slide prints the narrow definition counted from the child (see the red pen above); writing the standard direction, or adding coalesce as an example, shows that you understand it. The second half, stages cut at shuffles, is what makes the answer complete.

Lesson 38 · Spark: RDDs, transformations and actions · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

A worker node that holds cached partitions of an RDD fails while a Spark application is running. How does Spark recover the lost data? Compare this mechanism with the way HDFS protects data.

Show model answer

Spark does not keep copies of RDD partitions. Every RDD records its lineage: its parent RDDs and the deterministic transformation that produced it, down to the data in stable storage. When a worker fails, the driver uses the lineage to recompute only the lost partitions: it schedules tasks on the surviving workers that re-read the corresponding input blocks, which HDFS still holds on other DataNodes, and re-apply the transformations, for example filter and then map; lost shuffle outputs are recomputed in the same way. The partitions cached on the other workers are not touched. HDFS instead protects stored data by replication: each block is written to three DataNodes by default, so the other copies cover a lost node. Lineage costs nothing while no failure happens and its recovery is proportional to the lost data, while replication multiplies the storage used. This is also why Spark transformations must be deterministic.

Three things are checked: lineage as the recipe, recomputation of the lost partitions only, and the contrast with replication. The follow-up thought — deterministic functions — is the bonus.

Lesson 38 · Spark: RDDs, transformations and actions · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

Consider the hospital heating management system of the exam of 30 Jan 2026: a Hospital (Hospital_ID, Name) has several Heating Systems (Heat_ID, H_KW_Cons, Heat_Power), as in the tables of its Spark exercise. Every heating system writes its events into one log file stored in HDFS, one event per line, in the form LEVEL<TAB>Heat_ID<TAB>message, where LEVEL is INFO, WARN or ERROR. (The log and the questions are ours, in the style of the papers.)

Write a Python code with PySpark RDDs that keeps the ERROR events in memory and then computes (a) how many ERROR messages contain the word "boiler" and (b) the number of ERROR events of each heating system. Say which lines start a Spark job.

Show model answer 
from pyspark.sql import SparkSession
spark = SparkSession.builder.appName("HeatingLog").getOrCreate()
sc = spark.sparkContext                                            # the RDD API lives here

lines = sc.textFile("hdfs://.../heating.log")                      # nothing runs
errors = lines.filter(lambda s: s.startswith("ERROR"))             # transformation
errors.cache()                                                     # lazy: filled by the first action
n_boiler = errors.filter(lambda s: "boiler" in s.split("\t")[2]).count()   # action: job 1
per_system = (errors.map(lambda s: (s.split("\t")[1], 1))
                    .reduceByKey(lambda a, b: a + b)
                    .collect())                                    # action: job 2

Only the two actions start jobs: count() runs job 1, a single stage in which each task reads its block, keeps the ERROR lines, stores them in the cache and counts the matches; collect() runs job 2, whose first stage reads the cached errors instead of HDFS and whose second stage, after the shuffle of reduceByKey, adds up the events per Heat_ID. All the other lines only build the lineage.

Run on a six-line test log with PySpark 4.2.0, this code gives 2 boiler messages and the pairs ('HS_1', 2), ('HS_2', 1), ('HS_3', 1), in whatever order the partitions return them. What to get right: startswith in lower case, cache() before the first action, count and collect as the only lines that start a job, and reduceByKey rather than groupByKey. The papers' own Spark exercises use DataFrames: lesson 39 solves them.

Lesson 39 · Programming Spark: key-value operations and PySpark DataFrames, exam-style

Lesson 39 · Programming Spark: key-value operations and PySpark DataFrames, exam-style · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam of 30 Jan 2026, exercise 4 (a hospital heating management system). ER: Hospital (Hospital_ID, Name); Heating System (Heater_Sys_ID, Heating_Power), with the children HVAC System (Hourly_KWh_Cons, Cost_Per_KWh) and Gas Heating System (Hourly_SQM_Cons, Cost_Per_SQM), ISA (T, E); Has H. Sys.: Hospital (1:N) – Heating System (1:1). The exercise draws the tables Hospital (Hospital_ID, Name, Heating_Systems = [HS_1, HS2, ...]) and Heating System (Heat_ID, H_KW_Cons, Heat_Power).

"Write a SparkQL query to compute and return the total number of unique H_KWh_Consumption for each Hospital whose name includes the word "Care". Note that you are not allowed to use SQL syntax. Use PySpark instead. (2 PT)"

Show model answer

The text says H_KWh_Consumption, the table H_KW_Cons: I use the table's column name.

# different consumptions per hospital whose name contains "Care"
from pyspark.sql import functions as F

unique_cons = (hospital_df
    .filter(F.col("Name").contains("Care"))
    .select("Hospital_ID", "Name",
            F.explode("Heating_Systems").alias("Heat_ID"))
    .join(heating_df, "Heat_ID")
    .groupBy("Hospital_ID", "Name")
    .agg(F.countDistinct("H_KW_Cons").alias("Unique_Consumptions")))
unique_cons.show()

Keep the hospitals whose name contains "Care", explode the list into one row per heating system, join with the heating table to get H_KW_Cons, group by hospital and count the distinct consumptions.

On the test rows: Milano Care Center 1 — its two systems both consume 120 — and Lecco Care Clinic 2; Como General is filtered out. With F.count Milano would say 2: "unique" is the whole point of countDistinct. Check also the explode before the join and the parentheses around the chain. Filtering on the name first is not required, but it explodes fewer rows.

Lesson 39 · Programming Spark: key-value operations and PySpark DataFrames, exam-style · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam of 21 Jan 2025, exercise 5 (tracking Netflix's digital content). ER: Digital Content (ID, title, duration, description, language, original (Y/N)), with the child Film (budget, number_oscars), ISA (T, E); Person (Personal_ID, name, surname, birth_date, nationality, biography), with the child Director (number_oscars), ISA (T, E); Direct: Film (1:1) – Director (1:N). The exercise draws the tables Director (Personal ID, Name, …) and Film (Director ID, Film ID, …).

"Write a SparkQL query to collect the total number of original films directed by directors whose nationality is equal to the film's language (1.5 PT) N.B. Consider the check between nationality and language works."

"Write a SparkQL query to collect the total budget of films whose titles include the word "Curse" and whose director was born after 08/09/1994, for each director (1.5 PT)"

Show model answer

Assumed columns, from the ER: directors (Personal ID, Name, nationality, birth_date as a date); films (Director ID, Film ID, title, language, original as "Y"/"N", budget).

# (a) original films whose language is the director's nationality
from pyspark.sql import functions as F

joined = films.join(directors,
                    films["Director ID"] == directors["Personal ID"])
n_original = (joined
    .filter((F.col("original") == "Y") &
            (F.col("nationality") == F.col("language")))
    .agg(F.count("Film ID").alias("n_original_films")))
n_original.show()
# (b) budget of "Curse" films per director born after 8 Sep 1994
budget_per_director = (joined
    .filter(F.col("title").contains("Curse") &
            (F.col("birth_date") >
             F.to_date(F.lit("08/09/1994"), "dd/MM/yyyy")))
    .groupBy("Director ID", "Name")
    .agg(F.sum("budget").alias("total_budget")))
budget_per_director.show()

On our test rows: (a) 2; (b) Anna Rossi (born 1995) 100.0 and Marco Verdi (1999) 30.0, the sum of his two Curse films; John Smith (1980) is excluded. The date is where answers break: comparing birth_date with the string "08/09/1994" stops with [CAST_INVALID_INPUT] on Spark 4, so parse it with to_date and the paper's day/month/year format, or write F.lit("1994-09-08"). Without the parentheses around the date comparison the query stops with [DATATYPE_MISMATCH]. The join keeps both id columns, which is harmless here: they have different names.

Lesson 39 · Programming Spark: key-value operations and PySpark DataFrames, exam-style · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

Exam of 30 May 2025 (its header prints June 30, 2025), exercise 4 (tracking puzzles' production and sales). ER: Puzzle (Puzzle ID, Name, Collection Name, Number of Pieces, Width, Height, Description); Frame (Frame ID, Width, Height, Material, Color); Fit: Puzzle (1, N) – Frame (1, N). "N.B. All sizes are in cm." The exercise draws the tables Frame (Frame ID (PK), …) and Puzzle (Frame ID (FK) = [X, Y, …], Puzzle ID (PK), …).

"Write a SparkQL query to return the frames that perfectly fit the puzzle they are fit for. (1.5 PT)"

"Write a SparkQL query to count the total number of (distinct) frames with a width and height greater than 100 and 70 cm, respectively, that are associated with a puzzle from the "Landscape Collection". (1.5 PT)"

Show model answer 
# one row per (puzzle, frame); puzzle sizes renamed before the join
from pyspark.sql import functions as F

pz = puzzles.select(F.explode("Frame ID").alias("Frame ID"),
                    "Puzzle ID", "Collection Name",
                    F.col("Width").alias("P_Width"),
                    F.col("Height").alias("P_Height"))
fitted = pz.join(frames, "Frame ID")
# (a) frames whose width and height equal those of their puzzle
perfect = (fitted
    .filter((F.col("Width") == F.col("P_Width")) &
            (F.col("Height") == F.col("P_Height")))
    .select("Frame ID", "Puzzle ID"))
perfect.show()
# (b) distinct frames over 100 x 70 cm on Landscape Collection puzzles
n_frames = (fitted
    .filter((F.col("Width") > 100) & (F.col("Height") > 70) &
            (F.col("Collection Name") == "Landscape Collection"))
    .agg(F.countDistinct("Frame ID").alias("n_frames")))
n_frames.show()

On our test rows: (a) FR1–PZ1, FR3–PZ2, FR4–PZ3; (b) 2, because FR1 fits two Landscape puzzles and is counted once — a plain count says 3. The rename is not decoration: without it the joined table has two Width and two Height columns, and F.col("Width") stops with [AMBIGUOUS_REFERENCE] (the slip example of the playground). Equality between two double columns is safe; equality between a float column and a literal is not.

Lesson 39 · Programming Spark: key-value operations and PySpark DataFrames, exam-style · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

An exercise of ours in the form of the papers, on the ER of the exam of 6 Sep 2024 (a restaurant), whose paper had no Spark exercise. ER: Dish (Dish_ID, Name, Price, Recipe, Description); Ingredient (Ingredient_ID, Name, Allergies (Yes/No)); Use: Dish (1:N) – Ingredient (1:N). Consider the tabular representations Dish (Dish_ID, Name, Price, Ingredients = [ING_1, ING_2, ...]) and Ingredient (Ingredient_ID, Name, Allergies). Consider only the attributes defined.

(a) Write a Python code to create a dataframe for the first table and to add one element in the dataframe.

(b) Write a SparkQL query to compute and return the total number of ingredients that may cause allergies for each dish whose name includes the word "Pizza". Note that you are not allowed to use SQL syntax. Use PySpark instead.

(c) Write a SparkQL query to compute and return, for each ingredient, the average price of the dishes that use it, returning only the ingredients used in at least two dishes. Note that you are not allowed to use SQL syntax. Use PySpark instead.

Show model answer 
# (a) three dishes, then a fourth one by union
from pyspark.sql import SparkSession
from pyspark.sql import functions as F

spark = SparkSession.builder.appName("Restaurant").getOrCreate()
dish_cols = ["Dish_ID", "Name", "Price", "Ingredients"]
dish_rows = [
    ("DISH_1", "Pizza Margherita", 6.5, ["ING_1", "ING_2", "ING_3"]),
    ("DISH_2", "Pizza Bianca", 7.0, ["ING_2", "ING_4"]),
    ("DISH_3", "Octopus with Potatoes", 25.0, ["ING_5", "ING_6"])]
dishes = spark.createDataFrame(dish_rows, dish_cols)
new_dish = spark.createDataFrame(
    [("DISH_4", "Pizza Tonno", 8.0, ["ING_1", "ING_2", "ING_7"])],
    dish_cols)
dishes = dishes.union(new_dish)
# (b) allergenic ingredients per pizza; dish Name renamed first
n_allergens = (dishes
    .filter(F.col("Name").contains("Pizza"))
    .select("Dish_ID", F.col("Name").alias("Dish_Name"),
            F.explode("Ingredients").alias("Ingredient_ID"))
    .join(ingredients, "Ingredient_ID")
    .filter(F.col("Allergies") == "Yes")
    .groupBy("Dish_ID", "Dish_Name")
    .agg(F.count("Ingredient_ID").alias("n_allergens")))
n_allergens.show()
# (c) average dish price per ingredient used in two dishes or more
avg_price = (dishes
    .select("Dish_ID", "Price",
            F.explode("Ingredients").alias("Ingredient_ID"))
    .join(ingredients, "Ingredient_ID")
    .groupBy("Ingredient_ID", "Name")
    .agg(F.avg("Price").alias("avg_dish_price"),
         F.count("Dish_ID").alias("n_dishes"))
    .filter(F.col("n_dishes") >= 2)
    .select("Name", "avg_dish_price"))
avg_price.show()

On these rows and the seven ingredients of the playground: (b) Pizza Tonno 2 (mozzarella, tuna), Pizza Margherita 1, Pizza Bianca 1; (c) Tomato 7.25 and Mozzarella 7.166666666666667. Both tables have a column called Name: without the rename in (b), groupBy("Dish_ID", "Name") after the join stops with [AMBIGUOUS_REFERENCE]. In (c) averaging the dish's price is right, because each dish appears once per ingredient row; summing it per dish would be the Calzone slip. A pizza with no allergenic ingredient would be missing from (b), not shown with 0 — say so if you think the question wants it.

Lesson 39 · Programming Spark: key-value operations and PySpark DataFrames, exam-style · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following Spark RDD operations are actions? Select all that apply.

  • collect() It returns the content of the RDD to the driver as a list: an action (p. 1722).
  • reduceByKey() It returns a new RDD of pairs, so it is a transformation, lazy like map; the action is reduce().
  • count() It returns a number to the driver, so the job must run: an action.
  • flatMap() It returns a new RDD: a transformation, computed only when an action asks for it.
  • saveAsTextFile() It writes the elements out, so Spark must compute them: an action.
  • union() It returns a new RDD with the elements of both: a transformation.
Show model answer

collect(), count() and saveAsTextFile(). Transformations (map, filter, flatMap, distinct, union, reduceByKey, groupByKey, sortBy) create a new RDD and are lazy; actions (collect, count, take(n), reduce, saveAsTextFile, first) force the execution and return a result or write it.

The trap is the name: reduceByKey sounds like reduce but is a transformation. Ask of each operation what it returns — another RDD means lazy.

Lesson 39 · Programming Spark: key-value operations and PySpark DataFrames, exam-style · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

The slides state that "reduceByKey also automatically implements combiners on the map side". Explain what this means and why it matters, and why groupByKey followed by a sum is slower even though it gives the same result. Use the word count as an example.

Show model answer

In the word count, each partition produces pairs (word, 1), and the final count of a word needs all its pairs on one machine, so they must be shuffled across the network by key. reduceByKey(f) applies f inside each partition before the shuffle: a partition that holds "to" a thousand times sends one pair (to, 1000) instead of a thousand pairs — this local pre-aggregation is the combiner of MapReduce. The reducers then merge the partial results with the same f, which is why f must be associative and commutative. groupByKey cannot pre-aggregate, because it must deliver every value of a key to the reducer as a list; the sum happens only after the shuffle. Both give the same counts, but groupByKey moves far more data over the network and can build very large lists on one reducer, so for a reduction reduceByKey is preferred.

A complete answer has four parts: the shuffle as the costly step, the partial aggregation in each partition before it, the condition on the function, and the contrast with groupByKey. A number makes it concrete, as in Fig. 1: with both lines of the slide in one partition, 4 values travel instead of 6.

Lesson 40 · Spark SQL, Catalyst and the columnar file formats

Lesson 40 · Spark SQL, Catalyst and the columnar file formats · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Describe the phases of query optimization in Spark SQL's Catalyst, from a query written as SQL or with the DataFrame API to the RDDs that execute it. Which phase uses a cost model, and for what?

Show model answer

An SQL string (parsed into an AST) and a DataFrame both produce an unresolved logical plan: the same pipeline serves both. Analysis resolves it with the catalog — it looks up relations by name, maps named attributes to the inputs of each operator, gives each attribute a unique id and propagates and coerces types — and yields a logical plan. Logical optimization applies rule-based rewrites such as constant folding, predicate pushdown, projection pruning, null propagation and boolean expression simplification, producing an optimized logical plan. Physical planning generates one or more physical plans made of Spark operators, and a cost model selects one (for example the join algorithm: a broadcast join for a small table). Code generation finally compiles the selected plan, using Scala quasiquotes, into code that runs as RDD operations. Only physical planning is cost-based; the other phases apply rules.

A complete answer gives the four phases in order, what goes in and comes out of each, the catalog in analysis and the cost model only in physical planning. Name a few rules of logical optimization too: they are the easiest part to leave out.

Lesson 40 · Spark SQL, Catalyst and the columnar file formats · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

What is an unresolved attribute in Spark SQL? Using the query SELECT col FROM sales, explain what the analyzer does, and what happens when sales has no column col.

Show model answer

An attribute is unresolved if its type is not known or it is not matched to an input table. After parsing, col is only a name and sales an unresolved relation. The analyzer looks up sales by name in the catalog, matches col to one of its columns, gives it a unique id so that every reference to the same value is recognised, and propagates and coerces types through expressions (in 1 + col the literal is cast to the type of col). If sales has no column col, analysis fails at once with an error, before any data is read: the logical plan is analyzed eagerly, even though the query is executed lazily.

The core is the definition in the slide's words and the catalog lookup. "Eager analysis, lazy execution" shows that you understand why the error comes early.

Lesson 40 · Spark SQL, Catalyst and the columnar file formats · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following are rule-based optimizations of Catalyst's logical optimization phase, as listed in the lecture? Select all that apply.

  • Constant folding Listed on p. 1280; the rule of p. 1276 turns x + (1 + 2) into x + 3.
  • Predicate pushdown Listed on p. 1280: a filter moves towards the data, below projections and joins.
  • Projection pruning Listed on p. 1280: only the columns used above are kept.
  • Choosing a broadcast join because a table is small That choice belongs to physical planning, where the cost model selects among physical plans.
  • Null propagation Listed on p. 1280 among the standard rules.
  • Generating Java bytecode with quasiquotes That is the last phase, code generation (p. 1283), not logical optimization.
Show model answer

Constant folding, predicate pushdown, projection pruning and null propagation (with boolean expression simplification, the list of p. 1280). The join algorithm is chosen in physical planning by the cost model; bytecode is produced by code generation.

The distractors are real Catalyst work, done in another phase. Wrong answers may carry a penalty, so the phase of each optimization is what to know for sure.

Lesson 40 · Spark SQL, Catalyst and the columnar file formats · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: because DataFrames are evaluated lazily, a reference to a column that does not exist is reported only when an action such as show() or collect() runs.

  • True Execution is lazy, but the logical plan is analyzed eagerly (p. 1273): a wrong column name fails when the line is written.
  • False "Logical plan analyzed eagerly → identify code errors associated with data schema issues on the fly" (p. 1273).
Show model answer

False. Spark SQL analyzes the logical plan eagerly: column names and types are checked against the catalog as soon as the DataFrame operation is defined, while the data is processed only when an action runs.

The trap is to stretch "lazy" from execution to analysis. Analysis needs only metadata, so it is cheap to do at once.

Lesson 40 · Spark SQL, Catalyst and the columnar file formats · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Describe the internal structure of a Parquet file and explain how it lets a query that needs two columns and a filter read less data than a row-oriented format such as Avro.

Show model answer

A Parquet file is a sequence of row groups followed by a footer, with the magic number PAR1 at both ends. A row group holds a set of rows in columnar format (parquet.block.size, 128 MB by default) and never straddles two HDFS blocks; inside it each column is stored contiguously as a column chunk, and each column chunk is divided into pages, the unit of access, encoding and compression (1 MB by default). The footer stores the schema and, for every row group and column chunk, its offset, size, encodings, codec and statistics such as min and max. A reader reads the footer first. With projection pushdown it reads only the column chunks of the two requested columns. With predicate pushdown it uses the footer statistics and the dictionary pages to skip row groups that cannot contain matching rows, and page statistics to skip pages. Avro stores whole records one after another, so the same query must read every byte of every record.

A complete answer has the hierarchy (row group, column chunk, page), the footer's content and both kinds of pushdown, each tied to the structure that makes it possible.

Lesson 40 · Spark SQL, Catalyst and the columnar file formats · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the Parquet schema message Document { required int64 DocId; optional group Links { repeated int64 Backward; repeated int64 Forward; } }.

(a) Give the maximum repetition and definition levels of each column. (b) Shred the record DocId: 10, Links { Forward: 20, Forward: 40, Forward: 60 }, which has no Backward value, into (value, R, D) triples.

Show model answer

(a) DocId: max R 0, max D 0 (required, nothing optional or repeated on its path). Links.Backward and Links.Forward: max R 1 (one repeated field on the path), max D 2 (Links is optional, the field itself repeated).

(b) DocId: (10, 0, 0). Links.Backward: (NULL, 0, 1) — Links is present but Backward is not, so one of the two fields of the path is defined; R 0 because the value belongs to a new record. Links.Forward: (20, 0, 2), (40, 1, 2), (60, 1, 2) — the first value starts the record's list, the next two repeat at level 1, and both Links and Forward are defined.

The slide's record (p. 1253) has a Backward list; this one does not, and the NULL with D = 1 is the point of the exercise. Max D counts optional and repeated fields, max R only repeated ones.

Lesson 40 · Spark SQL, Catalyst and the columnar file formats · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the following ER scenario: a city app tracks its Users (User_ID, Name, Age, City) and the Events they generate (Event_ID, Timestamp, Kind). Each user generates zero or more events (0,N); each event is generated by exactly one user (1,1). Consider the following Users and Events tables.

User_IDNameAgeCity
U1Ada31Melbourne
U2Bob24Sydney
…………
Event_IDUser_IDTimestampKind
E1U12026-03-01 09:12click
E2U22026-03-01 09:15view
…………

(a) Write a SparkQL query to compute the average age of the users of each city.

(b) Write a SparkQL query to return the timestamps of the "click" events generated by users living in Melbourne.

(c) Write a SparkQL query to compute the total number of events for each city, returning only the cities whose total is greater than 2.

Note that you are not allowed to use SQL syntax. Use PySpark instead.

Show model answer 
from pyspark.sql import SparkSession
from pyspark.sql import functions as F

spark = SparkSession.builder.appName("SMBUD").getOrCreate()
# users and events: DataFrames with the columns of the two tables

# (a) average age per city
users.groupBy("City").agg(F.avg("Age").alias("Avg_Age")).show()

# (b) timestamps of the clicks of users living in Melbourne
(events.join(users, "User_ID")
       .filter((F.col("City") == "Melbourne")
               & (F.col("Kind") == "click"))
       .select("Timestamp")
       .show())

# (c) events per city, only cities above 2 (the HAVING)
(events.join(users, "User_ID")
       .groupBy("City")
       .agg(F.count("Event_ID").alias("N_Events"))
       .filter(F.col("N_Events") > 2)
       .show())

Executed on four users (U1 Ada 31 and U3 Eva 27 in Melbourne, U2 Bob 24 in Sydney, U4 Ugo 45 in Perth) and seven events (E1 U1 click 2026-03-01 09:12, E2 U2 view, E3 U1 purchase, E4 U3 click 2026-03-02 18:05, E5 U2 click, E6 U3 view, E7 U4 click): (a) Melbourne 29.0, Perth 45.0, Sydney 24.0; (b) 2026-03-01 09:12 and 2026-03-02 18:05; (c) Melbourne 4.

What to get right: the join on the key column's name, join(users, "User_ID"), which keeps one User_ID column; each comparison in parentheses combined with &, groupBy with agg, and the HAVING as a filter on the aggregate's alias. In (b) it does not matter whether you filter before or after the join: Catalyst pushes both conditions below it, as the widget of this lesson shows.
